# Visual-bible extraction: drafts/14–20 (~files read in full)

## 1. Josiah Morrow

**Body/bio:** born Virginia 1824 (18:17), age 34 ("thirty-four years feeling nothing," 18:53); youngest of four (18:17); enlisted 1845 (18:19). No face/hair/beard description anywhere in these files.
**Clothing:** corpse wears a coat, shirt beneath (20:13); "a bundle of clothes so worn they barely deserved the name" left at camp (19:19); "worn boots" known from his track prints (15:87). No color, hat, or coat type stated.
**Equipment (complete camp inventory):** bedroll with blankets "neatly arranged" at back of hollow; a knife; a tin cup; the clothes bundle; the journal. "No animals, no supplies" (19:5, 19:19). No weapon, no food, no cookware beyond the cup.
**The journal (physical object):** leather-bound book, "heavier than it looked, the pages thick and dense with writing," cover stained with dirt "and something darker that might have been blood," binding intact (19:21-23); found sitting on the bedroll "as if left to be found" (19:21); first entry dated *April the Nineteenth, Eighteen Fifty-Six* (19:27); Farrell sleeps with it clutched to his chest (19:65), then carries it in his saddlebag (19:73). Written the final night "by the glow of a single candle" (18:7).
**Final camp:** "a hollow in the lee of a low hill, where a cutbank offered shelter from the wind"; fire pit at the entrance, cold, ashes wind-scattered (19:5); hollow "perhaps six feet deep, carved by water or wind into the side of the hill" (19:19); a stunted pine at the camp's edge (19:11); single set of footprints leading north, down into the valley (19:5-7).
**Corpse:** face-down, arms spread wide, half-buried in a drift formed around him; snow churned/trampled for yards in every direction, streaked dark with blood (20:5). Rolled over: face peaceful, eyes open staring at the gray sky (20:11); chest crushed, ribs staved in, body shape "wrong in ways that spoke of massive force"; coat torn, shirt shredded and dark with frozen blood; "trampled, gored, broken" (20:13).
**Movement alive:** steady stride, "never varying, never stopping" (15:87); "slow, deliberate, as if every step required careful thought" (17:91).

## 2. Henry Farrell

No body/face/age description in these files.
**Gear:** rifle loaded with powder (spills powder when shaking, 15:9); coffee pot and coffee, bacon ("ate the last of my bacon and most of my coffee," 15:119); blankets (15:65); saddle, reins, saddlebag (15:9, 19:73, 15:77). Food down to "perhaps two days" (15:107) and effectively exhausted at 15:119 — no eating mentioned afterward.
**Withdrawal arc (what a bystander would see):** constant hand tremor — can't pour coffee, load rifle, or grip reins (15:9); "hands shook like leaves in a wind" (15:9); day 4: full-body convulsion, wakes rigid, ends lying outside his blankets, face wet with sweat and tears (15:65-69); shaking spreads to legs, "world kept going gray at the edges," stands blank in the snow until cold reminds him to move (15:101); braced rifle still describes circles, misses a rabbit by a foot-plus (15:105); talks aloud to people who aren't there (15:29, 17:17, 19:13); three tries to tie a knot (19:11); by Ch 11 "the shaking... a constant tremor that made every step uncertain," legs "did not want to work properly" (20:7).

## 3. Farrell's horse

Male ("get him dry," 15:111). No color, markings, or tack description anywhere. **Condition arc:** slowing, head low, heavy uncertain steps in deep snow (15:99); flanks dark with sweat despite cold — "A sweaty horse in winter is a dying horse" (15:111); fed stripped cottonwood bark, recovers partially — "Not well... but better," stands steady under saddle (15:113-121); breathing "heavy and labored" (17:99); tied to the stunted pine during the reading night (19:11); in the finale stands behind Farrell, shifting in snow, "breath coming in slow clouds" (20:75).

## 4. The white buffalo

**Size/build:** "fully grown, or nearly so, a thousand pounds of muscle and bone" (14:5); "massive shoulders, the great humped back, the horns that have curved and thickened over the two years I have followed her. She stands taller than any cow in the herd" (16:17); "enormous" in Farrell's dream (15:151).
**Coloring/features:** white coat "bright against the green" of summer meadows (14:5), "bright against the brown of the dead grass" (16:77), "white on white" against snow (20:21); eyes "dark and liquid and utterly without expression" (20:21); dream-eyes "dark and deep" (15:151).
**Blood on her:** "darker stains—blood, dried and frozen, crusted on her chest and forelegs" (20:21).
**Behavior physicality:** leads the herd — goes first, tests ground, stands watch (14:31); "When she walks, the ground trembles... a terrible deliberation in the motion" (16:19); stands apart from herd (16:77), at herd's edge alone facing north (16:91); facing east toward sunrise on the final morning (18:59); motionless and silent at the death scene, breath steaming (20:23); turns "slowly, deliberately," walks north (20:59); "seemed to glow, to carry her own illumination" in the winter light (20:61).
**Distance from Farrell in Ch 11:** "perhaps fifty yards" (20:21).
**Herd context:** ~"A hundred buffalo" (14:37); herd settles for winter "into a valley north of the river, a sheltered place" (16:77).

## 5. The Hardin killings as recorded (via Farrell's reading, Ch 10)

The killing entry itself is not in the extant journal entries (JE VII ends Aug 3 "I cannot wait any longer," 14:97; JE VIII resumes Sept 3). Ch 10 gives: watched them for weeks; killed them (method never stated in these files); "buried them himself, taken two days to do it properly, piling stones on the graves to keep the animals away" (19:47); quoted line "*I did not know I would feel this... I thought I was past feeling*" (19:45).
**Homestead/Hardins visuals (JE VII, from Morrow's ridge):** sod house with chimney smoke (14:35); barn (14:81); man out before dawn to barn ~an hour, then fields (14:81); woman near house, builds fire, reads by the window "turning pages slowly" (14:83); her carriage/movement marks her as made for "a softer life" (14:85); herd grazing within a mile of the homestead (14:33).

## 6. The Lakota horsemen (Ch 11)

"Three or four of them," appearing on the ridge to the north (20:65); "Men on horseback, dressed in furs and leather, their faces painted or simply weathered to the color of old copper" (20:65); expressions "impossible to read" (20:65); a leader "in front" who looks at Farrell for a long moment, then turns away (20:69); they turn their horses and the buffalo "fell in among them," moving north (20:71). Earlier: "small groups of riders on the ridges, watching from a distance, never approaching" (16:49). No horse colors, weapons, or gear specified.

## 7. The visions

**Soldiers:** "Men in blue coats," faces "pale and still," at the edges of vision, gone when looked at directly (15:21); stand in circles around the fire, "dead eyes reflecting the flames," never speak or move (15:25); walk alongside the horse, stand watch, "dead eyes and their silent mouths" (17:7); "patient as stones" (19:11); coats "bright against the white" (20:7). Also in firelight shadows: his father (dead twenty years), his mother, men he killed, accusing strangers (15:133-135).
**Catherine Hardin:** "dark hair blowing in the wind, her Eastern dress dragging through the snow," walking ahead, never looking back, fixed distance (17:15); "pale face" (19:55); stands beside Morrow's body, "hands folded in front of her," calm, watching the buffalo with wonder (20:43).
**Others (Ch 11):** "a man and a woman, older, dressed in the clothes of an earlier time" (20:45).
**Dream imagery:** empty Mexican village — shuttered houses, cobblestones, the white buffalo "bright against the brown of the buildings" (15:147-151); Chapultepec — walls, short ladders, room of 15–16-year-old cadets (17:67-75).

## 8. Places

**Morrow's trail:** old tracks softened by two snowfalls (15:3-5); rediscovered "at the bottom of a shallow draw," buffalo prints deep and sharp-edged, man's lighter (15:81-87); rest stop in a draw with shallower snow and a cottonwood stand (15:111-113); night camp "in a hollow between two ridges" (15:133).
**Approach country:** "hills had given way to something flatter... a wide valley stretching between ridges that seemed too far away to matter. The snow was deep here, untouched by wind, a blank white page" (17:87); buffalo and man visible "perhaps a mile" ahead (17:89).
**Winter herd valley:** "north of the river, a sheltered place where the wind cannot reach and the snow does not drift so deep" (16:77); Morrow's watch-ridge above it (16:79).
**Death scene:** "where the valley opened into a wide meadow" (20:3); camp-to-valley distance "perhaps a mile" (19:71); ridges rise "at the edge of the valley" to the north (20:59); Lakota ridge close enough that the buffalo crosses half the meadow and reaches them within the scene (20:63-71).

## 9. Per-chapter/entry conditions

- **JE VII:** dated June 14, 21; July 2, 9, 17, 25; Aug 3, 1858. Early summer green meadows, creeks "full from the mountain snows" (14:5).
- **Ch 8 (day-by-day):** winter; days 1–2 old trail; night snowstorm ("steady snow... for hours"), trail lost day 3 (15:35-37); day 4 seizure, thin gray clouds, pale light (15:71); day 5 fresh trail, quarry "a day ahead, maybe less" (15:89); one rest day for the horse; "one more day" resumed (15:123). ≈7 days from the homestead.
- **JE VIII:** Sept 3, 15, 28; Oct 12, 26; Nov 8, 20, 1858. Grass browning (16:5); "the cold has come again. The third winter" (16:75); Nov 20: weather cleared, wind died (16:89).
- **Ch 9:** "weather had been clear for days" (17:57); multiple nights; ends at sunrise with the mile-off sighting (17:85-89).
- **JE IX:** undated, pre-dawn; "first gray light in the eastern sky"; severe cold; single candle (18:5-7); sun rising as he closes (18:81).
- **Ch 10:** camp found at dusk (19:3); tracks left "this morning, perhaps, or late last night" (19:7); reads through the night by fire at the hollow's mouth (19:33); rides "in the gray light before sunrise" (19:71-73).
- **Ch 11:** morning, "sky the color of steel" (20:3); sun invisible behind clouds, light gray → paler gray → "first hints of gold" (20:55); wind picks up, then dies (20:55).

## 10. Material culture

Farrell: powder-loaded rifle (15:9), coffee pot (15:9), bacon/coffee (15:119), blankets, saddle/saddlebag/reins. Morrow: knife, tin cup, bedroll/blankets, worn-clothes bundle, leather journal (19:19-21), candle (18:7). Homestead: sod house, chimney, barn, window the woman reads beside, her book (14:35, 14:81-83). Lakota: horses, furs, leather, face paint (20:65). Mexicans at Buena Vista in blue uniforms (18:33) — historically fine; note US soldier-visions are also "blue coats" (15:21), so the two blues coexist.

---

## UNDERSPECIFIED (could not be drawn from these files alone)

- **Farrell's horse**: no color, markings, age, or tack description anywhere in Ch 8–11 — the most-present physical entity in these chapters is visually blank.
- **Farrell himself**: no body/face/clothing description in these files (check earlier chapters for coverage; his coat, hat, and boots are never shown here).
- **Morrow alive**: no hair/beard/face/coat description in these files; at-death clothing is generic "coat/shirt."
- **Morrow's writing implement** (pen? pencil? ink freezes in winter) — never stated; not in the camp inventory either.
- **Morrow's subsistence**: "no supplies" (19:5), no weapon, no food anywhere — how he eats in November–December is physically unaccounted for in these files.
- **The journal's dimensions/page count** (2.5 years of near-daily entries in one leather book).
- **The Lakota horses** and any gear beyond "furs and leather."
- **The river** the winter valley is north of (16:77) — unnamed; [inferred: Niobrara].
- **Horn specifics** (length, curve direction, color) beyond "curved and thickened."
- **The meadow's dimensions** ("wide"; buffalo visible at fifty yards; ridge reachable within the scene).

## CONTRADICTIONS / TENSIONS

1. **Timeline gap, journal vs. Farrell (strongest finding).** JE VIII ends Nov 20 with "Tomorrow, I think" (16:95); JE IX reads as that next morning. But Farrell — on the trail roughly late December (Christmas passes in Ch 6, then ~9–11 days of travel per Ch 8–10 day-counts) — finds tracks left "this morning" (19:7) and a fresh corpse. Either Morrow waited five-plus weeks after "Tomorrow" with no journal entry, or the two timelines are ~a month out of joint. Cross-check against Ch 6's Christmas dating (other fork's files).
2. **"A dark shape moved against the white—the buffalo" (17:89).** A white buffalo on snow cannot read as a dark shape; the man should be the dark one. As written, the color assignment is backwards.
3. **The vanished herd.** ~100 buffalo (14:37) are with her through JE VIII (16:77, 16:89, 18:59 "the herd gathered there"), but Ch 9's snow shows "only two stories... the tracks of the buffalo, and the tracks of the man" (17:87), and the Ch 11 meadow contains her alone — a hundred animals leave neither tracks nor mention.
4. **The candle.** Morrow writes his last entry by candlelight (18:7), but the exhaustive camp inventory (19:19) contains no candle or writing kit.
5. **"The same battles I had served in" (19:35).** Morrow's battles: Palo Alto, Resaca de la Palma, Monterrey, Buena Vista (Taylor's northern campaign, 18:21-23). Farrell's: Monterrey, Chapultepec/Bishop's Palace, march to Mexico City (Scott's campaign, 17:27-29, 15:147). Overlap is Monterrey only; "same battles" plural overstates, and the two campaigns diverged after early 1847.
6. **Buffalo maturity.** Born spring 1856, she is ~2.5 years old in late 1858, yet "fully grown," taller than any cow, with a "great humped back" (16:17) — bison cows reach full mass at 4+ years; the build described reads bull-like.
7. **"Creeks run full from the mountain snows" (14:5)** — there are no mountains in Niobrara drainage country.
8. **Fine detail at ridge distance.** Morrow, watching from a ridge with the herd "within a mile," sees the woman "turning pages slowly, as if savoring each one" (14:83) — page-turning is not resolvable at anything like that distance.
9. **Corpse minor strains.** Face "peaceful," undamaged, eyes open and staring after face-down trampling violent enough to stave in the chest, plus ~a day frozen (20:5-13) — borderline; and the drift half-burying him must have formed within that same day.
10. **Subsistence (see UNDERSPECIFIED):** Farrell's food runs out at 15:119 with ~3–4 travel days remaining; no further eating is mentioned — consistent with his deterioration but never acknowledged.

Out-of-scope note in one sentence: Ch 7 (another fork's file) reportedly contains three graves at the homestead while only two Hardins exist in these files — worth a cross-fork consistency check.</result>

Big working session — here's where things stand.

**Landed and committed (three commits):**
- `notes/visual-bible.md` — the full physical spec of the book, every fact cited, gaps marked `[gap]`, plus a do-not-illustrate list (evidence, not events) for any future plate pass.
- `notes/continuity.md` — refreshed stale v0.5 state and a severity-ordered findings log from the audit.
- **v0.6 batch 1: ten prose fixes** for the unambiguous mechanical findings, each edited in-voice at the site: the Sharps carbine anachronism (now an unspecified rifle "carried since the war," with "powder and ball" — which also makes the loose-powder loading in Chapter Eight correct); Chapter Nine's physics-backwards sighting (the buffalo was a "dark shape against the white" — now the *man* is dark, and "she was white on white, and I found her only when she moved"); "creeks run full from the mountain snows" in Niobrara country (no mountains); Morrow's journal-writing and fire-making now physically possible (flint and steel, a pencil, a candle stub added to his kit and to the camp inventory Farrell finds); "the same battles I had served in" corrected to the Monterrey-only overlap between Taylor's and Scott's campaigns; the impossible ridge-distance view of Catherine turning pages (now: "she sits by the window for an hour, hardly moving. Reading, I suppose. I cannot think what else would hold her so still."); and the frozen-men claim re-sourced to something Morrow could have seen. Stale outline drift (three graves, the removed torn-page beat) also corrected.

**Still open, in next batches** (all logged with proposed fixes): the **~5-week timeline dislocation** — Morrow's journal ends November 20 "Tomorrow, I think," but Farrell arrives around January 1 to tracks "left this morning"; fix is shifting Journal VIII's tail dates into late December. Then the three-way **creek-distance conflict** (trader says a mile; Chapter Five says two days; Chapter Six says four), the **whiskey arithmetic** (ten bottles acquired, three counted), and the **vanished herd** (~100 buffalo present through the final journal entry, absent from every Farrell chapter — needs a small beat, not just a patch).

**One design flag for your reaction** — the buffalo biology knot: she's "six months old" in April (buffalo calve in spring, so she was born out of season) and "fully grown... taller than any cow" at 2.5 years (cows mature at 4+). My lean is to *embrace* the first as canon — a white buffalo born out of season, wrong twice over, which Morrow would note and which turns two bugs into one deliberately sweaty detail — and soften the second toward "grown early," which Morrow's projection-prone narration absorbs naturally. I'll implement that in batch 2 unless you'd rather she simply be aged correctly.

**One genuine cord-pull before the revision goes further:** CLAUDE.md still says ~60k words target; the book settled at ~33k and the v0.5 notes treat that as a complete shape. Is 33k the accepted length (tight novella, polish from here), or is expansion on the agenda? The answer changes what v0.6 *is* — I'd sequence expansion before any line-level tic-cutting pass, since there's no point polishing prose that might be reflowed.

Your geo-ledger suggestion paid off immediately — it caught a glitch nobody's readerly intuition had flagged: Farrell crossed Iowa at a hundred miles a day. Everything is committed across two batches; here's the summary.

**The ledger** (`planning/geo-ledger.md`): nominal lat/longs for every location from Oswego to the death meadow, explicit travel rates, and a day-by-day calendar from the November 1 hiring to the January 9 finale. Every leg now computes: Chicago→Mississippi on the sixth day (was the fourth), Council Bluffs "seventeen days out of Chicago" (was the *seventh* — ~480 miles), Fort Kearny December 9 matching the text's "early December," homestead December 27 matching "nearly a month" of riding and Christmas passing unmarked on the creek.

**The timeline dislocation is resolved.** Journal VIII's last two entries are re-dated January 1 and January 7, 1859 (the two-month journal silence after "I have decided what I will do" reads as resolve, and has precedent in the terse winter entries). Morrow now walks out at dawn January 8; Farrell reaches the camp that dusk — so "tracks left this morning" is true — reads through the night, and finds a body one day dead at dawn January 9, which makes "dead, but not long" true as well. The trapper's and Creighton's sighting arithmetic both check out against the herd's positions. As a bonus, the planning doc's own "January 1859" row, which the draft had silently contradicted, is now correct.

**The lampshade is spent where you said** — three lines, for your reaction:
- Journal I, at the calf: *"An autumn calf, born when no calf is born."*
- Journal VIII, replacing "fully grown": *"Grown early—no cow reaches her size in three summers, but she was born out of season and has been early in everything since."*
- Journal VII: "fully grown, or nearly so" → *"grown beyond her years."*

No explanation attached to any of them — the wrongness is stated and left alone. (One earlier flag dissolved under scrutiny: "white against the brown grass" is *fine* for April plains grass, so Journal VI kept its image and only had its arithmetic fixed — "a year and a half.")

**Length decision recorded** in CLAUDE.md: no fixed target, ~33k felt right at v0.5, expansion welcome but lowest priority — later versions should earn any growth rather than aim at a number.

**Still open for the next batch:** the whiskey-bottle arithmetic (needs a read of the consumption sites), the vanished herd (~100 buffalo present in Morrow's final entries, absent from Farrell's chapters — needs a small beat, and I have an idea: the herd drifting off after the death is something Farrell could notice by its *absence*), the seven-letters clause, Farrell's food gap, and one new geography flag the ledger raised: "coach north of Rochester" lands in Lake Ontario farm plain, not logging country — proposed fix is routing both men through Syracuse, pending because it touches Chapters One and Two. After those, v0.6's continuity phase is done and the tic-reduction pass begins — fresh-context cast critics per the process rules, one Part at a time.

## ASSISTANT

All three of your notes are handled, and batch 3 closed out the audit. Milestone state:

**Your notes:**
- **Context guidance updated** in CLAUDE.md and process.md: 1M-token primary model, ~200k subagents, the whole manuscript (~45k tokens) fits comfortably — the old strategies are kept explicitly as attention/cost discipline rather than hard constraints, and "masking" is reframed as a verification technique.
- **"Year and a half"** logged in the critique's §12 style-pass items: directionally correct, rhythmically awkward, rework without reintroducing the false number.
- **Railroad research done** (sources: [Oswego & Syracuse RR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswego_and_Syracuse_Railroad), [Rome, Watertown & Ogdensburg RR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome,_Watertown_and_Ogdensburg_Railroad), [National Archives on the Rock Island bridge](https://www.archives.gov/publications/prologue/2004/summer/bridge.html), [Our Iowa Heritage on the M&M RR](https://ouriowaheritage.com/mm-railroad-2/)). It both fixed and vindicated the text: Oswego had rail since 1848, so Aldridge now rails to Syracuse and takes the cars north from Rome to Watertown, with the logging town moved to Tug Hill country (which also makes the Broken Oar's "fifty miles from any water" joke nearly literal); Farrell reverses, "changing at Rome." Meanwhile "the railroad did not extend much past the Mississippi" turned out to be *exactly* right — the M&M railhead sat at Iowa City from 1856 to 1860 — and the Rock Island bridge was operating in November 1858, which validates the ferry crossing and gifted us the *Effie Afton* incident hook.

**Batch 3 (autonomous):** whiskey arithmetic accounted ("the trader's cloudy bottle had gone first, then two of the three from Fort Kearny"); the vanished herd restored to Chapter Eleven in one unmoralized image — *"Beyond her, at the far end of the valley, the herd stood bunched and dark. None of them came near."*; the seven letters acknowledged as Aldridge's selection ("the ones he could bear to part with, I supposed"); and the food gap closed with withdrawal anorexia ("The want of drink had crowded out every smaller want.").

**Every finding from the visual-bible audit is now closed** — the timeline computes day-by-day, the geography has coordinates, the physical world is inventoried, and the only carried items are style-pass notes in the critique's §12. The manuscript sits at ~32.8k words across ten commits today.

Next phase is the one the process rules were written for: the **tic-reduction pass** — Part by Part, with fresh-context cast critics (not the incumbent author), counter-briefs on contested cuts, and the §10 checklist as the lens. Per the versioning discipline I'll record this continuity-complete state in versions.md and tag it before the style work begins, since it's exactly the kind of state worth returning to if the style pass goes somewhere we regret.

Part One's pass is committed (`edd9696`) and a cold-reading line editor is now going over it with no knowledge of what changed — checking for seams from my cuts, surviving mannerisms, period breaks, and places where restraint tipped into inertness. I'll adjudicate its findings, then carry the calibrated hand into Part Two.

Representative edits from the pass, for your reaction:

- **Stock beat**: `"You've been waiting," he said. It was not a question.` → `"You've been waiting," he said.`
- **Hedged face-reading** (the formula ran six times in Part One, now zero): `his expression shifted—something flickered across it that might have been pity, or might have been recognition.` → `He looked down at Aldridge.` (The next line — "You should prepare yourself" — carries the pity.)
- **Theme self-announcement, undercut instead of deleted**: `I am being refined. I feel it happening... I am becoming simpler, purer. I am becoming what I need to be.` → `...are falling away like dead skin. I am becoming simpler. Whether I am being refined or only starved, I cannot tell.`
- **Trauma-etiology register slip**: `Perhaps I was born with it. Perhaps it was made in me by the things I saw as a boy—my father's rages, my mother's silences, the poverty and violence that were the only constants of my childhood.` → `I do not know when this emptiness came to me, whether I was born with it or it was made in me. It does not matter where it came from.`
- **Foreshadow signpost**: `But I remembered it. I would have cause to remember it later.` → cut entirely; "It meant nothing to me, not then" already whispers it.
- **The creed, de-recycled**: Journal III's `I need to die by something sacred, something that should not exist, something that carries meaning in its blood and bones` → `I need to die by something sacred.` — the blood-and-bones coinage now lives only in Journal I, where it's strongest.

Also caught and fixed a leftover continuity edge: Chapter Two still had Farrell crossing the Mississippi "in the morning" from Chicago — now a six-day ride under the new calendar. And one ruling recorded: "like a ship through water" stays in *both* places — Farrell only reaches for it after the night he spends reading Morrow, so the borrowed simile is diegetic, not accidental.

<result>Findings below. File paths are relative to /home/dlk/workspace/white-buffalo/drafts/. Line numbers from the files as read.

## 1. SEAMS

**01-chapter-one.md:161 vs 175 — hard continuity break.** Aldridge tells Farrell "I have prepared a packet. It is at my room, at the boarding house" (161). Fourteen lines later, still at the tavern table: "He did not touch his pocket, did not check that the packet of letters was still there, though he wanted to" (175). The packet cannot be both at the boarding house and in his coat. This reads exactly like a scene where the packet's location was moved in revision and one instance survived.

**05-chapter-three.md:119–121 — adjacent sentences cancel each other.** "The conversation moved on, but I did not stop thinking about it" (119) is immediately followed by "It meant nothing to me, not then. Just another strange story from a strange country" (121). He cannot both keep thinking about it and have it mean nothing. One of these paragraphs belongs to an earlier draft's intent.

**01-chapter-one.md:37 — dropped leg in the itinerary.** "The first day by railroad to Syracuse, then the cars north from Rome to Watertown, then a coach east." Syracuse to Rome is silently skipped; the sentence rhythm ("then... then...") makes the gap audible. Either add the leg or cut Syracuse.

**05-chapter-three.md:47 — the Taylor pivot interrupts its own list.** "I had served under men who would lead that war, when it came. I had seen what they were capable of. Zachary Taylor was dead now, president for a year and then gone, but there were others still living." Taylor is introduced inside a list of men who *would* lead the war only to be immediately disqualified from it; the sentence reads like a fact-check patch inserted mid-paragraph.

**03-chapter-two.md:115 — retro-inserted prop.** "I had bought a proper map in Cleveland" — the Cleveland stopover is narrated in full (67–79: hotel, chophouse, whiskey, ceiling cracks) with no map purchase. The map arrives in Chicago as a pluperfect patch.

**01-chapter-one.md:181 — summary papering over a skipped beat.** "They spoke briefly, exchanged what information there was to exchange." The second clause is tautological — it says nothing the first didn't. It reads like a placeholder for a scene that was cut.

**04-journal-entry-ii.md:41 — internal pull.** "I killed seventeen men with certainty and perhaps more... After a time I stopped counting." A man who stopped counting does not hold "seventeen with certainty." Pick one: the count or the abdication of counting.

**Cross-file redundancy, 01:175 vs 03:13.** Chapter One paraphrases Catherine's last letter ("the winter was finally breaking and... the snow was melting and... she hoped the worst was behind them"); Chapter Two then quotes it verbatim ("The winter is finally breaking. The snow is melting..."). Adjacent files, same content twice within a few pages of reading. The Chapter Two quotation is the stronger placement; the Chapter One paraphrase should be thinned.

## 2. SURVIVING MANNERISMS

**Verbatim machinery repeated across the two narrators — the most serious problem in the batch, because it erases voice distinction:**
- "I have waited in the trenches before an assault, listening to the guns" (02:47) / "sitting in trenches or behind walls, listening to the guns" (03:65). Two different men, same clause.
- "The fire is small—I have little to burn—but it is enough" (02:53) / "The fire was small—I did not want company—but it was enough" (05:9). Identical frame, identical dash-parenthesis, identical button.
- "The days blur together on the plains" (02:5) / "The days blend together" (04:53) / "The next days blurred together" (05:17) / "the names and places blurred together" (05:45). Four instances, both narrators.
- Both men were "made" by Mexico: "I was made into a killer in Mexico" (02:61), "In Mexico I was made into a weapon" (04:39), "anyone other than what the war had made me" (03:61).

**Repeats within a single narrator:**
- "lay down on the bed without undressing" (03:79) / "lay on the bed without undressing" (05:79).
- "Whole country's going to hell" (05:44) and "The whole territory is going to hell" (05:73) — two separate overheard conversations in one chapter given the same line.
- "The map was useless" (03:3) / "The hand-drawn sketch from Aldridge's packet was useless" (03:115).
- The "which was..." button: "which was worth more to me than clean sheets" (03:5), "which was all I required" (03:87), "which was what mattered" (05:59).
- "testing the air" appears at 04:21 and again at 06:9, where the whole clause set recurs: "wakes before the others and stands apart, testing the air" — Journal III re-reports Journal II's observations nearly verbatim (compare 06:9 with 04:21–25, including "apart from the herd").
- "eyes half-closed" twice in one file (06:9, 06:47).
- "expression did not change" twice in Chapter One (01:17, 01:95) plus "without expression" (01:9).
- Gray sky: "gray lattice of branches against a grayer sky" (01:5), "gray expanse of Erie" (03:55), "gray morning" (05:3), "the gray sky" (05:7), "The sky stayed gray" (05:17), "The sky was gray" (05:128).

**Stacked triads** — the dominant tic, in both voices: "methodically, thoroughly, with attention to contingency" (01:19); "of snow, of bone, of the clouds" (02:27); "in her blood and her bones and her impossible coat" (02:35); "white and sacred and wild" (02:61); "the shaking and the fever and the visions" (03:45); "they filed him down and they put an edge on him and they pointed him at other men" (04:39); "I need her to kill me. I need to die by something sacred. I need my death to mean something" (06:51); "a storm... or a fire, or the edge of a knife" (06:63). 02:47 is a triad of triads ("news that never came, letters that were never written, a God who never answered").

**Negation-pivots:** "He directed me. He did not send me" (01:83); "Not pale, not cream-colored, not the faded tan... White" (02:27); "Not by storm or starvation or the arrows of men... By her" (02:35); "But it was not courage" (06:31); "not because I wanted to but because I needed the supply to last" (05:11). Any one is fine; the accumulation is a signature.

**One-line paragraph buttons** — all six pieces close on one, and interiors are studded with them: "None of it seemed adequate now" (01:21); "And now she was gone" (01:65); "So I walk, and I do not die. / Until today. / Today I saw her." — three consecutive (02:15–19); "This is how I will die" (02:33); "That is what I want" (02:59); "A weapon must be used or destroyed. I am choosing destroyed" (04:47); "I would find out what. That was my job" (05:85); "I did not look back" (05:131 — itself a stock Western close); "I can wait. I have learned how" (06:69). The device stops landing around the fourth use.

**"Something" as vague intensifier** — roughly a dozen instances doing no work: "something behind it did" (01:17), "something dangerous, something barely contained" (01:69), "something more than casual interest" (01:73), "something had shifted underneath" (03:9), "something that might have been concern" (03:25), "something in his voice" (05:105), "something in the man's eyes" (05:117), "something in them, or I imagined there was" (04:61), "something out of a dream" (06:65). Keep two or three; cut the rest.

**One cute aside:** "The place was called The Broken Oar, though they were fifty miles from any water that would require one" (01:5). It's a wink; the book otherwise plays it straight.

**Structural repetition:** each journal installment pivots mid-entry into a Mexico-confession block (02:47ff, 04:39ff, 06:25ff). Three-for-three, the reader learns the shape and stops being surprised by it.

## 3. PERIOD/VOICE BREAKS

- **05:101 — "Snow already in the Dakotas."** Hard anachronism. Dakota Territory was not organized until 1861; "the Dakotas" as a plural is post-1889. In 1858 a Chicago liveryman would say Minnesota Territory, the upper Missouri, or the Sioux country.
- **05:47 — "Robert Lee, who... was now superintending West Point."** False for 1858. Lee left the superintendency in 1855; by 1858 he was lieutenant colonel of the 2nd Cavalry, on extended leave in Virginia settling the Custis estate.
- **03:51 — "The whole pace of life had accelerated in ways that still felt strange to me"** and "it had changed everything." Sociology-textbook framing under period costume.
- **05:49 — "war was mathematics—so many lives spent for so many yards gained."** "Yards gained" is a Western Front concept; nothing in the Mexican War was reckoned in yardage. "Do the arithmetic without flinching" compounds the modern flavor.
- **04:47 — "I am choosing destroyed."** Modern syntactic stunt (participle as noun-object); no 1856 hand writes this.
- **05:105 — "The Sioux call this kind of winter a white death."** Coinage smells of the 20th century, and the ethnographic aside is implausible in this speaker's mouth.
- **01:71 — "taking inventory"** as a metaphor for a glance; the figurative use is 20th-century.
- **03:59 — "moving toward something instead of away from it"** — therapeutic framing.
- **05:113 — "No, I'm telling you, this is real."** "This is real" as emphasis is modern; period speech would run to "it's a fact" or "God's truth."
- **03:89 — "a transcontinental line."** The word existed but the period term of art was the Pacific railroad. Worth changing.
- **Verify:** "Your funeral" (05:109) — attestation for the 1850s is thin; and "cobblestones" in Chicago (03:125) — Chicago streets in 1858 were notoriously mud and plank.
- **Borderline modern-lyric compressions:** "The body needs less than it believes. Mine is learning" (04:13); "I had forgotten that I was capable of noticing" (06:5); "the wanting was so strong" (02:39).

## 4. FLATSPOTS

- **01:181** — the morning handoff. After 180 lines of fully dramatized tavern scene, the entire transaction the chapter was building toward is dispatched in "They spoke briefly, exchanged what information there was to exchange." One beat of scene — Farrell's hands on the letters, one question — would pay off the chapter.
- **02:43 — "Her hooves are soft. Her weight is nothing."** Overstated into confusion. A six-month bison calf runs 300–400 pounds; "bruised but not broken" is defensible, "her weight is nothing" is not. The logic of why she can't yet kill him needs one true sentence, not two false ones.
- **02:29 — the lore paragraph.** "They believe a white buffalo calf woman brought them their most holy things, their pipe, their rituals." A solitary Virginian veteran on the plains in 1856 knows this catechism from where? One sentence of provenance — a trader, an Army guide, a book — would secure the whole conceit. As written it's authorial knowledge wearing his coat.
- **05:59 — "a small town whose name I did not catch."** The idiom is wrong (one "catches" a spoken name, not a town's), and the scene exists solely to deliver the Spirit Lake exposition; it needs either one concrete detail that makes the town a place or folding into the previous section.
- **06:67 — "I do not know what the message is. Perhaps it is different for everyone who sees her."** The entry's strongest image (the copper light, 06:65) is followed by a shrug that dissolves it. Cut the second sentence or replace with something Morrow actually risks.

## 5. BEST AND WEAKEST SENTENCES

**Best:**
1. "On my walk out I saw a man frozen solid where he sat, his eyes open, his hands still raised as if in conversation." (02:9) — one concrete image carries the whole death-wish premise; the "as if" earns its keep for once.
2. "An autumn calf, born when no calf is born." (02:43) — the mythic claim made through a husbandry fact; the book's method at its best.
3. "She did not complain directly—she was not the type, I gathered—but the shape of the complaints was visible in what she did not say." (03:9) — characterizes Catherine, Farrell, and the epistolary evidence in one motion.

**Weakest:**
1. "A weapon must be used or destroyed. I am choosing destroyed." (04:47) — the syntax is a 21st-century flourish that shatters the journal's period voice at its most solemn moment.
2. "The whole pace of life had accelerated in ways that still felt strange to me, though I had lived through it." (03:51) — analytic historiography in a hired killer's mouth; the trailing clause is pure filler.
3. "It meant nothing to me, not then. Just another strange story from a strange country." (05:121) — "not then" winks at the reader, and the sentence contradicts "I did not stop thinking about it" two lines above.

Runner-up for weakest: "like a star fallen to earth and walking among cattle" (06:23) — the simile collapses (the star walks among the herd she belongs to) and "cattle" jars from a narrator who has spent five months distinguishing buffalo from everything else.</result>

## ASSISTANT

Part One is complete through the full two-stage process — my tic pass, then a cold-context critic, then adjudication — and the process rules just proved themselves on me: I had introduced a bug (moving the letters' delivery to the morning left Aldridge checking his pocket for a packet that was now at the boarding house) and self-certified it. The cold reader caught it instantly, along with things outside my pass's lens entirely.

**What the critic caught that I'd missed:**
- **Two real seams**: the packet-location contradiction, and my "not then" cut leaving "I did not stop thinking about it" colliding with "it meant nothing to me" two lines apart. Both fixed — the wink is gone entirely now; the strange story just sits there.
- **Two period facts**: Lee left West Point in 1855 (now "held a cavalry command in Texas"), and "the Dakotas" is post-1889 (now "the upper Missouri"). Plus a rack of smaller ones: "Your funeral" → "Suit yourself," "this is real" → "it's a fact," Chicago cobblestones → planking, "transcontinental line" → "Pacific road," "war was mathematics… yards gained" → "arithmetic… ground taken."
- **Cross-narrator clause bleed my pass hadn't reached**: both men sat "listening to the guns" in the same syntax; both kept fires that were "small… but it was enough"; days "blurred together" four times across both voices. Deduplicated, keeping Morrow's originals.
- **A logic pull**: Morrow claiming "seventeen men with certainty" and "I stopped counting" in the same paragraph — kept the number, cut the abdication.
- **Two flatspots worth a rebuild**: the Chapter One morning handoff ("exchanged what information there was to exchange") is now a dramatized beat — Farrell counts the letters and holds the daguerreotype to the window light. And the white-buffalo lore now has provenance ("trader's talk, at the posts on the Missouri") instead of authorial knowledge wearing Morrow's coat.
- **Its weakest-sentence pick was one of mine to fix**: "I am choosing destroyed" — striking but a modern syntactic stunt in an 1856 journal. Now: *"A weapon must be used or destroyed. There is no more use in me."*

**Rejected with counter-briefs** (logged in the commit): the "made by Mexico" verbal parallel across both narrators is the book's deliberate rhyme, not bleed; the Broken Oar joke stays under your idiosyncrasy license; the remaining triads and one-line buttons are now at a density where further cuts would flatten voice rather than remove tic; and the critic's "every journal entry pivots to a Mexico confession" observation is real but deferred — the winter entries in Parts Two–Four already break the pattern.

Part One stands at commits `edd9696` + `34ad642`, ~470 words leaner. Parts Two through Five get the same two-stage treatment next, with one calibration carried forward: the critic's best catches were *facts and seams*, not taste — so the cold-reader prompt for the remaining Parts will keep the period-pedant lens sharp, and I'll run my own post-edit contradiction check before handing off.

## ASSISTANT

Part Two's pass is committed (`d8e6393`, ~51 edits) and its cold critic is reading now, primed with the fixed facts (bay gelding, muzzleloader, the three-day creek) so it can check arithmetic as well as prose.

Beyond the tic work, close reading surfaced two more continuity catches the audit had missed: Morrow claimed kills "at Monterrey and Buena Vista *and Chapultepec*" — Chapultepec is Farrell's campaign, not his — and Chapter Five still said the homestead was "a day's ride, maybe less" from the night of the ice crossing, three days before he arrives under the reconciled geometry. Both fixed. Journal VI needed the heaviest hand in the book so far: the felt-nothing beat compressed to four sentences, a kill-sentence duplicated verbatim from Journal V cut, the re-coined "something unfinished" removed, the essayistic time-meditation ("heavy with meaning and light with consequence") gone, and — a subtle one — Morrow echoing Catherine's letter phrase "building something that will last," which he could never have read.

When the critic reports back I'll adjudicate as before, then take the same two-stage pass through Parts Three to Five (the climax files), where the docket already includes: the forge/kiln decision (keep only the grounded kiln), the "dead eyes and silent mouths" missed revision, the remaining pre-reading bleed ("finding every gap," "pressed in from all sides"), the "I can wait" refrain's last instances, and the Chapter Eleven ending — which I'll touch with the lightest hand of all, since "nothing left to follow" is the sentence the whole revision serves.

<result>FINDINGS — line edit, drafts 07–13

## 1. SEAMS

**S1. Chapters Five and Six narrate the same leg of the journey twice, with contradictions. This is the largest defect in the section — it reads as two alternate drafts both retained.**

- drafts/09-chapter-five.md:143 — "The fourth day I found the right creek." After a day lost to a false creek ("On the third day, I crossed a creek I thought was the one I was looking for," 09:139) and eroding confidence ("I began to lose confidence in my count," 09:129).
- drafts/11-chapter-six.md:3 — "The creek the trader had described was easy enough to find." Flat contradiction of the entire lost-day sequence.
- The homestead is discovered twice: 09-chapter-five.md:171 — "On the seventh day, I found the homestead." vs. 11-chapter-six.md:51 — "I found the homestead on the afternoon of the fourth day." Chapter Five's count is three days up the creek (day 4 creek, day 7 homestead); Chapter Six's is four days up the creek. Off by one, and the discovery beat is spent before Chapter Six can use it.
- The ice contradicts itself across the seam: 09:151–155 the creek ice cracks and nearly drowns the horse ("We were halfway across when the ice cracked"); 11:3 the same creek's ice is "cloudy, thick enough to walk on," with deer crossing it. Neither chapter acknowledges the other's incident.
- 09-chapter-five.md:125 — "camped that night in a stand of pines on a south-facing slope" vs. 11-chapter-six.md:7 — "patches of pine on the northern slopes where the sun could not reach." Chapter Six's ecology (correct for the sandhills) contradicts Chapter Five's one chapter later.

**S2. Broken referent — the trader never told Farrell to count creeks.** 09-chapter-five.md:125 — "I counted the creeks as I passed them, as the trader had instructed." The instruction given (09:67) was "There's a creek comes in from the north—can't miss it, it's the only one for ten miles." No counting instruction exists; worse, "only one for ten miles" makes counting pointless, and 09:131 quotes that very fact while the narrator goes on counting. The Chapter Five search sequence hangs on a sentence that isn't in the book.

**S3. Fort Kearny arithmetic fails.** 07-chapter-four.md:13 — storm on "the ninth day"; 07:39 — "By morning the storm had passed" (day 10); 07:41 — "Fort Kearny was three days ahead. I made it in four."; 07:89 — "Fort Kearny appeared on the twelfth day." Days 10–12 is three days of travel, and the Gunderson meeting confirms it (07:45, "On the second day after the storm" = day 11, fort the next day). "I made it in four" requires day 13. Only strained inclusive counting saves it.

**S4. The last-drink continuity breaks.** 13-chapter-seven.md:9 — "I had one drink left. I saved it for morning." Then, the evening of that same day, 13:99 — "I finished the last of my whiskey that evening, drinking it slowly, trying to make it last." Both cannot be the last of the whiskey. A sentence bridging the morning drink appears to have been cut.

**S5. Gunderson's mules appear from nowhere.** 07-chapter-four.md:77 — "Take what you can carry, put the children on the mules, and head back." No team, mules or otherwise, is established in the scene (the wagon, contents, wife, and children all are). Reads as a stitched-over cut of the sentence describing the animals.

**S6. Farrell's two incompatible reconstructions of the killing, eleven paragraphs apart, unreconciled.** 13-chapter-seven.md:41 — "George Hardin, coming to answer a knock or confronting someone who had entered uninvited" (struggle at the house door). Then 13:53 — "George Hardin, I guessed. He had been in the barn when it happened, had tried to fight or flee, had failed." He revises George's location without noting the revision. Both are hedged as speculation, but a careful tracker-narrator would mark the correction; as written it reads like two drafts of the scene.

**S7. The care/violence observation is raised and then abandoned.** 13-chapter-seven.md:25 — "The care taken here did not match the violence that must have preceded it." Twelve paragraphs later, 13:69 — "I knew, with a certainty I could not have defended, that the man who followed the white buffalo... killed the people who lived here and buried them behind their barn." The one piece of evidence cutting against his conclusion is never re-engaged. If the unearned certainty is deliberate, it needs one sentence acknowledging what he's stepping over.

**S8. Journal Entry VI contradicts itself and Entry V on the homesteaders' first winter.** 12-journal-entry-vi.md:35 — "They have been here since early summer." Two sentences later, 12:37 — "The first winter is the hardest, they say, and they have survived it." They arrived June 1857 (10-journal-entry-v.md:41, June 4: "They were building something—a house, or the start of one"); by September 29, 1857 they have survived no winter. Leftover from a draft with a different arrival date.

**S9. Chapter Seven's closing direction contradicts the book's own sighting evidence.** 13-chapter-seven.md:103–105 — "rode north. The man who followed the white buffalo was out there somewhere, in the frozen emptiness beyond the last homestead. The stories I had heard all agreed on that." They don't. The trapper put him "Up on the Niobrara, maybe three weeks back" (07:117); Creighton put him "About two weeks ago, crossing the Loup Fork" (07:175) — south of the Niobrara, i.e., a southward drift, consistent with a wintering herd. The freshest sighting places the man south of the homestead; Farrell follows August tracks north and asserts unanimity that doesn't exist. Either give Farrell a reason to prefer the old trail or fix the geography of the sightings.

**S10. The cradle beat dangles.** 11-chapter-six.md:81 — "No cradle, no child's things... The letters had been silent on this point, and the house confirmed the silence." "This point" gestures at a question (pregnancy? a child?) the surrounding chapters never raise. Reading cold, the referent is broken — it looks like the setup sentence lives in an earlier chapter or was cut.

**S11. Two backstories for the same withdrawal episode.** 09-chapter-five.md:9 — "years ago, when I had run out entirely and had to wait three days for a supply train that never came." vs. 11-chapter-six.md:23 — "once, years ago, when a winter storm had trapped me without supply for too long." If these are the same event, the details drifted between drafts; if two events, "once" in Chapter Six forbids it.

**S12. Morrow claims to have lost track of time in a journal that datelines every entry to the day.** 12-journal-entry-vi.md:47 — "The days still pass, but they have lost their names" — directly under the dateline "*October the Twelfth*," in a journal that elsewhere notes "No entry for two weeks" (08-journal-entry-iv.md:17). The apparatus contradicts the claim. Either the datelines should degrade or the claim should be qualified.

Minor: 07-chapter-four.md:57 — "Three wagons passed this morning" — heavy westbound traffic two days after a trail-erasing blizzard, in December, strains belief. 13-chapter-seven.md:61 — the Hardins kept a horse and a cow (11:53); Farrell reads "the deep hoofprints of an animal heavier than any horse" as buffalo without ever eliminating the household stock.

## 2. SURVIVING MANNERISMS

**Verbatim or near-verbatim repeats across files (worst first):**

- "**I can wait.**" closes both Journal IV (08:95) and Journal VI (12:115). Identical curtain line on two entries a year apart. If intentional as ritual, it isn't earned; it reads as the same button reached for twice.
- "**barely large enough for a horse and a cow**" — 11-chapter-six.md:53 ("a lean-to shelter barely large enough for a horse and a cow") and 13-chapter-seven.md:51 ("The barn was small, barely large enough for a horse and a cow"). Verbatim, adjacent chapters.
- **The August/September deduction is run twice, nearly verbatim.** 09-chapter-five.md:117 — "Four months ago, the trader had said. That put him in this area around August, September. Around the time Catherine Hardin's letters had stopped." 13-chapter-seven.md:67 — "maybe four months back. That would have been August, September. Around the time the Hardins stopped writing letters." The reader does this arithmetic once.
- **Hollow-in-the-bluffs shelter**: 08:37 — "I am sheltering in a hollow in the bluffs, out of the wind" / 12:107 — "a hollow in the bluffs where the wind cannot reach."
- **Cold-pressing figure**: 08:61 — "It presses against you from all sides" / 12:113 — "the cold pressing in from all sides."
- **Careless-deity similes twice in two chapters**: 07:89 — "like a collection of boxes dropped by a careless god" / 09:5 — "made by a god who could not decide what he wanted."
- **Country-as-antagonist button twice in one chapter**: 09:147 — "But the country was not done with me yet." / 09:169 — "But the country had reminded me what it could do."

**Shared between the two narrators (voice bleed):**

- **Beard-freezing three times, both voices**: Farrell 07:21 — "My beard froze solid"; Farrell 11:7 — "My breath froze in my beard"; Morrow 08:27 — "The blood froze on my beard before I could wipe it away." Morrow's is the good one; the Farrell instances should yield.
- "breath steaming" four times: 07:105, 09:31, 09:165, 08:37 (plus "steaming" 08:37 herd). Both narrators.
- **The two homesteader-watching journal scenes are the same scene**: 10:67–73 (woman hanging clothes on a line, man joins, she laughs, "I did not feel anything, watching them") and 12:69–73 (woman hanging laundry, laugh carries, "I felt nothing. Not envy, not regret"). The second adds nothing the first didn't; the numbness beat lands identically twice.

**Machinery:**

- **Stacked triads are the signature tic of both voices and need thinning by half**: 08:7 — "Still white. Still visible. Still alive."; 08:21 — "She endures. The herd endures. I endure because I must."; 08:51 — "I did not move. The herd did not move. She did not move."; 07:3 — "I passed emigrants... I passed traders... I passed soldiers"; 11:57 — "No smoke rose... No light showed... No tracks disturbed"; 13:91 — "still out there, still empty, still moving."
- **One-line paragraph buttons**: 07:11 — "The first week passed without incident. The second did not."; 07:17 — "I was wrong."; 09:27 — "There was nothing else to do."; 11:39 — "I was not going to be careful."; 13:15 — "They had not been a dream."; 08:43 — "That life is over."; 10:33 — "I will wait." The device works when it carries an event (07:23); most of these carry attitude.
- **"I told myself" three times, Farrell**: 07:15, 11:33, 13:83 — the same self-deception hinge each time.
- **"I think" as Morrow's hedge, four-plus times**: 08:49, 10:41, 10:55, 12:23. Also inert "as if": 11:35 — "as if he could sense something wrong"; 09:17 — "as if grasping for something he could not quite reach" (the hand reaching IS the grasping; the hedge doubles it).
- **The empty-eyes testimony is delivered three times by three consecutive informants**: trapper (07:121 — "There was something wrong with him... No man walks like that"), Creighton (07:179 — "there was nothing in his eyes. Nothing at all"), trader (09:91–95 — "He looked at me like I wasn't there... You ever see a man who's given up?"). Three witnesses, one speech. Cut or differentiate one.
- 11:11 — "unmarked, unremarked" — wordplay; the manuscript's own rule (nothing clever) condemns it.

## 3. PERIOD/VOICE BREAKS

- **07-chapter-four.md:101, the sutler**: "Fair point." and "Just saying, if you're heading into that country, keep your eyes open." Both are late-20th-century conversational idiom. "Just saying" is the single worst period break in these seven files.
- **09-chapter-five.md:35**: "a man who looked like he had been waiting there since the ice age." Glacial theory was a fresh scientific hypothesis in 1858, not frontier vernacular. Anachronistic in register even if the term technically existed.
- **09-chapter-five.md:53–57, the trader**: "You law?" is 20th-century noir clip (period would be "You a lawman?" or "You carrying a warrant?"). "Bounty hunter?" as a compound noun for a man-hunter is likewise anachronistic; period usage runs to "man-hunter" or "hunting a bounty."
- **11-chapter-six.md:21–27 and 41**: the withdrawal passage is modern addiction-medicine framing under costume — "The first symptoms came on the third day" (11:31), "each time I drank the relief would be shorter, the return of symptoms faster" (11:41), "my body was beginning to protest its absence" (13:7). A period drunk knew the horrors and the shakes; he did not model dose-response curves. The concrete details (tremor, sweat, visions) are fine; the analytic scaffolding around them is not.
- **12-journal-entry-vi.md:45–49**: "one of many facts that together made up the shape of who I was"; "Time moves differently when you stop counting it." Contemporary-memoir phrasing and a fortune-cookie aphorism. Nineteenth-century diarists philosophized, but in period furniture.
- **13-chapter-seven.md:87**: "men who lost something essential inside themselves" — modern psychological register.
- **10-journal-entry-v.md:87**: "The man who killed seventeen men at Monterrey and Buena Vista" — a precise personal kill-count from massed volley combat is a modern sniper-memoir convention; a Mexican War infantry veteran would rarely know, let alone tally, seventeen.
- **13-chapter-seven.md:33**: "A dark stain on the floorboards" — a first-year sod house whose barn is a lean-to almost certainly has a dirt floor; Chapter Six's interior inventory (11:79) mentions no planking. Consistency as much as period.
- Verify: 07:89 gives Fort Kearny "a stockade that would not have stopped a determined child" — the historical Fort Kearny (post-1848) was famously unstockaded, open buildings around a parade ground.
- Lighter: 07:65 "I did the calculation in my head" (period: made the count/figured it); 07:107 "everyone half-wanted to check on" (modern phrasal). Gunderson's broken English (07:53–73) passes; the trapper and Creighton pass except that all five informants share the narrator's laconic-aphoristic cadence (e.g., 09:109 "the land doesn't like anyone") — a voice problem more than a period one.

## 4. FLATSPOTS

- **09-chapter-five.md:133, the antelope paragraph** — pure inventory: sees antelope, considers hunting, doesn't, nothing follows, nothing pays off. Cut or make it cost him something.
- **12-journal-entry-vi.md:41–51, the October 12 time-meditation** — the one entry with no image in it: all abstraction, ending on the aphorism flagged above. Every other entry hangs its idea on a thing seen; this one needs its object or the axe.
- **13-chapter-seven.md:39–45** — the reconstruction is cut to the point of confusion (see S6); "The story was incomplete" papers over incoherence rather than naming what's missing.
- **10-journal-entry-v.md:73 / 12:73** — the numbness beat ("I did not feel anything... But there was nothing" / "I felt nothing. Not envy, not regret") is asserted flatly both times. Once, dramatized, would do more than twice, declared.
- **08-journal-entry-iv.md:73–95, February 2–14** — "The worst of the cold has broken" and "I have survived the winter," declared February 14 in the Nebraska sandhills, where March is a killing month and no plainsman calls winter in mid-February. Also 08:75 has the herd "drifting north" in early February. Even allowing Morrow's derangement, this reads as calendar convenience, and the section's triumph note ("The herd is strong. She is strong.") is unearned by anything shown.
- **13-chapter-seven.md:101** — "Just the wind. Just the ghosts of the people who had lived here." The second sentence cashes in the ghosts cheaply; the wind alone was the truer ending.
- 11-chapter-six.md:81 — the cradle observation (see S10) needs one more true sentence or none at all.

## 5. BEST AND WEAKEST SENTENCES

**Best:**

1. 07-chapter-four.md:23 — "I found the creek by falling into it." — Event, irony, and the country's whole character in seven words; the one-line button earning its keep.
2. 11-chapter-six.md:117 — "The Hardins slept under that sound, behind their barn, and would go on sleeping under it until spring came and the water ran free." — The dead given weather instead of sentiment; the best close in the section.
3. 07-chapter-four.md:179 — "Like looking at a dead man who hadn't had the sense to fall down yet." — Character voice doing exposition's job; the only one of the three empty-eyes speeches that needed saying.

**Weakest:**

1. 09-chapter-five.md:147 — "But the country was not done with me yet." — Dime-novel personification, telegraphing the next scene, and duplicated at 09:169.
2. 10-journal-entry-v.md:5 — "carrying the winter away into the rivers and down to wherever rivers go." — Faux-naive from a narrator who has seen the Gulf of Mexico (12:87); the not-knowing is a pose.
3. 12-journal-entry-vi.md:49 — "Time moves differently when you stop counting it." — An aphorism in modern register, in a dated journal entry, from a man claiming not to count — wrong three ways at once.

No category is empty. The urgent items are S1/S2 (the Chapter Five/Six double narration and its phantom instruction), S8 (the survived-winter error), the "Just saying"/"Fair point" dialogue at 07:101, and the twice-used "I can wait."</result>

## ASSISTANT

This critic went deeper than Part One's — and its biggest finding is structural and real: **Chapters Five and Six narrate the creek leg twice**, with contradicting ice, ecology, and day counts, because Chapter Five's closing line ("On the seventh day, I found the homestead") pre-spends the discovery that Chapter Six then re-earns. The fix is clean: Chapter Five ends at the ice-crossing night, Chapter Six carries the approach and the discovery alone, and every count aligns with the trader's "three days, maybe four." It also caught a phantom instruction (Farrell "counted the creeks as the trader had instructed" — no such instruction exists), the "survived it" first-winter error in Journal VI, "Just saying" in an 1858 sutler's mouth, and the whiskey running out twice. I'm rejecting a couple (the "cradle beat dangles" referent lives in Chapters One–Two, outside its file set; the hollow-in-the-bluffs repeat is Morrow's actual wintering habit), and applying the rest — about thirty edits:

## ASSISTANT

Part Two is complete through both stages (`d8e6393` + `8700ebd`). The manuscript stands at 31,497 words — about 1,100 leaner than this morning, all of it tic, rerun, and contradiction.

The headline discovery: the critic found that **Chapters Five and Six have narrated the same creek journey twice since v0.1** — Chapter Five found the homestead "on the seventh day," then Chapter Six rewound and found it again "on the afternoon of the fourth day," with contradicting ice (cracked and nearly drowned the horse / "thick enough to walk on"), contradicting ecology, and a phantom instruction ("counted the creeks as the trader had instructed" — he never instructed that). Five revisions passed over it, including my own audit — a cold reader caught it in one sitting. The fix: Chapter Five now ends at the ice-crossing night ("But the country had reminded me what it could do."), and Chapter Six alone owns the approach and arrival, every day-count aligned. It also caught the sutler saying "Just saying" and "Fair point" (1858!), Journal VI crediting the homesteaders with surviving a winter they hadn't yet faced, and a lovely opportunity disguised as a contradiction: Morrow claiming to have lost track of time in a journal he datelines daily — now: *"I still set a date at the head of these pages, out of an old habit of order, and I believe it less each time I write it… She is the only calendar I keep now."*

What remains is Parts Three through Five (files 14–20) — and that includes the ending, which is the checkpoint the original andon plan reserved ("before Part Five: verify approach"). So before I take the pass through the climax files, here is the approach I'll use, for your reaction or veto:

- **Journal VII–IX and Chapters Eight–Ten** get the same two-stage treatment as Parts One–Two. The docket: the forge/kiln thesis (cut the ungrounded forge in Chapter Nine, keep at most a stripped kiln in Chapter Ten), "dead eyes and silent mouths" (the v0.3 missed fix), the remaining pre-reading bleed ("finding every gap," "pressed in from all sides" — fixed on Farrell's side), "They did not answer. They never answered." deduplicated, the DT-vision stock set thinned toward the one genuinely strange image (the buffalo in the empty Mexican street), and Morrow's final entries left almost untouched — the Buena Vista entry and the flat account of deciding to kill are the book's best pages.
- **Chapter Eleven** gets the lightest hand in the book: the climax's stacked-figure paragraphs ("It was not forgiveness… An acknowledgment, perhaps…") thinned per the profile, the "Peace." one-word paragraph reconsidered, the triple-"perhaps" at the Lakota sighting reduced — but the closing movement from the buffalo's turn through "nothing left to follow" I intend to leave word-for-word as v0.5 shaped it, minus at most one hedge. If any edit there feels non-obvious, I'll bring you the before/after rather than commit it.

## ASSISTANT

Parts Three through Five are committed (`5fc3d58`) and the final cold critic is reading now — with an extra lens trained specifically on whether the ending earns itself, and briefed on the sanctioned echo device so it can distinguish intentional borrowing from bleed.

The full-book pass is now applied. Manuscript at 30,422 words (~2,200 flensed from v0.6). The notable moves in this last batch, for your reaction:

**The two-men thesis is finally at outline spec.** Chapter Nine's forge passage (230 words of stated parallel, pre-reading and therefore ungroundable) is compressed to a recognition that names nothing shared: *"But I recognized him. I knew what he was, because I knew what I was… He had gone looking for something large enough to swallow it whole. That was the difference, and I did not like how small it was."* Chapter Ten keeps a single grounded figure — *"the clay was the same"* — now that Farrell has read the man's own account.

**One real continuity catch**: Journal IX had Morrow write "I killed the homesteaders and felt nothing" — directly contradicting the line Farrell quotes from the killing entry ("I did not know I would feel this"). Now: *"The homesteaders cost me something—I wrote of it at the time—but it was not enough, and it did not last."* The cross-reference also quietly explains why that entry exists for Farrell to find.

**The ending's surgery**, since you'll want to see exactly what changed there: "Peace." as a one-word paragraph became *"For the first time in longer than I could remember, I was at peace."*; the "An acknowledgment, perhaps. A recognition that…" apposition machine is gone (*"But the long journey was over, and whatever happened next did not depend on me."*); the ancestor-ghosts now appear unglossed — no "final act of a story" annotation; "something more than animal and something less than divine" is cut, leaving her simply seeming "to carry her own illumination"; the departing triad lands on *"together they moved north, toward the ridges."*; and "I watched until there was nothing left to watch" is cut so that four lines later, "nothing left to follow" is the only "nothing left" in the chapter. The horse paragraph and the final sentence are letter-for-letter as v0.5 made them.

One writing-back note: Journal VII's murder-logic gained the book a new figure in the license's spirit — *"I mean it the way one speaks of a stone in a field that must be moved before plowing"* — Morrow the farmer's son reaching for the only idiom he has for removing what's in the way. When the critic reports, I'll adjudicate, run the registry's timeline re-check, update versions.md, and tag v0.7.
