## 1. SEAMS

**A. The missing day (the worst problem in these seven files).** Ch. Nine ends with Farrell in visual contact with the living Morrow at sunrise: "Far ahead, perhaps a mile, a dark shape moved against the white—the man" (17-chapter-nine.md:81), "He was watching her, I knew. Waiting for something" (:83), "I rode forward, slowly, keeping my distance" (:97). Ch. Ten then opens: "I found the camp at dusk" (19-chapter-ten.md:3), and Ch. Ten:71 pins the sighting to the same day: "The valley below, where I had seen him yesterday, was perhaps a mile away." Per the fixed timeline, Morrow died at dawn Jan 8 — i.e., the "dark shape" Farrell watched at sunrise was Morrow's death-walk. So Farrell, mounted, a mile off, riding toward him, takes until dusk to arrive at the camp, and neither sees nor hears a buffalo killing a man in an open valley he was overlooking. The kill site's "churned up, trampled" snow (20-chapter-eleven.md:5) would have been in his sightline. Ten hours vanish, unaccounted for. Either Ch. Nine must not put him that close, or Ch. Ten needs a bridge (whiteout, collapse, lost sightline in the descent). As written, it reads like a witnessed-death scene was cut and the stitches left showing.

**B. Day-count collision between Ch. Eight and Ch. Nine.** Ch. Eight counts days from the homestead: "I lost the trail on the third day" (15-chapter-eight.md:35), "The fourth day was the worst" (:63), "On the fifth day, I found the trail again" (:81). Ch. Nine, which reads as strictly subsequent ("They had been with me for days now," 17-chapter-nine.md:7), then narrates "I saw Catherine Hardin on the second day out from her grave" (:13) and "On the third day, I understood something" (:35) — days already consumed inside Ch. Eight's span, with no flashback signal. The grave and the homestead are the same origin point; the two counts cannot both be true as sequenced.

**C. A pre-reading echo — a violation of the book's one sanctioned device.** Morrow's signature construction: "her white coat bright against the green" (14-journal-entry-vii.md:5), "her white coat bright against the brown of the dead grass" (16-journal-entry-viii.md:77). In Ch. Eight — before Farrell has found the journal — his dream: "her coat bright against the brown of the buildings around her" (15-chapter-eight.md:145). That is Morrow's phrasing in Farrell's mouth two chapters early. By the book's own rule, this is an error. (Related, softer: Farrell's pre-reading "the white buffalo stood in the snow and waited" (17-chapter-nine.md:85) anticipates Morrow's waiting-motif — "The white buffalo is waiting for me" (16:103), "She is waiting" (18:59, 81) — and Farrell's pre-reading "I had tried to fill the emptiness" (17:41) anticipates Morrow's "a life spent in emptiness" (18-journal-entry-ix.md:51).)

**D. The murder entry the reader never gets.** Entry VII ends Aug 3: "I cannot wait any longer" (14:97). Entry VIII opens Sept 3 with "The summer has passed" (16:5) and never mentions the killings. Yet Ch. Ten quotes an entry from that gap — "*I did not know I would feel this,* he had written afterward. *I thought I was past feeling*" (19:45) — and Entry IX refers to it: "The homesteaders cost me something—I wrote of it at the time" (18:53). Two later texts point at a page the book withholds; Entry VIII's total pastoral silence about a double murder committed four weeks earlier reads as a torn-out leaf, not a choice.

**E. Broken referent at the climax.** "And there were others now. People I did not recognize—a man and a woman, older, dressed in the clothes of an earlier time" (20:43). Who? If Farrell's parents (established at 15:129), he would recognize them and their clothes would not be "of an earlier time." If not, they refer to nothing in the book. This reads like the residue of a cut apparition.

**F. Timeline arithmetic in Morrow's own count.** Sept 15, 1858: "the two years I have followed her" (16:17) — fine. Jan 1, 1859: "The third winter I will spend in her company" (16:75) — fine. But the final entry, Jan 8, 1859: "I have followed her across these plains for two years" (18:45). From April 19, 1856 (19:27) that is two years and nine months; a man who correctly counts three winters a week earlier should not undercount here.

**G. Physical continuity at the body.** Morrow is found "face-down" (20:5) after ~24 hours, yet on rolling him over "His face was peaceful... the absolute stillness of his features" (20:11). A face pressed into snow for a day and a night is frozen, frost-caked, distorted — not legible serenity. And "the drift that had formed around him" (20:5) implies wind deposition that would also have softened the "churned up, trampled" snow said to be legible "for yards in every direction" (20:5).

**H. Minor.** "streaks of dark that I knew, even from a distance, was blood" (20:5) — agreement failure; "streaks... were." Also Ch. Eight:87 "moving with a steady stride, never varying, never stopping" vs. Ch. Nine:47 "he was slowing down, stopping more often" — the change in the tracks is never registered as a change.

## 2. SURVIVING MANNERISMS

**Verbatim frames repeated.** "They did not answer. They never answered" (15:31) / "She did not answer. She never answered" (17:19). And "Catherine Hardin was among them now" (17:87) / "Catherine Hardin was among them now" (19:55) — she is introduced as newly arrived twice.

**"Perhaps X. Perhaps Y." — shared across both narrators, which the sanctioned device does not cover.** Morrow: "Perhaps it is only that... Or perhaps it is something else" (14:21–23); "Perhaps she does. Perhaps she has always known" (16:93); "Perhaps I was. Perhaps this has all been the work of a broken mind" (18:69). Farrell: "Or perhaps it was simple. Perhaps I was just tired of turning back" (15:49); "Perhaps I was too tired, or too sick. Or perhaps I understood" (20:31). Roughly twenty "perhaps" instances across seven files; the doubled figure is the two narrators' identical tic.

**Negation-pivot openers/buttons, both narrators.** "It was not a decision exactly" (15:55); "I do not mean it the way a man means it" (14:51); "It is not clear" (16:33); "The silence was not silent" (17:89); "It was not forgiveness" (20:47); "It is not a complicated plan. It does not need to be" (16:69).

**Stacked triads, both narrators.** "could not hold my hands steady... could not load my rifle... could not even grip the reins" (15:9); "When she walks... When she runs... When she turns her head" (16:19); "The face of a man... The sound a woman made... The smell of the hospital tents" (17:29); "There was the wind... There was the crunch... there was the blood" (17:91); "This time I will not stop. This time I will not keep my distance. This time I will run" (18:63); "He had been trampled, gored, broken" (20:13).

**One-line buttons close nearly every section** — "I cannot wait any longer" (14:97), "So I went north, into the white" (15:59), "We went on" (15:123), "Soon" (16:87), "The white buffalo was waiting" (19:75). When every scene ends on a punchline, the final page's real one is devalued.

**"might have been" three times in three consecutive Farrell chapters:** "a voice that might have been words" (17:91), "something darker that might have been blood" (19:23), "something in them that might have been satisfaction" (20:11).

**"into the white" five times:** 15:59, 15:73, 15:91, 15:105; "she faded into the white" (17:21).

**"white on white" twice:** 17:81 and 20:21 — the first is pre-reading Farrell, so it cannot claim echo status.

**"bright against the" four times** (14:5, 16:77, 20:7, 20:21) — twice inside Morrow alone; twice in one Farrell chapter, diluting the one place the echo is licensed.

**Illegibility bookkeeping in Ch. Eleven:** the buffalo's eyes "utterly without expression" (20:21), Catherine's "expression one of wonder" (20:41), the riders' "expressions were impossible to read" (20:63) — three expression-reports in one short chapter, and pairing the Lakota with the animal in shared inscrutability is an unfortunate rhyme.

**"hollow" five literal uses in Ch. Ten** (19:3, 15, 19, 33) hosting Morrow's figurative "this hollow thing I have become" (19:53) — the hollow man camped in a hollow is exactly the kind of cuteness the project's own checklist says to cut.

## 3. PERIOD/VOICE BREAKS

**Historical error, the big one — Buena Vista.** Morrow "enlisted in the army in eighteen forty-five" and fought "Palo Alto, Resaca de la Palma, Monterrey" (18:19–21) — that is a regular's record. Then: "I was in the center of the line, with the Mississippi Rifles" (18:31). The 1st Mississippi was a volunteer regiment mustered in June 1846; a Virginian regular of 1845 was not in it. Worse, Taylor's regular infantry had been stripped away to Scott before Buena Vista — the battle was fought by volunteers precisely because the regulars were gone. And the Mississippi Rifles' famous stand was on the American left/plateau (the "V" against Torrejón's cavalry), not "the center of the line." The set-piece description — "wave after wave... We shot them down and they kept coming... climbing over their own dead" (18:33) — is frontal-assault imagery foreign to Buena Vista's fluid flanking fight. This is the confession at the heart of the book and it will not survive a knowledgeable reader.

**Lexical anachronisms.** "Homesteaders" (14:7, 15, 47, 67; 18:53, 75) — the word belongs to the post-1862 Homestead Act; an 1858 diarist writes "settlers" or "emigrants." ("Homestead" itself is fine.) "Bedroll" (19:5, 19) — early-20th-century word; period would be "blankets" or "bed."

**Modern psychological architecture under period costume.** "I knew my mind was producing them" (15:23) — clinical framing; an 1859 sufferer says phantoms, the horrors. "I had used whiskey and time and the simple practice of not thinking to put it somewhere I did not have to see it. But now the walls I had built were crumbling" (17:31) — 20th-century repression mechanics. "at the edges of my perception" (17:5). "He had gone looking for something large enough to swallow it whole" (17:41) — therapeutic metaphor. "It does not matter. The belief is enough" (18:69) — mid-20th-century existentialist cadence, not an 1859 Virginian's. "The world had become unreliable" (17:3) — narratological abstraction as a chapter opener.

**DTs phenomenology.** The presentation — tremor, preserved insight ("I knew they were not real," 15:23), formed apparitions, one generalized seizure — actually matches alcoholic hallucinosis plus withdrawal seizure reasonably well. The *duration* does not: seizures cluster 12–48 hours after the last drink, not on "the fourth day" of an already-shaking trail (15:63–69), and the whole syndrome peaks by day four and clears within a week; Farrell's plateaus and escalates across ten-plus days of cold and starvation. Stretched two- to threefold for structural convenience.

**Lakota depiction.** They appear twice, both times as mute validating chorus: "they are waiting to see what I will do" (16:49–55) and the Ch. Eleven riders — "their faces painted or simply weathered to the color of old copper" (20:63), "their expressions were impossible to read." Painted faces on a winter traveling party is Hollywood shorthand; "old copper" is stock. More substantively, a white buffalo cow of this significance going unclaimed and her white shadow unconfronted for nearly three years strains belief, and the book's only gloss is Morrow's vague "The Indians know this. They have stories about her" (18:47) — no Ptesáŋwiŋ, no specificity anywhere. The vision-hedge (20:65) buys cover for the ending's mysticism, not for the thinness.

**Minor.** The Chapultepec dream's "We killed them. All of them. Every one" (17:65) — most cadets surrendered; as guilt-warped dream it is licensed, but the text frames the dream as truer than memory ("in the dream, I knew," 17:67), which asserts the falsehood.

## 4. FLATSPOTS

**A. Ch. Nine pre-empts Ch. Ten.** The recognition scene — "I recognized him. I knew what he was, because I knew what I was" (17:39), "He had gone looking for something large enough to swallow it whole" (17:41) — gives Farrell the journal's entire content by intuition, before he reads it. Ch. Ten's post-reading epiphany then restates it: "I had found a man who was not so different from me... the clay was the same" (19:59). The all-night reading, the book's structural hinge, delivers an insight the narrator and reader already own. Cut the Ch. Nine version or make Ch. Ten's differ.

**B. The climactic decision is narrated in the book's default shrug.** Farrell's one active choice — sparing the buffalo — gets: "I did not raise the rifle. / I do not know why. Perhaps I was too tired, or too sick. Or perhaps I understood that she was not my enemy" (20:29–31). The perhaps-doubling and "I do not know why" are the same hedge machinery used a dozen times on trivia; deployed here, it dissipates the only moment that must be decisive. This is restraint tipped into inertness.

**C. Entry IX re-runs Entry VIII's plan nearly verbatim.** "I will run at her... I will force her to react... she will choose fight. She will lower her head and charge" (16:65–67) vs. "I will run toward her, and I will force her to see me as a threat, and she will do what her nature demands" (18:63). The final entry spends its capital restating mechanics instead of only the Buena Vista confession, which is its real content.

**D. The unseen death, squared.** Both narrators miss the kill — Morrow's account ends before it, Farrell arrives a day late. Defensible as design, but combined with 1A (Farrell visibly close at the fatal dawn, then an unexplained cut to dusk) it reads not as withholding but as absence. The reader is never given even the sound of it.

**E. The old couple (20:43)** — cut past comprehension; see 1E.

## 5. THE ENDING

The last page mostly earns itself. The buffalo's departure carries the sanctioned echo well ("like a ship through water," 20:59), and "She had done what her nature demanded" (20:31) and "I was at peace" (20:33) are the echo device working as intended (Morrow: "what her nature demands," 18:63; "a great and quiet peace," 16:97). The horse paragraph (20:73) is the best-judged beat on the page — the pull back toward the living world routed through the animal, ending on "He would carry me, if I could make myself move." The riders are the weak link: "appearing on the ridge like figures out of a dream" (20:63) is a dead simile made redundant two lines later when the text literally entertains that they are a vision ("Or another vision—another gift from the fever, in a day full of impossible things," 20:65 — "a day full of impossible things" instructs the reader how to feel); the leader's long look and turn away (20:67) is the one live element there. "she fell in among them" (20:69) — the drill-idiom verb from a soldier narrator I read as deliberate and good.

**Weakest sentence in Ch. Eleven:** "The end he had sought for so long had finally come" (20:11). The peaceful face and open eyes have already said this; the sentence explains its own image, and "finally" is dead weight.

**Strongest sentence in Ch. Eleven:** "I was not moving. I was kneeling in an empty valley beside a man I had never met, with blood on the snow and nothing left to follow" (20:75). "A man I had never met" is the whole book in five words; "nothing left to follow" collapses trail, buffalo, and purpose in one clause. (Runner-up: "Beyond her, at the far end of the valley, the herd stood bunched and dark. None of them came near," 20:25.)

## 6. BEST AND WEAKEST SENTENCES ACROSS ALL SEVEN FILES

**Best:**
1. "I mean it the way one speaks of a stone in a field that must be moved before plowing." (14:51) — the murder's moral horror rendered entirely in period-true agricultural idiom; the book's coldest and most credible line.
2. "A sweaty horse in winter is a dying horse, if you cannot get him dry." (15:109) — hard, earned competence; the kind of texture that makes everything around it believable.
3. "The want of drink had crowded out every smaller want." (17:49) — period diction ("want of drink") doing precise psychological work with zero modern apparatus.

**Weakest:**
1. "The woman is never far from the house, cooking and cleaning and doing whatever it is that women do to keep a home running." (14:15) — "whatever it is that women do" is authorial slackness wearing Morrow's detachment as an excuse; it costs the diarist his observational authority.
2. "It lasted for what felt like hours, though it was probably only minutes, and when it finally stopped I was lying in the snow outside my blankets, my face wet with sweat and tears." (15:69) — the felt-like-hours/probably-minutes hedge is stock, and it defuses the seizure it describes.
3. "The end he had sought for so long had finally come." (20:11) — over-explanation at the exact moment the book most needs to trust its image; see section 5.

**Empty categories:** none. Every category returned findings.

**Priority order if revising:** 1A (the missing day), the Buena Vista/Mississippi Rifles error, 1C (the pre-reading echo in Ch. Eight's dream), 4A/4B (the pre-empted revelation and the hedged climax), then the mannerism dedup.</result>

## ASSISTANT

This is the strongest critique of the three — and its top finding is real: between the sunrise sighting and the dusk camp-arrival, thirty-six hours vanish unexplained, right across Morrow's death. The Buena Vista error is also serious and has an elegant fix (Morrow left Virginia young for the river trade and was in Vicksburg when the war came — which keeps Davis, Monterrey, *and* Buena Vista, all historically sound for the Mississippi Rifles, and dovetails with the existing "I went *back* to Virginia"). I'm accepting most of the docket, including un-sanctioning the "clay" echo so Chapter Nine owns sameness and Chapter Ten owns only the difference. Rejecting with reasons: the DT duration (his last drink is December 27, seizure day 3–4 — in range, prolonged by cold and starvation), and the unseen death stays unseen by design. Executing — globals first:

## ASSISTANT

**v0.7 is complete and tagged.** The full style pass is done: every file in the book edited against the profile, every Part cold-read by a fresh-context critic, three rounds of adjudication, all committed.

The final critic was the best of the three, and its two biggest catches were exactly the kind that would have embarrassed the book in the wild:

- **The missing thirty-six hours.** Farrell sees Morrow alive at sunrise, a mile off — then the next chapter opens at dusk the following day with no account of the gap, across the very morning Morrow dies. Chapter Ten now opens with a four-sentence bridge: the valley folds into draws, the sightline goes, he camps, and he spends the next short day working along the rim — which puts him blind in the draws at the fatal dawn, preserving the unseen death as *design* rather than absence.
- **Buena Vista was historically impossible as written.** An 1845 regular-army Virginian could not have been in the Mississippi Rifles — a volunteer regiment — and Taylor's regulars were gone before the battle. The fix threads every needle: Morrow *"left Virginia young and worked the river trade, and was at Vicksburg when the war came"* — volunteer, 1846, Davis's regiment, Monterrey and Buena Vista both, on the plateau — and it dovetails with the already-canonical "I went *back* to Virginia" after the war.

Also from this round: "homesteaders" is gone manuscript-wide (the word is post-1862; they're settlers now), Journal VIII now marks the withheld killing entry as Morrow's own silence (*"I have written of what was done in it, and I will not write of it again."*), the corpse physics work (he lies on his back; Farrell brushes the snow from his face), the riders lost their Hollywood paint and "old copper," the referentless ancestor-ghosts are cut, and the rifle decision is now declarative — *"I did not raise the rifle. She was not my enemy. She had done what her nature demanded."* — the one moment the book cannot afford to hedge. The critic's pick for strongest sentence in the book was the final one, unchanged since v0.5; its pick for best line overall was one this pass wrote — the stone in the field.

**Where things stand:** 30,335 words (from 32,639 this morning); "perhaps" down from 96 to 53; two milestone tags today (`v0.6-continuity`, `v0.7-style`). Every audit finding closed, all voice-bleed resolved with the registry enforcing the borrowed-language rule, and every reference doc in sync.

**Carried forward, logged:** the "year and a half" phrasing (your note — awaiting a better rhythm), the critique's §12 style leftovers, and two additive candidates from the final critic now in expansion-hooks with guardrails — the biggest being the one real thinness no line edit can fix: the Lakota's three-year distance from an unclaimed sacred animal, which is research-first, additive-later work that will pair naturally with the image-gen phase. That's where I'd point the next session.

## ASSISTANT

The research is done and committed (`a69c4ca`), and it turned up something better than texture — a structural answer that was hiding in the book's own dates.

**The timeline lock.** Blue Water Creek — Harney's massacre of Little Thunder's Brulé, 86 killed including women and children, in country just southwest of our setting — was September 1855. The Fort Pierre council, where Harney dictated "obedience and docility" to the leaders of the seven Lakota bands, was March 1856. Morrow sees the calf in *April 1856*. The entire novel runs inside the first three years of the post-Harney dictated peace, when Brulé bands were grieving, watched by the Army, and deliberately keeping wide of white men. A band declining to approach a wakan animal shadowed by a solitary white man isn't thin — it's the historically correct posture for these exact years. And the book already planted the seed without knowing it: the Fort Kearny sutler's "Sioux been restless since Blue Water" is the white man's obtuse reading of a people keeping their distance.

**The complication that makes it richer:** white buffalo weren't automatically left alive — Catlin saw a white robe raised as a supreme offering in the Mandan village in 1832, killed by Blackfeet and bought for eight horses. So the ceremonial tradition ran through the *consecrated hide* as much as the living animal. Leaving her alive and attended for three years is therefore a *choice* with weight — which both the Ptesáŋwiŋ living-sign tradition and the political moment support, and which makes the ending's leading-her-away-alive mean more.

**Three beats designed** (drafted in expansion-hooks for your reaction, not yet in the text), all inside the narrators'-eyes-only rule:
- **B1, the offering** — Morrow finds a peeled stake with a weathered strip of red cloth where the riders had watched: she has been *attended* all along; neither man could read it. This one retroactively deepens the ending most.
- **B2, the band that keeps wide** — a village at horizon distance bending its line of march around Morrow, "the way a river bends around a snag."
- **B3, sharpening the existing seed** — one trail voice allowed to correct the sutler: "Restless ain't the word. Harney did his work on the Blue Water. They keep wide of us now, and I don't fault them."
- **B4, rejected** — a winter-count beat: real background (the Lakota record of her predates and outlasts both men's documents), but neither narrator can see it, so it stays out of the text and silently informs the ending instead.

One verify-flag noted in the reference doc: specific white-buffalo year-entries in the winter-count literature should be checked in Greene & Thornton's *The Year the Stars Fell* before anything leans on them.

Sources: [Battle of Ash Hollow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ash_Hollow), [Smithsonian on the Blue Water massacre](https://www.smithsonianmag.com/history/how-recovering-history-little-known-lakota-massacre-could-heal-generation-pain-180985226/), [NPS: Harney Re-Examined](https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/harney-re-examined-part-ii-harney-s-treatment-of-native-americans.htm), [All About Bison: white buffalo / Catlin's Mandan robe](https://allaboutbison.com/white-buffalo/), [Wikipedia: Winter count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_count), [High Dog Winter Count](http://thefirstscout.blogspot.com/2015/12/the-high-dog-winter-count.html).

My recommendation: B1 and B3 go in, B2 if Journal VI takes it gracefully — about 120 words total, the book's first additive work, each earning its place by the same standard everything else survived. Say the word and I'll write them in.

## ASSISTANT

Woven in and committed (`49603a0`) — the book's first additive work, 158 words. The three beats as they now stand in the text:

**Journal IV gains a new dated entry** (January 9, 1857, six days after the riders):
> *The herd crossed the ridge where the riders had watched. Where they had sat their horses I found a peeled stake standing in the ground, a strip of red cloth tied at its head. It had been there since before the snow, by the way it stood, and the wind had worked the cloth to threads. It faced the valley where the herd winters.*
>
> *I did not touch it. I did not camp near it.*

The stake predating the snow is the quiet payload: she was being attended before Morrow ever saw a rider. Neither man's account could read it; the ending's riders were never absent.

**Journal VI** (October 1857): the village passing two miles west, *"their line bent wider as it came abreast of me, the way a river bends around a snag"* — post-Harney avoidance made visible, and Morrow characteristically reads being shunned as suiting him.

**Chapter Four**: Creighton now corrects the sutler's obtuseness — *"The fort people will tell you the Sioux are restless up there. Restless ain't the word. Harney did his work on the Blue Water, and they keep wide of us now. I don't fault them."* — the one trail voice allowed to say the true thing.

**And the verification landed**, better than expected: the winter-count link isn't just plausible, it's embodied in a documented person — **White Cow Killer**, a real Oglala winter-count keeper whose calendar Corbusier collected for Mallery's Smithsonian comparative work. A man named for killing a white buffalo cow, entrusted with his band's history. That verifies the practice, its name-conferring significance, and the intersection of both traditions in one figure — and it sharpens the book's tension in exactly the right direction: killing her was real and *honored* practice, so the bands' three-year choice to attend her instead, in the Harney years, carries genuine weight. It has no visible effect on the text, as you predicted — but it's now load-bearing background in the reference doc, and it means the offering stake is the tip of something historically true.

Manuscript at 30,493 words. Sources: [Mallery/White Cow Killer context](http://faculty.humanities.uci.edu/tcthorne/wintercount/whatisawintercount.html), [Winter count (Lone Dog's 1850–51 entry)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_count), [Greene & Thornton, *The Year the Stars Fell*](https://blogs.loc.gov/loc/2022/11/lakota-winter-count-artistry/).

> *Upstream the railroad bridge stood black on its piers, the first ever thrown across the river. Its draw was open for a steamer coming down.*

>

**The newspaper** (Chapter Two, on the cars): a left-behind paper — Kansas, the Congress, and Davis *unnamed*: "a speech on the Southern question by a man I had watched lead his volunteers into the streets of Monterrey" (the one battle where both campaigns, and both narrators' wars, overlap) — folded and put under the seat. **The anniversary rework** (your note, resolved): the unit-anaphora is gone in favor of season-counting — *"It was spring of eighteen fifty-six when I first saw her… Since then, two summers of walking and watching, and one winter that tried its best to kill me."* — which also quietly seeds the entry's later "She is the only calendar I keep now."

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   Daniel Klein (DK) is revising *White Buffalo*, a ~30k-word literary Western novella (1858–59, Nebraska Territory) co-written with Ben Cohen and Claude (Opus 4.5 drafted the prose; humans conceived/directed/edited). Sequence of requests:
   - Align on playing the book straight (no meta-textual games) — agreed and codified.
   - Build an evidence-based critical profile of Opus 4.5 (tics, style, "discovery-not-invention" process) to drive revision; it stays out of the text (may inform credits only); explicitly do NOT build an equivalent profile of the human co-authors ("Avoiding glazing and anti-glazing seems like it would burn a lot of attention").
   - Assess integrating a Z-Image-style image-gen process; adopted as coherence auditor (visual bible + prompt-extraction discipline), not imagination source. Constraint: "(Don't treat my decisions in ../z-image as 'word of God' about White Buffalo.)" — text is only canon.
   - Work largely autonomously; DK is a director giving taste notes ("longer ears" style); andon cord without strict budget; amended craft principle: don't force inert details — license weird/sweaty/idiosyncratic significant ones; orphaned payoffs acceptable.
   - Standing permission: "You have my permission to enable LFS and do other flake.nix-level changes as needed to support image-gen or other authorial tasks."
   - Geo-ledger (nominal lat-longs, daily cadence) to surface travel/geography glitches — built, hugely productive.
   - No fixed word-count target (supersedes ~60k); v0.5 length felt right; expansion welcomed but lowest priority; "later versions to feel right for a longer text"; additive work expected eventually, possibly with image-gen.
   - Buffalo "wrong twice over" approach approved: "If we have any lampshade-hanging budget, it makes sense to blow it exactly here."
   - Borrowed-language device endorsed (Morrow's phrasing in Farrell post-reading): "keep an eye on the cases of it we allow, in case further timeline jugglings make any of them ungrounded."
   - Full style pass ("Onwards!"), then Lakota-gap research, then "Great find. Weave it in. You can try for that verification even if it does not immediately have an observable effect on the text."
   - Most recently: "Suggested next actions look good. Proceed." → completed §12 leftover + top transfer beats, tagged v0.8.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Two-stage revision protocol (from CLAUDE.md process rules, empirically validated): my edit pass → fresh-context cold-critic agent (general-purpose, assigned persona, no conversation context) → adjudication with accept/reject reasons. "Self-review is not clearance"; "evaluations are briefs, not verdicts."
   - Fork agents (inherit context) for evidence extraction; general-purpose agents for cold critique.
   - Central critique thesis: Opus 4.5 is a *rhetorical* writer, not an *evental* one — intensity via stacked figures (negation-restatement, triads-ending-in-abstraction, hedged apposition, epistrophic doubling, cadence-line closers, "perhaps"/"something" density).
   - Borrowed-language grounding rule: Morrow-phrasing legitimate in Farrell only AFTER the reading night (Jan 8–9, 1859); earlier = bleed to fix on Farrell's side; re-verify on any timeline change.
   - Geo-ledger method: nominal coordinates + daily calendar makes travel claims computable.
   - Canonical calendar: hired ~Nov 1; Chicago dep Nov 8; Mississippi day 6; Council Bluffs "seventeen days out"; CB dep Nov 27; Kearny Dec 9 ("thirteenth day"); Gunderson Dec 7 (before fort); Creighton Dec 12; post Dec 19; homestead Dec 26 ("afternoon of the fourth day" up creek = day 7 from post); north Dec 28; sighting sunrise Jan 7; camp dusk Jan 8; Morrow dies dawn Jan 8; body found dawn Jan 9. JE VIII last entries Jan 1 / Jan 7 1859.
   - Historical grounding: Blue Water Creek massacre Sept 3 1855; Fort Pierre council March 1856; story runs inside post-Harney dictated peace (the structural answer to the Lakota-distance "thinness"); White Cow Killer (real Oglala winter-count keeper — verifies killing white cows was named-honored practice); Mississippi Rifles = the unique both-Monterrey-and-Buena-Vista volunteer regiment (hence Morrow as Vicksburg volunteer 1846); Rock Island bridge (Apr 1856, Effie Afton May 1856, repaired Sept 1856); Oswego & Syracuse RR 1848; Watertown & Rome RR 1851; M&M RR railhead Iowa City 1856–60; "homesteader" is post-1862 (→"settlers"); "the Dakotas" post-1889; Lee not at West Point in 1858.
   - Do-not-illustrate rule: evidence, not events (killings, Morrow's death, ending resolution off-page).
   - Memory files at /home/dlk/.claude/projects/-home-dlk-workspace-white-buffalo/memory/: MEMORY.md, z-image-not-canon.md, collaboration-mode.md.

3. Files and Code Sections:

     - JE VI (12): v0.8 anniversary rework: "A year and a half. / It was spring of eighteen fifty-six when I first saw her... Since then, two summers of walking and watching, and one winter that tried its best to kill me."; village beat: "Their line bent wider as it came abreast of me, the way a river bends around a snag"; "She is the only calendar I keep now."
     - JE VII (14): "I mean it the way one speaks of a stone in a field that must be moved before plowing."
     - JE VIII (16): "I have written of what was done in it, and I will not write of it again."; "Grown early—no cow reaches her size in three summers..."

Groundwork done — the repo is ready for you to restart the session inside the new environment.

**What's in place (commits 90126f9, f3ba9ee):**
- **flake.nix** — merged shell: the writing tools (pandoc, vale, make) plus the image-gen toolchain (uv, ruff, CUDA/cuDNN, git-lfs). It builds cleanly, sees the 4090, and picks up your HF token from `/etc/nixos/secrets/`.
- **Git LFS** — enabled locally; `.gitattributes` routes `art/**` images and `*.safetensors` through LFS. Layout convention: `imagegen/output/` for working generations (gitignored), `art/` for curated finals (LFS). Base model weights never enter the repo.
- **imagegen/** — uv project pinned to the known-working `../z-image` stack.
- **planning/image-gen.md** — the phase plan and rules: prompts derived only from the visual bible with citations alongside; the do-not-illustrate list enforced at the catalog level; generation failures treated first as coherence findings against the text.

**Model decision (from a web survey I commissioned): stay on Z-Image.** There is no successor — Z-Image-Edit/Omni were never published. The real news is **Z-Image (Base)**, the non-distilled checkpoint (Jan 2026, Apache-2.0, in diffusers upstream, first-class in musubi-tuner) — it fixes the old problem of LoRA-training against distilled Turbo. Plan: Turbo for fast drafts, Base for finals and LoRA training. Fallback if LoRA fidelity disappoints: FLUX.2 klein 4B base (also Apache-2.0). Full rationale and sources are in the planning doc.

One flag from recon: the old `../z-image` prompts are thoroughly non-canon (albino buffalo, blue uniforms at Buena Vista, and scenes like `killer_approach` that violate do-not-illustrate outright), and the existing Morrow LoRA was trained on that reference set — so the prompt catalog and character references get rebuilt from the visual bible, and the LoRA retrained.

**To restart:** `nix develop` in the repo, then launch Claude Code from inside it (the shell is pre-built, so it's instant). First moves in the fresh session: `uv sync` in `imagegen/`, smoke-test generation against the cached Z-Image weights, then start the prompt catalog.

**The six candidates are ready for your notes** — `imagegen/output/morrow-candidates/` (contact sheet: `morrow-contact-sheet.png`). My read:

- **Busts:** `s101` has a strong ruined face but the beard is too short for canon ("down past his chest"). `s102` is the best on-spec bust — full chest-length beard, long grayed hair, hollowed face. `s103` is the most extreme; the eyes read as sunken shut and the whole face is nearly cadaverous — arguably that's JE IX Morrow, but as an identity anchor it's already the end state.
- **Full figures:** all three land the silhouette. `s201` drew the knife stuck bare in the ground (spec says sheathed; easily re-prompted). `s202` carries the blanket and reads right. `s203` has the strongest silhouette — blanket roll under arm, rags, the gaunt vertical against empty snow.
- **One consistency flag:** the hairline wanders across seeds (s201/s203 go balding at the crown). The LoRA will lock whichever we anchor, so the pick matters: my recommendation is **s102 (bust) + s203 (full)** as the identity anchors — they're the closest facial cousins and the most on-spec.

Next step once you've given notes (or blessed the anchors): breed the full reference set (~16–24 images) from the chosen anchor — same identity across angles, distances, and settings — then set up the musubi-tuner training env (needs the gcc fix in the flake) and train against Z-Image Base.

Degree of cadaverousness on s102 and s103 crossess into "Monster Manual" territory, and is hard to square with a "peaceful" expression in Chapter Eleven. And s201 and s202 have an unfortunate "googly eye" appearance that breaks the engraving style.

The knife seems too prominent in all the full figures, although having it appear in anchor images is probably necessary to lock its appearance down for just the cases where it does appear.

Balding seems too aggressive on the hairline given his age, unless you think his physical condition would induce balding?

Let's try another round of candidates trying to correct these various incidental issues, so we can focus more on characterization and personal appearance.

## ASSISTANT

Round 2 is generating (six images, fresh seeds). On the balding question: starvation does cause hair loss, but as **diffuse thinning** (telogen effluvium) — lank, sparse hair shedding all over — not the male-pattern crown baldness the model drew, which is an age/genetics signature and reads ten years too old. If we ever want his condition legible in the hair, "lank and thinning" is the honest rendering; for now I've asserted a full head of long unkempt hair to kill the drift, since legibility of the identity anchor matters more than a subtle malnutrition cue the plate resolution would bury anyway.

Round-2 prompt changes, for the record: expression rebuilt around "lean and hollow-cheeked but alive," "quiet still expression, level far-off gaze" (a face that can plausibly die peaceful in Ch 11); negatives added against skull/corpse/zombie, bulging/staring eyes, and balding; knife retained but "small, sheathed, at his belt"; blanket roll kept under one arm. I'll put up the contact sheet when they land.

Round 3 fixed the busts. The field, with my read:

- **`s121` is the anchor.** Full beard well past the chest, long lank center-parted hair, hollow cheeks sitting on visibly young bone structure, and calm, level, *alive* eyes — the face can wait three years and still die peaceful. **Recommended anchor pair: `s121` (bust) + `s211` (full figure)** — they're plausible readings of the same man (dark center-parted hair, long dark beard, composed bearing), and s211 carries the kit correctly (blanket roll under arm, coat in rags, knife unobtrusive). `s213` is the more striking silhouette if you'd rather lean prophet-in-rags, but its hair runs wilder than s121's.

## ASSISTANT

The full run is off — 23 images queued (18 Morrow breed variants, then the 5 Farrell candidates), roughly a half hour, resumable if interrupted since both scripts skip existing outputs. Scripts and the Farrell spec committed at 8e8a972.

The Farrell contrast is built on six axes against Morrow, each either canon or a flagged reference choice: compact and squared where Morrow is tall and attenuated; cropped sandy hair vs long dark lank; a trail beard of weeks vs a beard of years; hatted (07:85) vs bareheaded; armed and provisioned (the rifle he's carried since the war) vs "no gun, only a knife"; and a watchful, *measuring* gaze vs Morrow's level far-off one — the tracker who reads things vs the man who watches one thing. The canon anchors are thin for Farrell (Ch 1's sketch: medium height, lean, weathered past his years, pale eyes, scarred knuckles), so more of him is reference choice than Morrow — flagged accordingly in `farrell-reference.md` for your sign-off when the candidates are up.

When the run finishes I'll contact-sheet both sets: the Morrow breed for curation down to facial cousins of the anchors, and the Farrell five for your contrast notes.

## ASSISTANT

The full read is done (all twenty files, one sitting) and committed at c1163b2. The findings, most consequential first:

**1. The controlling insight for Morrow's appearance: his composure is the horror, not his wildness.** Every witness account turns on the same axis — the walk "steady. Not fast, not slow, never once stopping to look around"; "like he had all the time in the world"; and what Farrell finds is a *neat* camp, blankets arranged, the journal set out "as if left to be found," graves dug deliberately with stones "placed just so," and years of "careful, formal handwriting." Morrow is a soldier gone to rags — erect, unhurried, orderly — and the wildness lives only in the beard and the clothes. The eyes should read flat and still ("nothing in his eyes. Nothing at all"), never intense. This retroactively explains why the Monster Manual candidates felt wrong: the text refuses exactly that register. It's now the lead section of `morrow-reference.md`.

**2. Two visual-bible "gaps" weren't gaps.** The corpse wears a coat and shirt ("His coat was torn, his shirt beneath it shredded," 20:13), and his tracks are "the print of worn boots" (15:87). So coat and boots are canon; only their color/material remain reference choices. Also the horns: "curved and thickened" (16:17). Bible corrected.

**3. One real continuity seam, needs your call.** Farrell twice anchors his killer-inference to "around the time the letters stopped" (09:117, 13:67) — but the letters stopped in **March** 1858 (he's read the dated last letter) and Morrow's passage/the killings are **August**. Five months. It's partially defensible — 13:71 explicitly flags his motivated reasoning ("I made it fit, because the tracks said what they said"), and the March cutoff quietly implies the Hardins' world contracted months before they died — but a reader doing the arithmetic will likely score it as an error, not unreliability. Cheapest fix, if you want one: retie the inference to the trader's "last summer" date, which is factually tight. Options logged in `continuity.md`.

**4. Smaller finds:** the frozen-stranger in Ch 5 ("His face was calm... he had not fought it at the end") deliberately prefigures Morrow's corpse — a lovely unannounced rhyme with JE I's frozen man; both men's hauntings wear blue (Farrell's US dead, Morrow's Mexican dead — historically correct on both sides, accidental-but-true); the Loup Fork/Niobrara sighting geography I briefly worried about is already reconciled *in text* at 13:107; Morrow's age arithmetic just barely coheres (born early 1824, left at 32 → departure early 1856 — "lost count of the months" strains slightly); and the stale v0.5 header block in continuity.md (pre-v0.4 Hardin dates, the cut Ch 12) is now flagged as superseded with the corrected timeline.

**5. One gift to the catalog:** the book's central object was missing from my scene list — the found journal on the neatly arranged blankets in the empty hollow, footprints leading away (19:21–23). Pure evidence, no people, utterly DNI-safe. Added as `journal-found`.

The breed run was on its last few Morrow variants when I last checked; Farrell's five follow. Contact sheets when it lands — and note the round-2 fulls were bred with the pre-read identity block, so I'll cull against the composure standard as well as facial resemblance.

## ASSISTANT

The breed round is curated and committed (c0da085); the top-up is generating. Where things stand:

**Morrow: 12 of 18 kept, face rock-solid throughout.** The identity block held so well across angles, framings, and seasons that the LoRA may need less work than budgeted — every rejection was kit or staging drift, never the face. Standouts: the **blanket-hood bust** (s304 — your survivable-winter look, and it works beautifully), the **writing full-figure** (s402 — journal on knee, composed, a plate candidate in its own right), and the three anchor-seed cousins. The systematic failures were instructive: three variants went barefoot because I'd never put boots in the identity block (the full read made them canon the same day — now added), and summer prompts inflate the beard toward waist-length. Six replacement variants are generating now.

**Farrell: the contrast lands.** Five candidates at `imagegen/output/farrell-candidates/` (sheet included). Hatted, compact, cropped, short-bearded, armed, watchful — set beside any Morrow image, they read instantly as the two answers to the same war. The first bust even foregrounds his folded, scarred hands unprompted. My anchor picks: **s701 (bust) + s704 (full, with the bay horse)**, pending your notes.

Contact sheets: `output/morrow-breed/breed-contact-sheet.png` and `output/farrell-candidates/farrell-contact-sheet.png`.

Remaining before training: your pass over the breed keeps and Farrell picks, then the musubi-tuner env (gcc is already in the flake) and the caption pass, then the Morrow LoRA against Z-Image Base. One design note from curation: distant-figure plates need no identity block at all — the LoRA is only ever for faces at readable distance, which trims the training-set requirements.

Everything through the top-up is committed (3aba998). Summary of where the session stands:

**Letters seam — fixed per your direction.** Last letter is now dated May the twentieth with the garden in ("George says the worst is behind us" survives, and now reads against the first winter); Aldridge carries the marginal shift himself — "This past year they came a little slower, but they came. They stopped in May." — no regime change, just what a father would count; and both of Farrell's inferences now anchor to the trader's testimony, with the letters honestly convergent instead of five months adrift. A side benefit: Aldridge's inquiry runway tightens from a listless eight months to about three — last arrival midsummer, alarm by August, Farrell hired in November — which reads more like a worried father. Committed at 908a859, manuscript rebuilt (30,621 words).

**Morrow training set — 15 images, awaiting your pass.** Contact sheet at `imagegen/output/morrow-breed/training-set-contact-sheet.png`: seven busts (including the blanket-hood winter look and a profile), eight full figures (writing, fire with tin cup, blanket-wrapped, walking, following the herd, and the anchors). Same man throughout. The two generation lessons worth keeping: back-view and distant shots must be prompted *without* the identity block (face tokens overpower everything, and those shots never need the LoRA anyway), and summer prompts inflate the beard (both summer variants dropped — the set is winter-weighted, which matches where the book actually looks at him).

**Next, on your sign-off of the set (plus any swaps):** musubi-tuner training environment (gcc is already in the flake; bitsandbytes returns in a training-only project), captioning pass separating identity tokens from setting tokens, then the Morrow LoRA against Z-Image Base. Farrell's contrast anchors (my picks: s701 bust, s704 full) are also ready for your notes whenever — no training planned for him unless the contrast proves useful enough to want consistency.

## USER

On `nvitop`, I'm seeing sustained power draw around 440W / 480W, GPU MEM around 90%, GPU UTL 100%, and %GMBW around 60%. Is this roughly the best we can hope for on a task / system like the current one?

---

## ASSISTANT

Yes — for this recipe on this card, that profile is essentially the ceiling, and each number tells you why:

- **100% utilization at ~92% of the power cap** means the SMs are saturated with no pipeline bubbles. That's largely because both caching passes ran up front — training is pure DiT forward/backward with nothing waiting on the dataloader or the text encoder.
- **~60% memory bandwidth** alongside pegged compute is the signature of a *compute-bound* dense transformer in bf16 — the tensor cores are the bottleneck and the bus has headroom. Higher %GMBW here wouldn't mean better; it would mean something (like optimizer state paging) had gone wrong.
- **~90% VRAM** is the sized-to-fit result of training a 6B model on 24 GB: it's `batch_size 1` at 1024² *with gradient checkpointing*, which trades ~20–30% extra compute for fitting at all.

The levers that exist are all blocked or bad trades: dropping gradient checkpointing is the one real speedup and there's no VRAM for it; a bigger batch likewise; `--fp8_base` would free memory but changes training numerics on a run where fidelity is the whole point; and `sdpa` already hits the flash-attention kernels on Ada. Gradient accumulation changes step semantics, not throughput.

So the honest assessment: ~440 W sustained, compute-saturated, memory-fit — this is what a healthy single-GPU LoRA run on a 6B DiT looks like. And it's a ~2,400-step job; even a heroic 25% win would buy back twenty minutes on a run we'll do a handful of times. The place we bought real throughput was upstream: pre-caching both encoders so none of this hour is spent on anything but the DiT.

## ASSISTANT

**The Morrow LoRA is trained, validated, and deployed.** The full arc, for the record:

- **Training:** 16 epochs (~2,400 steps, 1h40m) on Z-Image Base, dim 32, the signed-off 15-image set. Checkpoints every 4 epochs.
- **Validation:** the checkpoint grid exposed a real finding — at multiplier 1.0 with a descriptor-free prompt, the bare trigger loses to the base model's priors (it drew a clean-shaven young gentleman and rendered "jmorrow" as plate caption text). At 2.0 with the verbatim training caption, the trained Morrow appeared perfectly — so learning was never in question, only dosage. The working recipe: **multiplier ~1.3 plus light descriptors** ("the tall thin bearded man" alongside the trigger).
- **The test that matters passed:** the wolf-kill probe — Morrow kneeling at a frozen carcass with his knife, straight from canon (08:27) but absent from the training set — renders the right man in an invented pose. Identity generalizes; it didn't memorize compositions.
- **Deployment:** both formats committed via LFS (`imagegen/loras/`), and the diffusers conversion verified end-to-end in the main catalog pipeline (peft added). Epoch 16 shows no overcooking; we're using final.

Two stumbles worth confessing: the first validation grid died because I launched it while training was still releasing VRAM, and the rerun died to a zsh quirk (it doesn't word-split unquoted variables, so the LoRA arguments went in as one mangled string) — both now structural fixes (`validate_grid.sh` is bash, with skip-if-exists resume).

**The pipeline is now complete end to end:** cited catalog → engraving register → character LoRA. What remains is production and polish: wire `--lora` into `generate.py` as a proper flag, run the Morrow scenes from the catalog (morrow-hollow, morrow-witnessed, the journal scenes), try the same-seed rhyme pairs (morrow-hollow ↔ reading-night now that both men render consistently), and start curating finals into `art/`. Say the word — or give notes on the validation images first (`imagegen/training/output/validate/checkpoint-grid.png`, plus the two `diag_p2` images that show the recipe working).

---

## USER

Is it worth re-running the grid at multiplier 1.3? I also notice that flat_diag_verbatim_x2.png somewhat uniquely doesn't have a yellowed background. Any idea what's going on there?

---

## ASSISTANT

Yes on both counts — and the two questions are connected.

**Re-running the grid at 1.3:** worth it. The original grid was supposed to be the checkpoint-selection instrument, but at multiplier 1.0 with descriptor-free prompts it never actually measured the checkpoints — every column failed the same way for the same reason. The only thing we've seen at the working recipe is the final checkpoint. Epoch 8 or 12 might deliver the same identity with less pressure on the register; the run is ~20 minutes.

**The white background:** `flat_diag_verbatim_x2` is unique in *two* ways, not one — it's the only image at multiplier 2.0, and (checking my command history) the only run where I **omitted the negative prompt** entirely. So there are two candidate mechanisms: the negative ("photograph, **color**") may actually be what nudges the base model toward the aged-print cream rendering in every other image, and/or 2× amplification collapses background context — a classic overcooked-LoRA artifact where the subject survives and the world dies (you can see the scene is nearly a void, not just uncolored). I'll disambiguate with two probes folded into the same run: verbatim@2.0 *with* the negative, and verbatim@1.3 *without*.

## ASSISTANT

Round 2 is complete and the recipe is now fully characterized (grid: `imagegen/training/output/validate2/checkpoint-grid-m13.png`):

**Your baseline question paid for itself.** The recreated baselines (descriptors, no LoRA) show that "tall thin bearded man" alone buys a groomed Victorian gentleman with `jmorrow` rendered as caption text — so the grid columns now cleanly isolate what the LoRA contributes: the *specific* man (ragged beard-length, center part, gauntness, the worn coat), plus the progressive absorption of the trigger token out of text-rendering entirely by the final epoch.

**The white-page mystery: an interaction, not a single cause.** 2.0-with-negative keeps the cream; 1.3-without-negative keeps the cream (but the plate border returns); only 2.0-without-negative bleached. So the negative prompt — "photograph, color" pushing toward the antique-print prior, "border" suppressing furniture — is genuinely load-bearing for the register, and high multipliers can overpower the paper tone without it. That's now documented in the reference as part of the recipe, not folklore.

**Production recipe, final form:** `final` checkpoint @1.3 for portrait plates (peak identity, zero text furniture); `ep12` @1.3 for narrative scenes (identity nearly equal, richest world — final mildly simplifies scenery around the figure). Both checkpoints deployed to `imagegen/loras/` in both formats, all committed (f1b96f2, 57555db).

Next up whenever you're ready: wire `--lora` into `generate.py` as a first-class flag, then the production runs — the Morrow catalog scenes, the same-seed rhyme pairs (morrow-hollow ↔ reading-night is now fully achievable), and first curations into `art/`.

## ASSISTANT

Three seeds are generating. The audit checklist for this plate, drawn from the text before I see the images (so the review is against spec, not vibes):

- **Must be present:** hollow in the bluffs sheltering him from wind (08:37, 12:113); a *small* fire (12:113 — he builds small fires, has little to burn); blanket around him (06:21); leather-bound journal, short pencil (19:19–23); winter, failing light.
- **Must be absent:** any horse (he has none), any gun (02:13), any tent or stove (the v0.4 rule — he survives in natural shelter only), any camp abundance — his entire kit is knife, cup, flint and steel, candle stub, bedroll.
- **Identity:** the trained Morrow, composure per the bearing note — writing is the one act where his "careful, formal" discipline shows.
- **Register:** cream ground, no plate furniture.

The absences are where the coherence engine bites: base-model priors love to furnish frontier camps with kettles, rifles, and tethered horses.

## ASSISTANT

Mechanically, yes — cleanly. Both our stacks support multi-adapter composition: in diffusers/peft it's `load_lora_weights` twice and `set_adapters(["morrow", "hollow"], adapter_weights=[1.3, 1.0])`; musubi's generator likewise takes multiple `--lora_weight` args. Each LoRA binds to its own trigger token, so the prompt grammar is exactly what you'd want: "`wbhollow`, a deep hollow... `jmorrow`, the tall thin bearded man seated within it." Two caveats from how the composition actually works (additive in weight-space, not syntactic): stacked LoRAs trained in the same engraving register will double-dose the style, so weights come down when combined (~1.0–1.2 + 0.8), and concept bleed is real — the environment LoRA's training captions must exclude figures, or its ghost-inhabitants will fight Morrow for the camp.

The interesting problem is upstream: **training data for the environment has a bootstrap catch** — we'd be training on images of the very thing the model fails to generate. Two exits:

1. **Figure-free self-distillation.** The failure mode we observed is figure-*dominance*: the man wins the composition and the geometry evaporates. The model may render hollows/cutbanks fine when no figure competes. If so, we generate-and-curate empty hollows, train the env LoRA on those, then compose Morrow in.
2. **Public-domain period engravings.** The Nebraska dugout/cutbank shelter is a genuinely well-illustrated 19th-century genre — real steel engravings exist. Curation would need to strip domestic features (doors, stovepipes) that contradict Morrow's wild camps.

Let me check whether the geometry-first prompt already fixed it — and test the figure-free hypothesis, which decides whether we need the env LoRA at all:

There's the answer to whether we need it: **the geometry-first prompt fixed it without any environment LoRA.** Seed 45 gives the hollow as architecture — earthen walls arching over him, snow-capped, Morrow seated inside wrapped in the blanket, writing, small fire at the mouth, every absence still holding. The failure was prompt *attention order*, not model capability: lead with the architecture and the figure takes up residence inside it; lead with the figure and the world becomes backdrop.

If anything it's now a shade cave-like (root-hung, very enclosed) versus "hollow in the bluffs" — though it rhymes strikingly with Morrow's own JE IV image of waking buried with "my breath making a small cave in the white" (08:5), and the final camp is canonically "six feet deep" (19:5), so it's within license. Seed 46 is still rendering as the variance check.

So the environment-LoRA answer, revised: compositionally available (multi-adapter with separate triggers, weights dialed down when stacked, figure-free training captions to prevent bleed) — but **kept in the back pocket** rather than built now. The cheaper tool, prompt-ordering, just proved out. I'll log the rule in findings — it generalizes to every architecture-bearing plate (trading-post, homestead, reading-night) — and reserve the env LoRA for the day a recurring location needs consistency *across* plates the way a face does, which is a real future case (the homestead appears in at least three scenes). Verdict on the plate itself once s46 lands: s45 is the current pick for `art/`.

<result>Reconnaissance complete. Report follows.

# Public-Domain 19th-C. Engraving Source Recon

## Subject 1: Prairie sod houses / dugouts / homesteads (1850s–1880s)

**Headline finding: period *engravings* of soddies are genuinely scarce.** The visual record of sod construction is dominated by photography (Solomon Butcher's ~1,000+ Nebraska homestead photos, 1886–1900s). True wood engravings exist but must be mined page-by-page from illustrated weeklies and travel books — they are not pre-aggregated anywhere.

**Best repositories:**

1. **Internet Archive — illustrated weeklies &amp; travel books** (best engraving source)
   - 8,913 items match "Harper's Weekly" in texts; Frank Leslie's runs also present. Engravings of dugouts/claim shanties appear in western-emigration features, but require page-level mining.
   - Standout: Albert D. Richardson, *Beyond the Mississippi* (1867), 200+ wood engravings of frontier dwellings — 10 copies, e.g. https://archive.org/details/cu31924030995942
   - Also: illustrated county histories and Andreas state atlases (1870s–80s Kansas/Nebraska) with farmstead lithograph views.
   - Rights: pre-1930 US publication, public domain. Scans: 300–600 dpi page images, typically 1,500–4,000 px wide — plates crop out above 1024 px fine.

2. **Library of Congress PPOC** — https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=sod%20house
   - ~139 hits for "sod house" but nearly all photographs/HABS drawings, not engravings. Useful adjacent hit: *The Grand Duke Alexis on the plains* (1872), 10 wood engravings. LOC serves uncompressed TIFFs (3,000–7,000 px). Rights: "no known restrictions" on these.

3. **Wikimedia Commons** — https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:Sod_houses_of_the_United_States
   - ~47 files + 10 subcategories, again mostly photographs (Butcher/NARA mirrors). Fulltext search suggests a few hundred candidate files but with heavy false-positive rate. Full-res originals, PD-marked.

4. **NYPL Digital Collections** — https://digitalcollections.nypl.org/search/index?keywords=sod+house
   - 24 results; PD items get free hi-res TIFF downloads. Small but clean.

**Volume estimate:** genuine period engravings/lithographs of sod dwellings: **dozens (roughly 20–60)** after mining. Photographs (if acceptable): thousands via Butcher/LOC/History Nebraska.

## Subject 2: American bison

**Much richer.** Natural-history plates cover life stages explicitly; popular lithography covers herds and hunts.

**Best repositories:**

1. **Internet Archive / BHL — natural-history plate books** (best for life stages)
   - Audubon &amp; Bachman, *Viviparous Quadrupeds of North America* — Plate LVI (bull) and LVII (**cow + calf**), hand-colored lithographs; 29 copies on IA, e.g. https://archive.org/details/b22014445_0001
   - Hornaday, *The Extermination of the American Bison* (1889), ~22 plates incl. calf, yearling, cow, bull, winter/snow scenes: https://archive.org/details/exterminationofa00horn ; BHL edition https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/bibliography/78803 (BHL blocks scripted fetch but serves full-res JP2/OCR via its Flickr and download UI; typically 1,500–3,000 px)
   - J.A. Allen, *The American Bisons, Living and Extinct* (1876), also on BHL's American-bison subject page: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/subject/American+bison

2. **Library of Congress PPOC — Popular Graphic Arts + general** — https://www.loc.gov/pictures/
   - 37 hits "buffalo hunt" (lithographs incl. Currier &amp; Ives *The Buffalo Hunt: "Surrounding the Herd"*, 1844 Catlin lithographs *Buffalo hunt, chasing back*); PGA has Bodmer's *Herd of Bisons on the Upper Missouri* aquatint and *Buffaloes at Rest* (after W.J. Hays). TIFFs 3,000–7,000 px, "no known restrictions."

3. **Catlin, *North American Indian Portfolio* (1844)** — 3 full scanned copies on IA (e.g. https://archive.org/details/Ayer_250_45_C2_1844_Catlin), ~25 plates, 6–8 buffalo-hunt subjects incl. winter/snowshoe hunt scenes.

4. **Wikimedia Commons** — Category:Bison in art (69 files, 13 subcategories incl. Paintings by George Catlin); "bison engraving" fulltext ≈ 750 files (noisy). Mirrors of LOC/Smithsonian at full resolution, PD-marked.

5. **NYPL Digital Collections** — 47 "buffalo hunt" results, PD filter + hi-res TIFF downloads.

**Standout single items:**
- Audubon Plate LVII, American Bison cow and calf (hand-colored litho) — via any IA *Quadrupeds* copy above
- Hornaday 1889 plate suite (calf through old bull, plus slaughter/winter scenes) — https://archive.org/details/exterminationofa00horn
- Bodmer, *Herd of Bisons on the Upper Missouri* — LOC PGA, via https://www.loc.gov/pictures/search/?q=bison
- Catlin, *Buffalo hunt, chasing back* (1844 litho, LOC + portfolio scans)

**Volume estimate:** **100–300 distinct usable period prints** (before augmentation), most ≥1024 px after plate cropping.

**Rights, both subjects:** everything above is pre-1930 US publication = public domain. LOC ("no known restrictions"), IA, BHL, and Commons impose no reuse claims; NYPL explicitly flags PD items. No repository-specific obstacles found.

**Level-of-effort estimate:** A deeper acquisition pass is roughly a 6–12 hour job, asymmetric between subjects. Bison is the easy half: ~4–6 hours of scripted pulls (IA fulltext bulk download + LOC TIFF endpoints + Commons category dumps) plus manual dedupe of hand-colored variants should yield **150–250 clean ≥1024 px bison training images** with calf/cow/bull and winter coverage. Sod houses are the hard half: engravings must be found by paging through Harper's/Leslie's volumes, *Beyond the Mississippi*, and county histories on IA — expect ~4–8 hours of eyeball mining for only **30–80 genuine engravings**; if the LoRA style target can tolerate mixing in period photographs re-rendered or used as secondary conditioning, the Butcher collection lifts subject-1 yield to 500+ images for another 2 hours of work, and that tradeoff is the main decision to make before committing to the pass.

Sources: [LOC PPOC](https://www.loc.gov/pictures/), [Wikimedia Commons sod houses](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:Sod_houses_of_the_United_States), [BHL American bison subject](https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/subject/American+bison), [Hornaday on BHL](https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/bibliography/78803), [NYPL Digital Collections](https://digitalcollections.nypl.org/), [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/), [Smithsonian "Picturing the American Buffalo"](https://americanart.si.edu/exhibitions/buffalo)</result>

## ASSISTANT

First, closing out the rhyme measurement, because it informs the decision: reading-night(s45) vs morrow-hollow scores **+0.578** against +0.429/+0.533 for the off-seed alternates — a real but modest premium, and the same-scene control (+0.724) shows content already dominates. Lesson: shared-seed rhyme buys little between *sibling* scenes; it's strongest between **dissimilar** scenes, where the shared bones surprise (your furrows-into-bodies example was landscape↔battlefield). The strong candidates were always first-sighting↔finale and stake↔graves.

Now the real question. My thoughts, and they run *with* your lean but land on a sharper line:

**Split the two axes.** "Which text gets plates" and "does Farrell's figure appear" are separable — and most of what the Farrell chapters offer the coherence engine never needed his figure at all. The homestead, the graves, the trading post, Fort Kearny, the found journal — those are **evidence plates**, figureless by construction, and they're where Farrell's half is densest in auditable physical detail. We'd be giving up almost nothing by keeping them.

So the rule I'd propose: **journal entries may show Morrow (LoRA'd); Farrell chapters contribute only evidence and places; Farrell himself is never depicted.** That gets you:

- No Farrell LoRA, no generic-guy dissonance — your instinct, honored by omission rather than by training cost.
- Full-text coherence coverage — the engine still audits both halves.
- A thematic alignment that I think is actually load-bearing: **Farrell is the seer, not the seen.** The reader rides behind his eyes for half the book; the plates then show exactly what witness shows — Morrow's world as the journal describes it, and the evidence as Farrell finds it. He stays outside the frame the way a narrator stays outside his own field of vision. Morrow is depicted because Morrow is the *object* of the book's whole apparatus of witness — trapper testimony, Creighton's account, the journal, the corpse.
- The rhyme survives, upgraded: morrow-hollow's true partner becomes **journal-found** — the journal being written / the journal as found object, the same camp with the man subtracted. That's arguably a better pair than writing/reading, and it's already in the catalog, figureless and DNI-clean.

Cost: reading-night as composed dies (it needs the reader at the fire). It could be recomposed from outside the camp — fire-glow in the hollow, horse at the pine, the reader implied — but I'd sooner shelve it; the implied-reader version flirts with cleverness.

If you ever want Farrell anyway (a frontispiece pairing, say), the machinery is proven and cheap now — an afternoon: breed from s701/s702, caption the before/after as states of one identity, same pipeline. Nothing about this rule burns that bridge. Want me to codify journal-plates-plus-evidence as the catalog policy and proceed on that basis?

## ASSISTANT

The stake it is — and since the beat is barely a day old in the manuscript, I'll treat this as its first real scrutiny pass. Reading the entry closely (08:59–63) before touching the prompt, three things surface:

1. **"It had been there since before the snow, by the way it stood"** — first snow was Nov 14; the riders watched Jan 3. So the stake *predates the watchers by two months* — they came to where it already stood. That's the v0.8 design (attended all along), and it constrains the image: the stake must read settled, weathered, snow packed undisturbed at its base — never fresh.
2. **"It faced the valley"** — a peeled stake has no face; this is Morrow's attributing of intention, which is right for him but unrenderable as stated. Visually it becomes position and wind: the stake on the ridge brow above the wintering valley, the cloth streaming.
3. **The red problem.** The cloth is red in a monochrome register — and it's one of the only color notes in the book's entire visual world (red cloth, blood on snow: the same thread). A spot-color accent would be striking and is period-plausible (hand-tinting existed) — but it would *announce* the connection, which is exactly the kind of move the book refuses. I'm rendering it monochrome-dark; the redness lives in the text. Flagging the spot-color option for you to veto into existence if you ever want it.

## ASSISTANT

You're right, and the constraint is productive rather than limiting — 19th-century book practice gives us a hierarchy that maps *exactly* onto the plate policy we just adopted:

- **Full-page plates are portrait** (tipped-in engravings, ~2:3 — our 832×1216 is already a 6×9 page's proportions). These become the journal-side images: Morrow, the hollow, the stake, the buffalo.
- **Landscape images in period books mostly weren't plates at all** — they were in-text wood-engraved vignettes, horizontal cuts embedded in the letterpress (the *Beyond the Mississippi* mode). That's a natural home for the Farrell-side evidence family: graves, homestead, trading post, fort — smaller, set into his narration like exhibits. The format hierarchy would quietly enact the policy: the journal's world gets plates; the investigation gets cuts.
- **Fold-outs were for panoramas.** If we ever spend the single exception, the scene shaped for it is `village-passing` — the one image whose subject is *width itself*, the line of a nation moving along a ridge. Flagged as the candidate; that's a production decision for much later.

Immediate consequence: the two rhyme bookends (`first-sighting`/`finale-fifty-yards`) should be **portrait plates**, not landscape — and portrait genuinely suits them both: the calf small and low in a tall sky-heavy frame, the cow facing the viewer filling the vertical. The run I just launched is landscape; I'll let it finish as a composition study, then recompose portrait. Codifying the format doctrine now:

The scale reads right, the herd is a distant thread. Faint corner marks survive the hardened negatives (they're croppable — that residue seems to be the register's floor).
2. **s66** — the strongest rival. The cleanest *poise* of the field, classic plate composition, fully white face, calm sky. What it lacks: breath and tracks — it's more emblem, less animal.
3. **s65** — the most *alive*: walking toward the viewer, breath visible, tracks behind. The directness is arresting but reads slightly confrontational for a cover, and the face runs woolly.
4. **s72** — airy and correct, white head, herd on the ridge; less presence than the top three.

Culls with reasons: s60/s70/s71 (the dark-face drift — whiteness eroding where the genre prior is darkest), s61/s68/s71 again (heavy menace skies), s67 (landscape-souled, she's too small for a cover), s69 (prize-livestock portrait energy), s64 (weakest presence of the round-2 set).

One observation across all twelve: the neighborhood is *consistent* now — whole-body white holds nearly everywhere, the herd stays dark, the sand hills stay flat. The diversity that remains is stance, sky, and living detail, which is taste rather than correctness. s62 vs s66 is essentially "creature vs emblem" — the tracks-and-breath animal that came from somewhere, or the perfectly composed plate. Given the book's ethic, I lean creature; but I'd genuinely welcome your eye here before it goes to `art/`.

## ASSISTANT

That's a sharp catch, and it's bigger than the cover — it's a register rule we've been missing. The engraving style constrains *surface*, but the vantage constrains *witness*: an 1859 illustrator drew a wild buffalo from a sketchable distance — eye-level, profile or three-quarter, the whole animal in the picture plane. The looming foreshortened closeness of s71/s65 implies optics (and survivable proximity) that don't exist yet; it's a camera's intimacy wearing an engraving's clothes. This joins the diegetic discipline: not just *what* is shown (evidence, not events) but *how seeing worked* — no telephoto compression, no wide-angle looming, no impossible closeness. Codifying and iterating with vantage language:

## ASSISTANT

Six vantage-corrected seeds are rendering. One observation while they do: checking the five plates already in `art/` against the new rule, **all conform** — and not by luck. Each was composed from a witness position the text itself supplies (Morrow's rise in first-sighting, Farrell kneeling at the camp, the ridge where the herd crossed). The cover is the one plate with *no* textual vantage — it's an emblem, nobody in the book stands where the cover's viewer stands — which is exactly where the modern eye leaked in first. The corollary is worth keeping: **plates anchored to a canonical witness inherit period vantage for free; unanchored plates need the rule enforced by hand.** The finale, composed from behind Farrell's eyes at a canonical fifty yards, should inherit it automatically.

The finale was the hardest tribunal yet, fittingly: three canon violations caught and fixed (spot-red blood, Farrell summoned into frame by my own vantage phrase, a sun the text explicitly hides), then a genuine impasse — the rhyme seed structurally refusing canonical blood placement — resolved by the session's last lesson: **rhyme pairs are picked jointly, never sequentially.** First-sighting swapped to its aloneness alternate to make the pair work; if that trade sits wrong when you look, the revert is one `cp` and both old candidates are preserved in `output/`.

Where things stand: six of the catalog's plates done; remaining are the Farrell-side landscape scenes now designated in-text vignettes (homestead, graves, trading-post, fort-kearny, morrow-witnessed, village-passing — a different production question with its own size/placement decisions), the three policy-shelved scenes, and the eventual assembly questions (plate placement against entries, cropping pass for corner marks, the fold-out). The engine is proven well past "one at a time" now — but the vignette family is a natural place to keep that discipline, since the format itself is new.

## ASSISTANT

Good eye again — and this one I can answer directly, because the Niobrara valley's flora is well documented (it's a famous biogeographic crossroads), and the answer indicts the image on two counts at once:

**What's actually there:** creek and river margins carry **sandbar willow** — shrubby, thicket-forming, head-high brush, not trees — and **peachleaf willow**, a small *upright* tree. The big watercourse tree is the **plains cottonwood** (canon already uses it — Morrow's gold cottonwoods, Farrell stripping bark for the horse). Draws get boxelder and green ash; the north-facing slopes carry ponderosa pine — which the text nails at 11:7, and which is a real Niobrara oddity; the uplands are treeless. The text's own language is right too: "willows along its banks, **brown and thick**" (11:3) reads as thicket, exactly what sandbar willow is.

**What the model drew:** *weeping* willows — long pendulous streamers. Weeping willow is an Asian ornamental, planted in Eastern churchyards, absent from an 1858 Niobrara creek. And the reason the model reached for it is the damning part: **the weeping willow is the classic mourning emblem** — it's on half the gravestones and mourning samplers of the century. Given "graves + willows," the engraving prior supplied cemetery iconography. So it's a botanical error *and* an announced-significance error — the tree weeping over the graves is exactly the kind of significance the book refuses to state.

The fix rides with the pending house fix: "low brushy willow thickets lining the frozen creek" in the positive, "weeping willow" in the negative — both held until the construction research reports, since house, cairn stones, and now vegetation all go into the same re-roll. Logging the finding now:
