# Feasibility report: two-person homestead, upper Niobrara, spring 1857 – Aug 1858

## Verdicts by element

**SOD HOUSE — Realistic, with adjustments.**
- **Size:** 14×16 ft interior is the documented norm (Oblinger's 1873 house was exactly this); 12×14 also common. Walls 2–2.5 ft thick, ~7–7.5 ft high, sod blocks cut ~12" wide × 4" thick × 2–3 ft long with a breaking/"grasshopper" plow; roughly an acre of sod builds one house.
- **Time:** the oft-cited "a week" assumes neighbors helping raise walls. Your couple has none — for two people (one of them an Eastern wife new to heavy labor), **2–3 weeks of wall-and-roof work** is the honest figure, plus plowing the sod first. NSHS notes wall-laying "required at least two men," so a husband-and-wife build is possible but slow.
- **Roof:** low gable (most common) or shed roof; cedar ridgepole — often with a forked post under each end inside the walls — willow/plum/chokecherry brush over rafters, then prairie hay, then thin sod grass-side up. Flat roofs existed but leaked worst; slight pitch was standard. **Key local twist:** the Niobrara valley has cedar in the canyons and pine on north slopes, so your settlers have good ridgepoles and rafters at hand — the desperate-treelessness trope doesn't apply here.
- **Stovepipe, not chimney.** Period-correct is a stovepipe through the roof, or at most a chimney of sod blocks or mud-plastered sticks. No brick, no dressed-stone masonry.
- **Windows:** glass was "hard-to-come-by"; frames set into the wall as it rose, with a slip-gap of rags/grass above the sash so settling walls didn't crush the panes (a lovely authentic detail). In 1857 on the upper Niobrara the nearest glass is a wagon-haul from Missouri River supply points (Fort Randall est. 1856; Niobrara/Rockport townsites with saw/shingle mills 1856–57, all far downriver) or Fort Laramie / the Bordeaux trading post to the west. **One small 4- or 6-pane sash carried carefully from the river, or oiled paper/hide, is right; a 12-pane double-hung is a later-period anachronism.**
- **At 16 months old:** walls rain-channeled and slumping at the corners, grass and sunflowers sprouting from the roof sod, roof sagged along the ridgepole, muslin ceiling cloth tacked up to catch dirt and mice, floor packed earth, everything drips for two days after a hard rain.

**LEAN-TO BARN/STABLE — Realistic if small.** A three-sided sod-and-pole stable (~12×14, or built against a cutbank), roofed with poles/brush/hay, holding one team and maybe a cow, is a standard first-winter structure; Oblinger literally converted his first soddy to a stable. A framed or large barn: **not plausible** (no sawmill within reach).

**HAND-DUG WELL WITH WINDLASS — Realistic near the creek; possibly unnecessary.** In valley alluvium near a creek, water table is shallow — settlers "dug until they found water and lined the hole"; expect **10–25 ft**, days-to-two-weeks of digging for one man below and one hauling spoil. The real problem in that country is **caving sand** — the well needs curbing (willow cribbing or a board curb, boards being precious). Windlass over a plank platform is the correct rig; **no windmill — windmills come to Nebraska wells in the 1870s+.** Note many creek-side settlers just used the creek or a seep spring the first season and dug the well in year two — a good sequencing detail.

**ROOT CELLAR IN HILLSIDE — Fully realistic.** Days of pick-and-shovel work in soft Rosebud silt/sandy loam; brush-and-sod or pole roof; often dug even before the house (some families wintered in the dugout). One of the easiest claims in your list.

**FIELD — Realistic only if modest.** Heavy tallgrass breaking took 3+ yoke of oxen on a 24" plow (2 acres/day with two men); a single team on a small 12–14" breaking plow manages **½–1 acre/day**, and breaking season is roughly May 1–July 1. First season (1857): **5–15 acres**, planted to "sod corn," squash, pumpkins — sod crops only, since fresh-turned sod grew little else. Second spring (1858): the 1857 breaking is now workable ground for a real corn/wheat crop, plus perhaps another 10 acres broken. **By August 1858: ~15–25 acres total broken, one real crop nearly ready.** 40+ acres would strain credulity. Sandhills caveat: valley hay-flat sod breaks easier than eastern tallgrass, but sandy uplands blow when broken — they'd plow the sub-irrigated valley floor only.

**GARDEN — Realistic.** Quarter- to half-acre spaded/plowed near the house, fenced with poles or brush (no barbed wire until 1874).

**STONE CAIRNS ON GRAVES — Realistic, with effort — and geologically specific.** The Sandhills proper are essentially stone-free (eolian sand; **no glacial erratics** — Pleistocene ice never reached there, erratics belong to easternmost Nebraska). But the Niobrara valley walls are the exception: the **Rosebud Formation** (pinkish-tan siltstone — soft, cuttable, but crumbly and a poor cairn stone) forms the cliffs and riverbed, and the **basal Valentine Formation carries hard, gray, opaline silica-cemented sandstone ("quartzite")** — dense, weather-resistant, lying as slabby ledges and broken blocks along the river and creek mouths; it's hard enough that it was later quarried for riprap. Farther upriver, **limy concretions and caprock ledges of the Ogallala/Ash Hollow beds** offer similar gatherable slabs. So: a settler **could** gather enough flat hard stone for two low cairns, but only by working the creek/river breaks and hauling it by wagon or stone-boat — the stones would not be lying around the homesite. That labor (a day or two of gathering and hauling per grave) is itself a strong scene. **Chalk is wrong for this setting** — Niobrara Formation chalk outcrops far downstream near the Missouri confluence, not on the upper river.

## What they could NOT plausibly have

Frame or shingled anything (no sawmill within ~150 miles); brick or masonry chimney; board floors; large barn; deep cased well; windmill; barbed wire; more than ~25 acres broken; a second full harvest (only the 1858 crop matures, and they leave in August — possibly before it's fully in).

## Setting-specific corrections to the standard soddy picture

1. **Timber is available here.** Cedar canyons and pine slopes mean good ridgepoles, rafters, door frames, fuel — and honestly make a log or log/sod hybrid cabin equally or more plausible than a pure soddy. If you keep the soddy, motivate it (speed, the valley timber being across the river, green logs, etc.).
2. **1857–58 is ahead of the settlement frontier — by decades.** Actual Anglo settlement of the upper Niobrara came in the late 1870s–80s. In 1857 the land is unsurveyed (surveys just beginning), there is no Homestead Act (1862), so they're squatters/preemption claimants — and the country is effectively unceded Lakota range under the 1851 Fort Laramie framework. Total isolation (no neighbors for barn-raisings, no town) is both your dramatic asset and the reason every build estimate above should run long.
3. **Sod itself differs:** Sandhills grass sod (needle-and-thread, prairie sandreed on uplands) is weaker-rooted than eastern big-bluestem sod; the good tough building sod is the dense wet-meadow sod of the valley floor — which is also their hay and their plow ground.

## Sources

- [NSHS Educational Leaflet No. 3: The Sod House](https://usgennet.org/usa/ne/topic/resources/NSHS/EDLFT/edlft03.html) (also [History Nebraska sod-house construction collection](https://history.nebraska.gov/collection_section/sod-house-construction-instructions-rg2034-am/))
- [NebraskaStudies.org — Building a Sod House](https://www.nebraskastudies.org/en/1850-1874/the-challenges-of-the-plains/building-a-sod-house/) and [Searching for Water: Wells and Windmills](https://nebraskastudies.org/en/1850-1874/the-challenges-of-the-plains/searching-for-water-wells-windmills/)
- [American Heritage, "That Zenith of Prairie Architecture — the Soddy" (Aug. 1973)](https://www.americanheritage.com/zenith-prairie-architecture-soddy)
- [NPS — Geologic Formations, Niobrara National Scenic River](https://www.nps.gov/niob/learn/nature/geologicformations.htm) (Rosebud siltstone, Valentine Fm. opaline sandstone)
- [USGS, Geology and Ground-Water Resources of the Upper Niobrara River Basin (Bradley &amp; Rainwater)](https://digitalcommons.unl.edu/usgspubs/100/)
- [Oblinger family letters, Prairie Settlement collection](https://history.nebraska.gov/publications_section/oblinger-letters/); [sod-breaking rates](https://www.itakehistory.com/post/sod-breaking); [1850s territorial context](https://history.nebraska.gov/archeology-of-nebraskas-territorial-period-1854-1867/)</result>

**The text survives its most material-culture-heavy audit yet, almost eerily well:** the lean-to "barely large enough for a horse and a cow" (11:53) is *exactly* the three-sided sod-and-pole stable the scholarship says two people could build; the well's "wooden frame... pulley and rope" is the correct windlass rig (no windmill — those are 1870s); the single small window matches glass-scarcity reality; the root cellar is the easiest build on the claim; the stovepipe-through-sod-roof is period-right; and v0.7's "homesteader"→"settlers" fix turns out doubly correct since there's no Homestead Act until 1862 — the Hardins are squatters decades ahead of the settlement frontier on unceded Lakota range, which *is the book's premise*: why they're alone, why no one finds them, why they're "in the way."

**The cairns get a geological blessing with a story in it:** the sand hills are stone-free (no erratics — the ice never reached there), but the Niobrara breaks expose hard opaline Valentine-formation sandstone in slabby ledges. So the flat gray slabs in s91 are *right* — and they mean Morrow hauled stone from the creek breaks, a day or two of labor per grave. That's what "placed just so" cost. The care Farrell can't square with the violence just got heavier.

## 1. FORT KEARNY, December 1858

**Bottom line: your "unstockaded" belief is confirmed.** The earliest surviving photograph of the post — taken June 1858 by Samuel C. Mills, photographer of the Simpson expedition — shows "a collection of adobe buildings without any wall or fortifications." No stockade existed until a small one was built on an earth embankment during the 1864–65 Indian War. Defense in the 1850s was artillery, not walls: a traveler counted "16 blockhouse guns, two field pieces, two mountain howitzers, and one prairie piece" positioned *among the parade-ground trees*.

**Buildings by 1858** (a detailed 1857 traveler account, via Legends of America): a mix of frame and earthen construction —
- ~**Five unpainted wooden (frame) buildings** and **about two dozen long, low "mud" buildings** (sod/adobe), the mud buildings extending outward along the roads off the square
- **Commanding officer's house**: two-story, unpainted frame, west side of the parade ground, "commodious, comfortable, well-finished"
- **Soldiers' barracks**: 70×30 ft, two-story frame, never fully finished
- Frame officers' quarters, hospital, and **sutler's store** (frame)
- Largest adobe/sod structure ~140×40 ft, 12 ft high (storehouse/stable scale) — good visual anchor for the "long low mud building" look

**Layout**: a square around a **four-acre parade ground**, flagstaff at center, laid out by Lt. Daniel Woodbury when the post was built in 1848.

**The cottonwoods**: planted around the parade-ground perimeter from 1848 — so by December 1858 they'd had about ten growing seasons. The 1857 account calls them essentially **the only trees visible in any direction** except stragglers on the Platte banks miles away. Render them as young but real trees (plausibly 15–25 ft), not the giants of later photos — the 1858 Mills photo is your ground truth and shows a low, horizontal post with modest trees.

**Winter approach from the east**: the traveler on the south-bank road would see, across dead-grass bottomland, first the flagstaff and the thin line of planted cottonwoods (bare in December), then the low brown scatter of sod/adobe roofs with a few taller unpainted two-story frame blocks — an open village, nothing military-looking in outline, smoke from many chimneys.

**Period imagery worth pulling**: (a) the **Samuel C. Mills 1858 photograph** — exactly your date, the single best reference; (b) **Frederick Piercy's 1853 sketch** (from *Route from Liverpool to Great Salt Lake Valley*, pub. 1855); (c) the Nebraska State Historical Society's "Fort Kearny, 1860" material.

Sources: [Wikipedia: Fort Kearny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Kearny) · [Legends of America: Fort Kearny](https://www.legendsofamerica.com/ne-fortkearny/) · [Samuel C. Mills (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_C._Mills) · [NSHS: Fort Kearny 1860](https://history.nebraska.gov/publications_section/fort-kearny-1860/) (403s to fetch, but exists as a lead)

## 2. SMALL TRADING POST, Niobrara/White River country

**Best documented analog — almost exactly your building**: the **Bordeaux Trading Post** on Bordeaux Creek near Chadron (now Museum of the Fur Trade), run by James Bordeaux (married into the Brulé; kin to Swift Bear's band) from the late 1840s through the 1870s, serving precisely the Lakota trade you describe. Reconstructed 1956 on the original foundation stones: **hand-hewn native timber walls**, small one-to-two-room scale on a 1.5-acre creekside site, original hearthstones and threshold stones still in place; eyewitness accounts guided the **shelves, counters, window locations, and furnishings** of the reconstruction. Trade: buffalo robes, furs, and ponies in; beads, blankets, guns, powder, knives, kettles, cloth, and whiskey out.

**Verdict on your details:**
- **Single room ~20×15 ft** — plausible; matches the scale of documented one-man wintering houses and Bordeaux's post.
- **Plank counter** — correct; counters and shelves are attested at Bordeaux.
- **Log-and-turf roof sagging in the middle** — correct and characteristic: pole-and-brush roof under a foot of earth/sod is heavy and sagged along the ridge within a few seasons. Grass often grew on the roof; in deep winter, a snow-covered dirt roof reads as a low white hummock with a dark smoking pipe or chimney.
- **Sod walls** — *defensible but flag it*: in the Niobrara/White River country, pine and cedar were available from the buttes and canyons, and the documented posts there (Bordeaux, the Fort Laramie–area houses) were **log or cedar-picket with earthen roofs**, not sod-walled. Pure sod-wall construction is more a treeless-Platte-valley / later-settler idiom (though not anachronistic — Fort Kearny had sod buildings from 1848). Safest render: **low cottonwood-log walls chinked with mud, or log front with sod banked heavily up the sides and rear** — visually "a sod house" from any distance, historically bulletproof.
- **Cast-iron stove + stovepipe** — *possible but flag it*: heavy castings were freighted to the country (via Fort Pierre and Fort Laramie supply routes), but a one-man post more typically had a **mud-and-stick fireplace/chimney at the gable end** (Bordeaux had a stone-hearth fireplace) or a light **sheet-iron stove**. If you keep the stove, the exterior tell is a **stovepipe elbowing through the roof, guyed, smoke-blackened snow around it** instead of a mud chimney.
- **Two candles** — fine (tallow candles were standard trade stock); a grease lamp would be equally right.
- **Lean-to stable** — entirely typical: pole lean-to against one wall, roofed with brush and hay, banked with manure/snow in winter; stock was corn-fed or driven to cottonwood bark in hard weather.
- **Winter exterior**: hides and firewood stacked against walls, a robe press or scaffold, packed dung-and-straw around the stable, the creek junction giving it its reason to exist (wood, water, and a wintering ground where Lakota bands camped).

Sources: [Museum of the Fur Trade — Bordeaux Trading Post](https://www.furtrade.org/about-us/bordeaux-trading-post) · [Wikipedia: Bordeaux Trading Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bordeaux_Trading_Post) · [Larpenteur, *Forty Years a Fur Trader*](https://user.xmission.com/~drudy/mtman/html/larpenteur/index.html) (the standard primary source on one-man wintering houses of exactly this period — worth a read for texture)

---

## 3. BRULÉ VILLAGE ON THE MOVE, autumn 1857, seen from two miles

**The single most important correction: a moving Lakota village did not travel single-file.** George Bird Grinnell's informants (quoted in Henderson's travois replication study) describe the trail of a band on the move as **"a quarter to half a mile wide"** where terrain allowed — a broad, loose swarm of parallel streams, not a thin line. At two miles, render a **wide, slow-drifting dark band** on the prairie, denser at the center, ragged at the edges, not a caravan string.

**Column composition** (Catlin, who watched a Sioux village of hundreds of lodges move in 1832): mounted men "strolling along in front or on the flank"; the mass of the column is the **baggage train** — women (Lakota women rode, by the 1850s, with children perched on loads or travois), packhorses, horse travois, **dog travois** low to the ground weaving among legs, elders, and children. The **loose horse herd** — for a horse-rich Brulé band, figure 5–15 head per lodge, i.e., several hundred animals for 30–60 lodges — is driven by boys, typically **off to a flank or trailing**, and at two miles it reads as a second, denser, faster-shifting mass distinct from the burdened column.

**Scale and pace**: 30–60 lodges ≈ 250–500 people. Parkman (1846) watched a village of ~150 lodges pour past "for a full half-hour." For your band: a procession **on the order of half a mile to a mile deep** and up to a quarter-mile-plus wide. Pace: Henderson's field replication clocked horse-travois travel at **3.3–3.8 km/h — strictly walking pace** (his key finding: horses moved camp no faster than dogs had, just with bigger loads). Day's move: ~10–15 miles. So the two-mile view is of something that barely seems to move.

**Dust**: an autumn move on dry shortgrass with hundreds of hooves raises a **low, thin haze hanging at leg height**, heaviest over the horse herd; note that dragging travois poles themselves cut almost no track (Henderson could never make a lasting trace) — so dust from hooves, not from the poles. After rain, none. A faint haze is right for a dry October; a big billowing cloud is wrong.

**The distinguishing silhouette features** (what says "Lakota village moving" and not "riders"):
1. **The travois X/V**: tipi poles lashed in two bundles, one on each side of the horse, tips crossing **above the withers in a shallow X**, butts dragging behind at roughly **30–40° to the ground** — every burdened horse becomes a long low triangle twice its own body length. This angle is the signature; get it on dozens of animals and the image identifies itself.
2. **Load platforms** slung between the pole bundles behind the horse — lumpy rectangles riding low, sometimes with a child or puppy silhouette on top.
3. **Lodge-cover bundles** — big soft packs on the packhorses, breaking the clean horse outline.
4. **Dog travois** — miniature versions of the same triangle, ankle-height, scattered through the column.
5. **The loose herd** as a separate unburdened mass, plus **outrider specks** ahead and on the flanks.

**Period imagery to reference**: **George Catlin, "Band of Sioux Moving Camp" (1837–39, Smithsonian American Art Museum)** — the canonical image, horse and dog travois in one frame; **Alfred Jacob Miller, "Breaking up Camp at Sunrise" and "Moving Camp" (Walters Art Museum, 1858–60 watercolors** from 1837 field sketches — Sioux were present at the rendezvous he documented). **Bodmer is the weak leg here** — superb for winter camps, tipis, and dress, but he left no strong moving-village composition; use him for texture, not for the column.

**Anachronism check on your framing**: nothing wrong with Brulé/autumn/1857 (post-Harney, pre-1858 — bands were moving warily in exactly this country). The only corrections: broad front rather than single file, walking pace, and restrained dust.

Sources: [Catlin, *Band of Sioux Moving Camp*, SAAM](https://americanart.si.edu/artwork/band-sioux-moving-camp-3893) · [Henderson, "Replicating Horse and Travois Travel," *Prairie Forum* 21:2 (1996)](https://iportal.usask.ca/docs/Prairie%20Forum/Replicating%20Horse%20and%20Travois%20Travel%20(v21no2_1996_pg137-147).pdf) · [Miller, *Breaking up Camp at Sunrise*, Walters](https://art.thewalters.org/object/37.1940.142/) and [*Moving Camp*, Walters](https://art.thewalters.org/detail/24860/moving-camp/) · [Parkman, *The Oregon Trail* (Gutenberg)](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1015/1015-h/1015-h.htm) · [Travois (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travois)

---

### Summary of flags for the manuscript/illustrations
1. **Fort Kearny unstockaded — confirmed.** Use the Mills 1858 photo. Trees young (~10 growing seasons), bare in December; mixed frame + low sod/adobe, two-story frame CO quarters and barracks; sutler's store was frame.
2. **Trading post**: sod *walls* are the one soft spot — log walls (or log banked with sod) with the sagging earth-and-turf roof is the better-documented idiom for Niobrara/White River country. Cast-iron stove possible but a mud-chimney fireplace or sheet-iron stove is more typical; keep the stovepipe visual if the stove stays. Everything else (20×15, plank counter, candles, lean-to stable) checks out.
3. **Moving village**: quarter-to-half-mile-wide broad front, not a single-file column; walking pace; thin low dust haze only; the travois pole angle (~30–40°) repeated across dozens of horses plus a separate loose herd is what sells the silhouette at two miles.</result>

## ASSISTANT

Excellent report — three text-audit results and one policy catch before the plates:

1. **Fort Kearny: the text survives again** ("wood and sod buildings," unstockaded — both confirmed), but the research surfaces a famous absence: the parade-ground **cottonwoods**, planted 1848, ~ten seasons grown by Farrell's visit, and by the 1857 account *the only trees visible in any direction*. The text's Kearny has no trees. Not a contradiction — but a period-savvy reader might miss them. Flagged as an enrichment candidate for your ruling, not applied.
2. **Trading post: the one soft spot found** — "sod house" walls are Platte-valley idiom; in timbered Niobrara country the documented posts (Bordeaux, almost exactly our building) were **log walls banked with sod** under the sagging turf roof. Defensible as written, but one clause would make it bulletproof: "a low house of cottonwood logs banked with sod." Also flagged for you. The plate will render the researched idiom either way, since it reads as "sod house, low and rough" from any distance — which is all Farrell says.
3. **Village: the text's "strung out along a ridgeline" survives via terrain** (broad-front is the open-prairie norm, but a ridge traverse strings out — Grinnell's "where terrain allowed"), and the river-around-a-snag bend is actually broad-front behavior. The plate gets the real silhouette signature: travois pole-triangles at 30–40° repeated across dozens of horses, dog travois low, the loose herd as a second mass.
4. **Policy catch in my own catalog**: fort-kearny's prompt had "a lone rider approaching" — that rider is Farrell. Removed; the plate *is* his approach view. And village-passing is a JE VI scene — journal-side — so it goes **portrait plate**, not landscape vignette, per doctrine.

Filing the research and updating the entries now (generation waits for the GPU — the homestead/morrow-witnessed batch is still running):

Plates 9 and 10 are in (349c8ca) — **trading-post** (pale-ink window, and the log-banked walls now match the *text* you authorized) and **homestead** (with a logged wall-texture caveat that only matters if the vignette ever prints large). Kearny is pulled per your blocking catch and re-rolling with the smoke bound to the buildings; morrow-witnessed re-rolls behind it.

The one decision on your desk: **village-passing has hit a genuine capability wall.** Seven attempts, zero travois — the model renders harness teams, wagons, or plain riders; it has no visual concept for the pole-drag. This is the concept-LoRA case you predicted ("we may need to LoRA ourselves some environment"), and the training data is unusually poetic: **Catlin's *Band of Sioux Moving Camp* and Miller's moving-camp watercolors — public domain, and literally the reference corpus our imagined 1859 engraver would himself have used.** The pipeline is proven; I'd estimate an afternoon (pull ~10–15 PD images, caption the travois structurally, micro-LoRA at low rank, compose into the scene). Alternatives: shelve the plate (it's one of the three Lakota beats — a real loss), or accept a travois-less distant procession (the plate stops meaning what it means). My recommendation is the micro-LoRA, but it's a scope call and the session's deep — it may also be a natural first task for a fresh session.

## USER

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   DK (Daniel Klein) is running the image-generation phase of *White Buffalo*, a ~30.6k-word literary Western novella (1858–59 Nebraska Territory, drafted by Opus 4.5, manuscript at v0.8). His explicit requests this session, in order:
   - Kick off image-gen; restart the session in a refreshed flake environment early (done twice — second restart to pick up gcc).
   - Confirm environment, proceed largely autonomously; DK gives director-style taste notes on concrete artifacts (standing collaboration mode).
   - Style register: DK picked **engraving** from the 4-register A/B.
   - Beat plate gibberish via negative prompting (cropping as fallback); DK also floated SFT — prompt-level fix sufficed.
   - Morrow appearance sign-off with riders: no-hat must be survivable (answer: the kit blanket over head/shoulders, 06:21); Mexican War flashback Morrow would be hatted (Mississippi Rifles kit), outside the LoRA's scope.
   - Fix candidate rounds: "Monster Manual" cadaverousness, googly eyes, over-prominent knife, over-aggressive balding; anchors s121+s211 agreed; final 15-image training set signed off "no notes."
   - Draft Farrell for contrast (s701=before journey, s702=after — "the conceit of the text's existence"); then ruled: if Farrell appears he'd need a LoRA; lean = tie images to journal entries. My synthesis — **journal plates may show Morrow; Farrell chapters contribute evidence/places only; Farrell never depicted; compose from behind his eyes** — approved: "Sounds good, aligned, codify and proceed."
   - Full personal (non-delegated) read of the whole text with Morrow's appearance in mind.
   - Letters-timing seam: fix via option (b) + partial (c); "I don't think it would cash out as a regime shift in letter frequency. At most... sitting with drafts a bit longer."
   - "Do one piece of art/ at a time and then bounce it off the text to drive revisions, at least until we prove out that engine."
   - Format doctrine: book physicality forces portrait plates, "with maybe a rare single exception for a fold-out."
   - Cover: iterate wider (5–10 candidates, prompt adjustment, gibberish at bottom); then the **period-vantage insight** (s71/s65 foreshortening reads modern — optics inaccessible to the imagined 1859 artist); final = **s78 "no reservations"**, s74 kept as study/reference.
   - Caught malformed calf + three-legged bull in promoted first-sighting → rescue or re-pick (resolved: joint s87 pair).
   - Re-read the six illustrated scenes against what the plates taught; then one more solo plate before batching.
   - "You should grab reference material and images on the sort of construction that the Hardins would be able to accomplish" (research commissioned/filed); "I'm also noting willow-ish trees in the background. What do we know about the trees we'd find in such a setting?" (weeping-willow catch).
   - Principle endorsed: "It striking how much more specific, interesting, and unsettling graves2/ is than graves/. Generally, moves like the one we just made are very valuable." → research-before-prompt-craft banked as standing rule.
   - Batch remaining scenes "as you see fit" (shaped batch: research first, then generation, one curation session).
   - Most recent: "Address the text-audit results you mentioned as you see fit. I'm not committed to the current text where it proves problematic against your more detailed research. And there's a blocking issue on fort-kearny.png. There appears to be smoke coming out of the top of the flag-pole, which suggests the model may be rendering it as a narrow smoke stack."

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Stack**: diffusers `ZImagePipeline`, `MODEL="Tongyi-MAI/Z-Image"`, `REVISION="04cc4abb7c5069926f75c9bfde9ef43d49423021"` (== hub main == Jan 2026 Base release; 20GB snapshot in /fastcache/dlk/huggingface via XDG_CACHE_HOME; Turbo cached separately). musubi-tuner (fresh clone, gitignored) for training; VAE + Qwen3-4B TE reused from /home/dlk/workspace/z-image/models/models/split_files/.
   - **NixOS fixes**: `TRITON_LIBCUDA_PATH=/run/opengl-driver/lib` in flake shellHook; bitsandbytes excluded from inference env; gcc in flake; training venv on uv-managed python (`python-preference = "only-managed"`) because nix profile python lacks Python.h (December z-image runs only worked via a warm ~/.triton cache).
   - **Morrow LoRA recipe**: morrow_engraving_v1, dim 32, 16 epochs (~2,400 steps, ~1h40m on the 4090); trigger `jmorrow` + light descriptors ("the tall thin bearded man"); **multiplier ~1.3**; **final checkpoint for portraits, ep12 for narrative scenes**; register negative is load-bearing (cream ground, border suppression); bare trigger at 1.0 loses to base priors; diffusers conversion via `convert_lora.py --target other`; loaded with peft (`load_lora_weights` + `set_adapters`).
   - **Banked rulebook** (imagegen/findings.md): #1 architecture/defining attribute LEADS the prompt (generalizes to color); #2 ~three compositional charges per frame + zero-sum attention corollary (compress validated details once won); #3 engraving landscape prior is ALPINE — assert plains landform, negate mountains; #4 anatomy promptable at species-and-age granularity (morphology blocks + domestic-livestock negatives + "strictly monochrome ink" for non-winter scenes); #5 period vantage (whole form, level, sketchable distance; witness-anchored scenes inherit it free, emblems need it enforced). Plus: **zoom-gate mandatory** (2× crop of small figures pre-promotion); **rhyme pairs picked jointly, never sequentially**; **negatives are probabilistic, not gates**; **research before prompt-craft**; ~30px figures are an anatomical lottery; back/distance shots go identity-free.
   - **Shared-seed rhyme**: same seed + same dims → shared low-freq massing; measured via 16×24 grayscale normalized Pearson correlation; pays most between dissimilar scenes. Pairs in art/: morrow-hollow↔journal-found (s45, +0.668), first-sighting↔finale (s87, +0.492).
   - **Plate policy**: journal entries may show Morrow (LoRA'd); Farrell chapters = evidence/places only; FARRELL NEVER DEPICTED.
   - **Format doctrine**: portrait plates (832×1216) journal-side; landscape in-text vignettes Farrell-side; village-passing = sole fold-out candidate.
   - **DNI list**: killings (traces: graves/stains/broken chair), Morrow's death (traces: churned snow, corpse as found, blood on chest/forelegs), ending resolution, Lakota interiority. **Spot color refused consistently** (red blood, red cloth, yellow window).
   - **Process hazards**: background Bash cwd drift (bake `cd /home/dlk/workspace/white-buffalo/imagegen &&` into commands); zsh doesn't word-split unquoted vars (use bash scripts); hung generate.py zombies hold ~14GB VRAM causing OOM cascades (check `nvidia-smi --query-compute-apps`, kill); never diagnose "stalled" from instantaneous GPU util; grep/tail filters swallow tracebacks.
   - Secrets/constraints: HF_TOKEN read from /etc/nixos/secrets/huggingface-token-2025-12-14 (value leaked once into command output — never reproduce it); ../z-image is NOT canon (memory file); do not build a profile of the human co-authors; standing permission for LFS/flake.nix-level changes.

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **flake.nix** — merged shell: git, git-lfs, gnumake, pandoc, vale, uv, ruff, stdenv.cc.cc.lib, zlib, cudaPackages.{cudatoolkit,cudnn}, **gcc**, libGL/libGLU/X11/glib; LD_LIBRARY_PATH incl. /run/opengl-driver; shellHook exports TRITON_LIBCUDA_PATH + HF_TOKEN.
   - **.gitattributes** — LFS: `art/**/*.png|jpg|webp`, `*.safetensors`, `imagegen/training/dataset/**/*.png`. **.gitignore** — imagegen/{.venv,models,output}, imagegen/training/{musubi-tuner,.venv,cache,output}.
   - **imagegen/generate.py** — production CLI: ASPECTS {portrait (832,1216), landscape (1216,832), square (1024,1024)}; `LORAS = {"morrow": "loras/morrow_engraving_v1_diffusers.safetensors", "morrow-ep12": "loras/morrow_engraving_v1_ep12_diffusers.safetensors"}`; `DEFAULT_LORA_WEIGHT = 1.3`; flags --scene/--prompt/--style/--negative/--aspect/--seed/--steps/--guidance/--lora/--list/--output-dir; scenes may declare `lora = "name@weight"` and per-scene `negative`; negatives merge style+scene+CLI.
   - **imagegen/catalog.toml** — cited scene catalog (prompt, aspect, citations file:line, underdetermined, dni, optional lora/negative); header carries DNI + plate policy; every plate's prompt iterated per the rules. broken-oar/blizzard/reading-night SHELVED-BY-POLICY with recomposition notes.
   - **imagegen/styles.toml** — engraving DECIDED: prefix "1850s steel engraving artwork, fine crosshatching, monochrome ink on cream paper, detailed linework, the engraved image itself filling the entire frame edge to edge"; negative "color, photograph, modern, digital, painting, soft focus, text, letters, words, caption, title, inscription, signature, plate number, border, frame, margin".
   - **imagegen/findings.md** — the engine's full log (register behaviors, gibberish rounds, rhyme method+numbers, plates 1–10 histories, all rules, batch scoreboards, research-first principle, village capability wall).
   - **imagegen/morrow-reference.md** — canon vs signed-off choices; composure section (controlling art direction: soldier gone to rags, flat still eyes, never crazed); FINAL TRAINING SET (15, signed off): s121,s125,s126,s301,s302,s303,s304,s211,s215,s401,s402,s405,s411,s511,s311; training results + production recipe.
   - **imagegen/farrell-reference.md** — contrast spec; anchors s701 (before) / s702 (after).
   - **imagegen/breed_morrow.py / draft_farrell.py** — single-load breeding scripts (IDENTITY + STYLE + NEGATIVE + VARIANTS, skip-if-exists).
   - **imagegen/training/** — pyproject.toml (musubi-tuner[cu124] editable, only-managed python, cu124 find-links); morrow_dataset.toml (res [1024,1024], bucketing, repeats 10); train_morrow.sh (cache latents → cache TE --fp8_llm → accelerate zimage_train_network.py: shift sampling, discrete_flow_shift 2.0, adamw8bit, lr 1e-4, lora_zimage dim 32, 16 epochs, save every 4); validate_grid.sh / validate_grid2.sh (bash); dataset/morrow/ (15 imgs + captions "jmorrow, 1850s steel engraving illustration, <setting>").
   - **imagegen/loras/** — 4 safetensors (musubi + diffusers formats, final + ep12) via LFS.
   - **art/** (LFS, current 9): cover.png (s78), morrow-hollow.png (s45), journal-found.png (s45), offering-stake.png (s53), first-sighting.png (s87), finale-fifty-yards.png (s87), graves.png (s93), trading-post.png (batch3b s104), homestead.png (batch1 s100, wall-texture caveat). **fort-kearny.png PULLED** (flagstaff-smokestack; re-roll s108/s109 generated, unverified).
   - **planning/image-gen.md** — role, infrastructure, model decision, plate policy, rules, plan (steps 1–6 done), format doctrine.
   - **reference/pd-engraving-sources.md** (bison rich: Hornaday life-stage plates/Audubon/Catlin, 150–250 imgs in 4–6h; sod-house engravings scarce, 20–60); **reference/homestead-construction.md** (text passes every element; cairn stones = Valentine Fm. opaline sandstone hauled from creek breaks; Hardins = pre-Homestead-Act squatters on unceded Lakota range = the book's premise); **reference/plate-settings-research.md** (Kearny unstockaded confirmed/Mills 1858 photo/planted cottonwoods flagged; trading post log-banked-with-sod idiom/Bordeaux analog; moving village broad-front, travois signature 30–40° pole drags, Catlin/Miller references).
   - **notes/continuity.md** — letters seam RESOLVED entry; plate-driven re-read (text held; PROTECTED SYMMETRY: calf trails herd "by perhaps fifty yards" 02:25 = Farrell-to-cow fifty yards 20:21 — future edits touch both knowingly).
   - **notes/visual-bible.md** — Morrow coat/shirt attested (20:13), worn boots (15:87, 13:61), horns "curved and thickened" (16:17).
   - **Manuscript edits**: (a) commit 908a859 letters seam — 01: "dated May" / "This past year they came a little slower, but they came. They stopped in May." / "dated the twentieth of May"; 03: "The last letter was dated May the twentieth. The garden is in, she wrote, and the creek is running high. George says the worst is behind us. I hope he is right."; 09:117: "That put him in this area in August—the same summer the trader had last seen the Hardins. The letters had gone quiet in May."; 13:67: "the same summer the Hardins had dropped from sight." (b) commit a8f8f41 research-driven — Ch 4: "with rows of planted cottonwoods still young and bare with the season"; Ch 5: "It was a house of cottonwood logs banked high with sod, low and rough, with a roof of poles and turf that sagged in the middle under the weight of snow."; Ch 6: dropped "the same" from "built of blocks of cut earth...". Manuscript 30,637 words. Trader's colloquial "Sod house, like this one" deliberately left (vernacular).

4. Errors and fixes:
   - triton /sbin/ldconfig missing → TRITON_LIBCUDA_PATH in shellHook; bitsandbytes eager-import breakage → excluded from inference env; training Python.h missing → uv-managed python + gcc in flake.
   - First validation grid failed silently: VRAM contention (launched while training released memory) AND `tail -1` swallowed tracebacks → capture fuller output.
   - zsh no-word-split broke inline arg expansion twice → bash script files (validate_grid.sh).
   - musubi `--save_path` is a directory (timestamped file inside); `find -name '*.png'` matched dirs named *.png → `-type f`.
   - LoRA weak at multiplier 1.0 with bare trigger (Victorian gentleman; trigger rendered as caption text) → 1.3 + descriptors; white-background mystery = interaction (2.0 × missing negative); negative prompt proved load-bearing for the register.
   - Background Bash cwd drift repeatedly ran commands from repo root (files not found; heredocs failing on relative paths) → bake `cd` in; verify outputs, don't trust exit status.
   - OOM cascades: root cause = **our own hung generate.py zombie holding 14.6GB** plus my double-launch after misdiagnosing a still-running batch as stalled from momentary GPU idle → kill zombie, run serially, check process table.
   - DK's catches, all addressed: Monster-Manual busts / googly eyes / knife prominence / balding (candidate rounds 2–3); cover corner gibberish (hardened negatives, residue croppable); s71/s65 modern foreshortening → Rule #5; malformed calf/three-legged bull in first-sighting s55 → zoom-gate instituted + joint s87 re-pick; journal-found strained scale → Rule #2; weeping willows (mourning-emblem prior; correct flora = sandbar willow thickets); fort-kearny flagstaff-smokestack → pulled, re-rolled (verdict pending).
   - Audit-caught canon violations: red spot-color blood; Farrell summoned into frame by "from a kneeling man's height" (name a thing, get the thing); visible sun disc vs "invisible but present" (20:51); wagons through negatives; hay-bale anachronism; dairy-calf/white-adult/sheep drift; warm yellow window.

5. Problem Solving:
   Full pipeline proven end-to-end: environment → model decision (Z-Image Base; survey confirmed no successor; FLUX.2 klein 4B fallback) → engraving register → Morrow LoRA (trained, validated on a novel-scene probe, checkpoint-split recipe) → plate-by-plate coherence engine with 5 banked rules → shaped batch. Nine plates in art/ plus two measured rhyme pairs. The text won nearly every tribunal (six-scene re-read forced zero tweaks); the two manuscript-level findings (letters seam; trading-post walls + Kearny cottonwoods) were fixed with DK's authorization. Open problem: **village-passing capability wall** — 7 attempts, the model has no horse-travois concept (renders harness teams, wagons, plain riders); recommended a micro-LoRA on PD Catlin ("Band of Sioux Moving Camp") / Miller moving-camp images — "the imagined engraver's own reference corpus" — possibly as a fresh-session task; DK has not yet ruled.

- **morrow-witnessed**: s108 rejected (the figure carried a staff and read as a drover); s109 was close — bareheaded, empty-handed, following the herd — but only ~20 yards behind the last animal, which loses the "lost dog" quality of the trader's testimony. I hardened the catalog entry (staff/drover negatives, the gap asserted as "a long stretch of empty unbroken snow") and seeds 110–113 are generating now.

**Rework underway** — my diagnosis of the two early plates matches yours: both violate the period-vantage rule (viewer inside the camp), both render the hollow as a fairy-tale cave mouth, and both predate the hardened register negatives. The text re-read also surfaced two real findings: the current journal-found says "first light" but Farrell finds the camp **at dusk** (19:5), and morrow-hollow misses the vigil entirely — both cited entries insist "I can see her from here," so the reworked plate should show Morrow small at his fire in the bluff face with the wintering herd dark in the valley below. The rhyme reframes from *writing/found* to *his camp with him in it / his camp without him*.

Per our research-before-prompt-craft rule, a research agent is out gathering material on Niobrara bluff/cutbank morphology, 19th-century firelight-nocturne engraving conventions (new register territory for us), and the material culture of a one-blanket winter camp — it'll file `reference/hollow-camp-research.md`. Prompts get rewritten when it lands, then a joint shared-seed sweep scored with the new `rhyme.py` tool (which reproduces the logged pair values, so we have a clean baseline of +0.66 to beat).

<summary>Agent "Research hollow camps + firelight engravings" completed</summary>
<result>Wrote `/home/dlk/workspace/white-buffalo/reference/hollow-camp-research.md` (~160 lines, sourced with URLs). Key conclusions:

1. Niobrara bluffs are soft layered sediment (crumbly Valentine sandstone, loess, Rosebud siltstone) eroding by seepage and freeze-thaw — hollows are undercut scoops beneath resistant lips, not rock caves.
2. Anti-cave cues: wide shallow re-entrant, lit back wall, crumbly bedding, hanging roots, spall piles, floor sloping out to grade — never a black arched mouth.
3. Sandhills "hollow in the lee of a low hill" = blowout: grass-rimmed bare bowl, feet to hundreds of feet across, opening downwind.
4. Scarp vegetation is scattered ponderosa/eastern redcedar (sparser in the 1850s than now); spring-line ice curtains on bluff faces are a period-credible winter detail.
5. Snow: windward flats scour bare; a drift cornice curls over the cutbank lip while the lee floor stays dry and dark — the man and fire sit in that pocket.
6. Engraved night is cut, not glowed: white-line/tint technique, fire the only pure paper-white, concentric hatched light zones, rim-lit or silhouetted figures, worked (never empty) blacks.
7. Anachronism failures: photographic bloom/lens flare, featureless chiaroscuro void, orange hue, fire too big or centered.
8. A chip or thin-brush fire is low, nearly flameless, ember-cored, ash-heavy, consumed in 1-2 hours — hat-sized, rebuilt 2-3 times a night in hard cold.
9. One-blanket posture: seated, knees up, blanket hooded, back to bank, feet to fire (or lying at right angles to it); the whole camp rolls into the blanket.
10. Cold abandoned camp: downwind ash streak, charred butt-ends, held body-print, snow re-softening edges — a smudge and a body-print from twenty feet; smallness is the visual.

Exemplars cited: Currier &amp; Ives *The Trappers' Camp-Fire* (LOC), Harper's moonlight plates, Doré tint engraving, Darley; Warren's 1855 *Dacota Country* report linked on archive.org as the period terrain baseline.</result>

## ASSISTANT

Full read complete — all twenty-one files, in order. The book holds together remarkably well under this kind of cross-examination; I stress-tested the timeline, the witness chain, the evidence trail, and the object continuity, and nearly every join held. Here's what I found, ranked.

## Findings that want a ruling

**1. Morrow's age seam (one-word fix available).** JE IX fixes his birth: "born in Virginia in the year eighteen twenty-four" (18:17), and "thirty-four years" of emptiness at his death in January 1859 (18:53) agrees. But JE VI has "I was thirty-two years old when I left Virginia" (12:47) — and by April 19, 1856 (JE I) he has already "walked a long time… lost count of the months." A man born in 1824 who left Virginia at thirty-two can't have months of plains walking behind him by April 1856. Leaving in mid-to-late 1855 at **thirty-one** makes everything click: months of walking by April ✓, and JE VI's very next line — "Now I am thirty-three, or perhaps thirty-four" (October 1857) — becomes exactly right without modification. The fix is literally one word: "thirty-two" → "thirty-one."

**2. "Like a ship through water" appears twice, once per narrator.** Morrow, JE III: "her white form moving through the brown grass like a ship through water" (06:9). Farrell, Ch 11: "Her white coat moved through the snow like a ship through water" (20:57). This is the profile's verbatim-recycling red flag — *except* that by Chapter Eleven Farrell has spent the night reading the journal, including that very entry. There's a defensible, even beautiful, reading: the journal colonizing Farrell's voice. But as a single unmarked instance it's more likely to read as authorial recycling than as character-borrowing. Options: (a) break Farrell's instance; (b) bless it and let it stand as a buried payoff; (c) bless it *and* reinforce — one more borrowed Morrow image in Farrell's Ch 11 narration would establish the pattern as deliberate. My lean is (b) — the book's stated tolerance for reader-missed payoffs covers it — but it's a taste call.

**3. Ch 8 conflates two witnesses.** "…north when the mail carrier passed him, north when the trapper watched him cross the frozen river" (15:55). But the trapper saw Morrow moving *along* the Niobrara bottom (07:117); it was Creighton the mail carrier who saw him *crossing* the Loup Fork (07:175). Farrell is days into withdrawal here, so fever-blur is a plausible defense — but the book's habit is to let Farrell correct his own errors ("So I had it half-wrong"), and unremarked ones read as ours. Cheap fix if wanted: "…north when the trapper saw him on the Niobrara."

**4. JE VII's window observation strains unaided eyes.** "Sometimes she sits by the window for an hour, hardly moving. Reading, I suppose" (14:83). Morrow's gear is blanket, knife, flint, journal — no glass — and his established watching distance is a quarter mile. Seeing *into* a small sod-house window at that range is a stretch. A period-true alternative is inference from absence: he doesn't see her *at all* for an hour together though the day is fine, and supposes reading. Small, optional.

**5. Gunderson's "good land near the Elkhorn" (07:73).** The Elkhorn country is well east of where Farrell meets him (two days east of Kearny — a family bound there is past their turnoff by 150 miles). Either a one-word swap to a plausible destination, or leave it: a man hauling his family west in December with no spare axle being past his turnoff is almost in character — but the text doesn't wink at it, so it currently reads as our error, not his.

**6. Geography note, log-only.** JE VIII puts the final valley "north of the river" (16:77) — if that's the Niobrara, Farrell crossed it during the pursuit without ever narrating it, despite it being the river he was told the Hardins lived on. The book's geography is impressionistic by design and I don't think any reader could convict, but it belongs in continuity notes in case we ever draw a map.

## Things that impressed me on this pass

The consistency lattice around the off-page confession is airtight: Farrell quotes an entry we never see (19:47), JE VIII refers to it obliquely ("I have written of what was done in it, and I will not write of it again," 16:5), and JE IX cross-references it again ("The settlers cost me something—I wrote of it at the time," 18:53). The killing itself stays off-page in *both* narrations — the DNI rule was already alive in the prose. The evidence in Ch 7 (blood at the barn door, blood at the bedframe) reads exactly against the routine Morrow documented in JE VII (man at the barn before dawn, woman waking later) without anyone saying so. The whiskey ledger balances bottle by bottle from Chicago to the last quarter-inch. The candle stub Farrell inventories in the hollow (19:21) is the candle JE IX was written by (18:7). Both narrators meet a frozen man early in their walking — Morrow's seated corpse with raised hands (02:9), Farrell's reaching hand in the drift (09:15) — an unannounced rhyme of exactly the kind the book should have. And the witness chain's repetition-with-variation ("like a dog following its master" → "like a dog that's lost its master") reads as folklore forming in real time, which Ch 4 explicitly thematizes.

**One plate-relevant extraction:** JE IX confirms Morrow's *final* camp also overlooks the valley and the herd ("I can see the valley below, and the herd gathered there," 18:59) — so both hollows in our reworked pair are watching-places above valleys. The occupied/abandoned rhyme is even more textually grounded than I'd realized; both reworked prompts already carry the valley relationship, so no changes needed.

Nothing here needs to block the image work. Item 1 is the only thing I'd call a defect; 2 needs your taste; 3–5 are polish; 6 is a note. Say the word on any of them and I'll make the edits.

## ASSISTANT

Text fixes are committed (d57844c, 30,640 words), and the morrow-hollow v2 sweep is still working through its VRAM-wait. On to (4) — this is a fun one to brainstorm, because the text already contains the seed of its own answer.

The core insight: Morrow doesn't need concealment, because he has spent two years becoming *ambient*. The book establishes it repeatedly — "I am part of the landscape to them, no more threatening than a rock or a tree" (02:21, of the herd), and the trader's testimony inverts it: "He looked at me like I wasn't there. Like I was a tree or a rock" (09:91). The asymmetry you want is already thematically paid for: **to the settlers, Morrow is the tree or the rock.** He doesn't skulk; he sits in the open and is not seen, because stillness at distance is invisibility and settlers' eyes are always on their work. What the buffalo taught him works even better on people.

Options, roughly cheapest to richest:

**A. Physics fix — the open window.** It's late July; a sod-house interior is unbearable and the window (and door) stand open. A woman sits *in* the window aperture because it's the only reading light — period-true. A framed, lit, seated figure is visible at long range to a patient eye; the text only ever claims shape and stillness. One clause: *"The window stands open in the heat now. Some mornings she sits by it for an hour, hardly moving. Reading, I suppose…"* Saves direct observation, costs almost nothing, leaves the asymmetry implicit.

**B. Asymmetry stated, in his voice.** Morrow names the occupational imbalance flatly, and the "why" can borrow his own established construction ("It would not do to make her fear me," 06:13): *"I sit in the open on the ridge and am not seen. The herd taught me stillness, and these are people whose eyes are always on their work. It would not do to be seen — not for my sake."* The last beat quietly carries the darker logic: being seen would bring attention near *her*. Risk: it explains; his register can hold it, but it's telling.

**C. Geometry fix.** Anchor the July watching to the cottonwood stand on the ridge that JE VI already established at a quarter mile (12:69), plus a light-angle detail (morning sun reaching the doorway). Concrete but edges toward surveillance-technical.

**D. Asymmetry dramatized — the look that doesn't land.** A micro-beat, no explanation: *"Once the man straightened from his work and looked a long while at the ridge where I sat. I did not move. After a time he went back to his hay. A man still enough in this country is a fence post, a burned tree, whatever the eye expects."* This shows the invisibility instead of claiming it, completes the trader-scene inversion, and — the part I find genuinely chilling — retroactively means Morrow was *in plain sight the whole time*. The reader feels the Hardins fail their one chance to see him coming.

**E. My recommendation: A + D.** ~Three added sentences in JE VII: the open window makes the observation optically honest; the failed look makes the asymmetry dramatic rather than asserted. No skulking anywhere — the opposite: openness as the deepest cover.

One tie-in either way: JE VI currently has "hidden in a stand of cottonwoods" (12:69) — that "hidden" is the one word in the book where Morrow does skulk. Dropping it (he's just *in* the stand) aligns the earlier entry with whichever option you pick.

Say the word and I'll draft the actual passage.

## ASSISTANT

JE VII and JE VI are in (commit 660b550). What the D beat became, post-triad-surgery:

> *Once the man straightened in his field and looked a long while at the ridge where I sat. I did not move. After a time he bent back to his work. Their eyes are on their work from first light to dark. A man sitting still on a ridge is a tree, or a rock.*

The triad's abstraction slot is gone — it ends on two concrete nouns, and those two nouns are doing deliberate work: they invert the trader's "like I was a tree or a rock" (09:91), which the reader encounters first in book order. Morrow's blindness to people; people's blindness to Morrow. Chronologically Morrow writes it *before* the trader says it — the world confirming his self-knowledge rather than him borrowing a phrase. Also: "I did not move." is Morrow's exact construction from the Lakota-watching beat (08:51) — his one fixed response to being looked at. And the new beat sets up an escalation the text already had waiting: July 25, the man looks at the ridge and sees nothing; August 3, he looks at the herd and misses her. Two failures of seeing, a week apart, before the decision falls.

The A half is one sentence — "The window stands open in the heat now" — which makes her framed, lit stillness readable at his distance without changing what he claims to see. JE VI's "hidden" became "where the trees broke my shape against the sky": occlusion as a fact of position, not an act. All protected in continuity notes, including a do-not-dedup flag on the tree-or-rock inversion.

The morrow-hollow v3 sweep (fire clause reinstated, seeds 122–124) is generating now; I'll gate the fire and bring you the final pair when it lands.

## ASSISTANT

**The pair is reworked, promoted, and committed** (c128997). Side by side with what they replaced:

**morrow-hollow** — was: a viewer crouched inside a cave mouth watching a giant Morrow write. Now: a winter nocturne at sketchable distance — Morrow small in a wind-scooped hollow under a snow-corniced cutbank lip, bareheaded, blanket at his shoulders, a small stick fire whose white flame-star is the only pure paper-white in the plate; and beyond the camp, under moonlit cloud, the massed dark herd with the white cow luminous and slightly apart among them. The vigil is finally *in* the plate — "I can see her from here" (08:37, 12:107) — and the wall behind him carries the seepage stripes and ice forms the research predicted.

**journal-found** — was: a grotto with a nest of giant blankets. Now: dusk (canon-corrected from "first light"), a six-foot scoop in a hillside lee, folded trade blankets with the dark book on them, a charred cold fire pit beside the single line of bootprints walking away, one stunted pine at the bank edge. The abandoned camp's smallness — the research's "a smudge and a body-print under a bank lip" — is the visual.

The rhyme reframed from *writing/found* to *his camp with him in it / his camp without him*: lit against unlit, night against dusk, same seed massing (+0.562 on shared seed 122 — the bookends pair sits at +0.484; the old pair's +0.66 was inflated by being the same cave composition twice). Three rounds total, with one new rule banked in findings: every compositional charge needs its *state* asserted — "a fire" buys sticks, not flame.

Worth your eyeball when convenient: `art/morrow-hollow.png` and `art/journal-found.png`.

Next up per your blessing: the village-passing micro-LoRA — dataset's in hand (18 PD images, Remington/Catlin strongest), so the build order is preprocessing (rotations, letterpress crop, grayscale), captions with a travois trigger, then training on the proven morrow recipe scaled down. I'll get the preprocessing moving.

## ASSISTANT

Gunderson ruling received — revising. The Wood River is the geographically honest destination: it parallels the Platte just west of Fort Kearny, had nascent settlement by 1858–59, and means he's actually heading toward his stated goal when Farrell meets him (the Elkhorn was 150 miles behind him). And while I'm changing his background: a German reads tighter than a Swede for 1858 Nebraska — the big 1850s immigrant colonies there (Grand Island, 1857) were German; Swedish settlement came mostly a decade later. His "Ja" survives the change. Checking for all references first.

## ASSISTANT

**Village travois LoRA is training** (task will run ~1¾ hours — 2,240 steps, same cadence as the morrow run). Full state of play:

**Dataset (committed, 586ba12):** 14 curated images in `imagegen/training/dataset/village/` — the imagined engraver's actual reference corpus: two Catlin line-engraving plates (the Comanche moving column is the star — dozens of pole-drags receding to the horizon), the Eastman steel engraving (single travois at close scale, exactly our 1850s register), the Remington (cleanest structural diagram of the whole set), Leslie's 1877 procession-from-behind with a child-cage travois, two Bodmer aquatints, and six grayscaled paintings (Catlin ×3, Eastman, Russell, Deming) plus Miller's cavalcade for the rider-mass component. Every crop was eyeballed at full size: captions gone, plate borders trimmed, the oval mount inscribed away, frame shadows cut. Photos excluded; concept hazards (standing tipis, Miller's mountains) bound to their own words in captions so "travois" stays clean. Worth a browse if you want to veto anything — checkpoints save every 4 epochs, so a curation change isn't catastrophic mid-run.

**Recipe:** cloned verbatim from the proven morrow run (dim 32, lr 1e-4, shift sampling, 16 epochs, saves at 4/8/12/16). Trigger is the word "travois" itself with structural glosses — we're teaching the model what the word means visually, so village-passing's existing catalog prompt should largely work as written once the LoRA loads.

**When it lands:** validation grid against the village-passing prompt at 2–3 checkpoints and 2–3 weights, zoom-gate the travois geometry, then the real seeds. The scene stays the fold-out candidate, so I'll try both portrait and a wide panorama once the concept proves.

Today's ledger, for the record: fort-kearny and morrow-witnessed promoted (batch4 closed), the hollow pair reworked and re-promoted, six full-read text findings resolved (age seam, journal-bleed borrowings blessed and extended, witness conflation, JE VII open window + failed look, Brandt/Wood River, Ch 9 Niobrara crossing), and the manuscript sits at 30,780 words.

## ASSISTANT

Harness is committed (f3b8efe) and training is genuinely underway — the VRAM guard held it back about ten minutes until your ollama server unloaded, then fired.

The grid, ready to run the moment training completes (`./validate_village.sh`, ~35 minutes, skip-if-exists so it resumes cleanly):

**Rows.** A controlled epoch-0 baseline (final checkpoint at multiplier 0.0, same seed — no new code path, mathematically identical to no-LoRA); the checkpoint ladder 4/8/12/final at 1.3 on two probes; a multiplier-response pair (1.0 vs 1.3) on the production scene; and a generalization probe at the two late checkpoints.

**Probes.** P1 is dataset-adjacent (single travois horse, close) — pure concept sanity. P2 is the *verbatim* village-passing catalog prompt under full production conditions (style prefix, style negative, scene negative, portrait) — because the capability wall was always about the concept surviving at tiny-silhouette distance, and that's the only test that counts. P3 is a novel composition (travois down a slope to a creek) to check the LoRA learned a concept rather than memorized fourteen paintings.

**Decision criteria, baked into the script header:** pole triangles not wagons/harness-teams; survival at silhouette scale; register integrity under the prefix (half the dataset is grayscaled oils — painterly mush is the failure to watch); leakage checks (standing tipis, Miller's mountains, unrequested willow cages); and the multiplier response, since morrow needed 1.3 but a concept may saturate earlier.

The morrow lessons are all encoded: never concurrent with training, `tail -3` instead of the traceback-swallowing `tail -1`, skip-if-exists, and the seven pre-LoRA failures in `output/plates` stand as the informal baseline if the m0.0 row ever looks suspicious. When training notifies, I'll fire the grid, zoom-gate, and bring you the ladder.

Only the **final checkpoint** drops wheels for true travois geometry, on both the close probe and the production scene. So unlike morrow's portrait/narrative checkpoint split, village deploys final-only.
- **The production-conditions probe is the proof**: baseline (multiplier 0.0) renders harness-teams hauling a wheeled load; the final checkpoint renders parallel pole-drags scoring the grass, triangles behind the horses, a procession receding — it genuinely reads like a Catlin plate.
- **The register negative is load-bearing, again.** Under a light negative the LoRA drifts toward its dataset's grayscaled oils (the generalization probe came out as soft tonal wash); under the full production negative it stays crisp engraving. The scene negative is hardened accordingly (cart, wheels, sled, harness team, plow, oil painting, tonal wash).
- **Deploying at 1.0**, not 1.3 — both carry the concept, but 1.0 composes closer to the text's two-mile distance.
- **The remaining fight is compositional, not conceptual**: the LoRA wants travois in the near field, because that's where its dataset puts them, while JE VI pins the village "along a ridgeline two miles off" (12:59). The catalog's distance language has to hold the line against that pull.

The real production sweep — village-passing, portrait, six seeds, full catalog conditions — is rolling now. When it lands: zoom-gates on travois legibility at silhouette scale, the two-mile check, and register integrity, then the candidates come to you. - `diagram-ewers-fig19-lodgepole-hitch.jpg` (p. 107) — close-up of poles lashed over a wood saddle; the withers-crossing geometry from a second angle.

- `diagram-wissler-fig56-travois-types.jpg` (Wissler 1910, p. 89) — full-page plan view of both frame types (netted oval hoop vs. transverse-bar rack) with the apex lashing wrap.

- `photo-barthelmess-children-travois-cage-1890.jpg` (4752px, 1890) — best photographic side profile acquired: pad-saddle lashing, shafts past the flanks, willow cage with children, drag angle. Note: Commons titles it "Huffman" but the print is signed C. Barthelmess — PD either way; both noted in the manifest.
- `photo-lac-blood-ponies-travois-1910.tif` (LAC copyright-deposit print, 1910) — three rigged ponies in one frame; rig consistency across animals.

- `photo-ewers-plate6-cheyenne-cage-travois.jpg` — second Cheyenne cage-travois photo (mounted rider) + an 1889 Southern Cheyenne ledger drawing of the complete rig.

## ASSISTANT

Yes — mapping the catalog against the full text, the coverage has a distinct shape: the Farrell chapters are well served through the middle of the journey, and the journal is served at its endpoints (JE I first-sighting, JE IV stake/hollow, JE VI village) — but **the long middle of the vigil, summers 1856–57 (JE II, III, V), has zero plates**, and the Farrell side is missing its two most potent *permitted* evidence images. Here's the gap list, filtered hard through our rules:

**Strong and rules-clean:**

1. **homestead-interior** (Ch 6/7, Farrell-side landscape cut). The DNI list *enumerates* its contents as permitted traces: the scrubbed floor patch, the cracked wall, the broken chair propped in the corner — plus the dust-thick table, cold stove, and the detail that does the real damage, her dress and his coat on their pegs (11:79). Composed from the doorway, behind Farrell's eyes. This is the most textually-armored unmade plate in the book.

2. **homestead-alive** (JE V, journal-side). The living farm seen from Morrow's watching distance — smoke up, tiny figures at their work, the surveillance made visual. It's the alive/dead counterpart to the existing homestead.png, and after today's JE VII work it's the watching theme's home plate. One rules-tension to resolve: format doctrine says journal-side = portrait, but a shared-seed rhyme with the dead-homestead *landscape* vignette requires matching dims. My lean: this is the case where the doctrine should flex — a distant farm across a valley is intrinsically horizontal, and the alive/dead rhyme is worth more than format purity.

3. **speaking-to-her** (JE II, July 1856). Morrow seated on his rise, the white calf grazing apart, a hundred yards of summer grass between them — "I am waiting for you" (04:57-63). Fills the summer register the catalog almost entirely lacks (it's winter-heavy), uses the proven distance grammar, morrow-ep12.

4. **catherine-daguerreotype** (Ch 2, evidence object). A portrait plate *of the daguerreotype* — engraving-after-daguerreotype was a real and ubiquitous period genre, so the imagined engraver has an unimpeachable source: the object Farrell carries (03:3, 01:181). Dark hair, pale eyes, composed for the long exposure, "she looked like her father." It gives the book's absent center a face without ever depicting her in scene. Needs your ruling: nothing in the policy bars Catherine, but it's a significant move — the only human face in the plates besides Morrow's.

5. **tracks-north** (Ch 7 close). The two track-lines in snow — deep hoofprints and boot prints, side by side, heading into the white (13:61-69, 13:111). The pursuit's entire thesis as a minimal in-text cut. Cheap, emblem-class (so Rule #5 must be enforced, not inherited), zoom-gate friendly.

6. **carcass-winter** (JE IV, Dec 11). Morrow hacking meat from the wolf-opened frozen carcass — the vigil's cost (08:27). Grim but DNI-clean, and the validation grid already proved this composition class renders (it was our novel-scene probe).

**Viable with flags:**

- **blizzard-recomposition** (Ch 4) — already noted in the catalog: from behind Farrell's eyes, the horse's snow-crusted neck and mane against the white wall. Rules-clean; the question is whether a 20-foot-visibility image is worth a plate.
- **frozen-hand** (Ch 5) — the dead man's hand reaching from the drift (09:17). Devastating and restrained, rhymes with Morrow's frozen man and prefigures his end — but it's a taste call whether a corpse-trace plate serves or sensationalizes.
- **ridge-watcher** (JE VII) — a plate where Morrow sits in the open on the ridge and the reader almost misses him, exactly as today's "tree, or a rock" beat describes. The risk is cleverness; it would need the flattest possible execution.
- **ferry-bridge** (Ch 3) — the Mississippi ferry deck with the Rock Island bridge's draw open upstream. Historically delicious, but it's the one candidate that decorates rather than testifies.

**Not fillable, correctly:** Ch 8–9's withdrawal interiority (needs Farrell in frame), JE IX's last walk (the DNI's spirit — a plate of the approach to death would compete with the journal's last line), and the Lakota departure (depicting it would fix the real-or-fever ambiguity the ending depends on).

My recommendation: draft **1, 2, 3, 5** into the catalog now — they fill both the journal-middle desert and the evidence chain, all four inside existing rules — with 4 pending your ruling on Catherine, and the flagged three held for a later pass. Say the word and I'll write the entries with citations.

## ASSISTANT

Both candidates pass their gates — and the choice between them is a genuine taste call, so it's yours:

**s144 — my lean.** The text's actual image: the procession as a receding diagonal of *genuinely tiny* silhouettes cresting the rise (the ridgeline zoom is gorgeous — a mounted silhouette topping the hill against the sky), vast empty October sky, the near group pushed to the right edge reading as "riders scattered ahead and on the flanks." It's also the DNI-conservative pick: the figures stay below face resolution everywhere. Two caveats at zoom: some near-group poles read hand-carried rather than lashed (others clearly drag), and the bottom-left grass holds a parked wrapped load that's fine at zoom but slightly ambiguous at plate scale.

**s140 — the craft showcase.** The near group is the training run's pride: two mounted women whose horses carry textbook rigging — poles lashed at the saddle, angling down to dragging loads — with dogs running between, and the village strung along the ridge behind. More classical, better balanced, the strongest travois legibility in the whole project. The cost: the near group's tiny faces are *just* resolved (simple period-plate features, but the catalog's DNI says "figures far too small for faces"), and the foreground is occupied rather than empty.

Rejected: s141 (a shape reading as a fallen figure), s142 (drags gone railroad-geometric), s143 (foreground-heavy, incoherent right edge), s145 (grew a drawn frame and caption gibberish — rescuable by cropping inside the frame if you ever want a third option; it's quite handsome under the frame).

One more decision rides on this: village-passing was the fold-out candidate. These are portrait plates per doctrine — if you want to keep the fold-out dream alive, the v2e4 LoRA is now clearly capable of a wide panorama run as a separate experiment. My suggestion: pick the portrait plate now (it fills the last catalog slot), and treat the panorama as an optional later study.

Which one goes to art/ — s144, s140, or another round?

## ASSISTANT

Zoom verdicts on the three:

- **s153 — my recommendation.** The line bends around the rise in file, properly tiny; and the midground delivers the anchor you asked for in canon form: a **dog-travois hauling its small load** beside a walking figure pulling another — the travois mechanism legible near the viewer with zero foreground riders, and "dogs" is cited text (12:59). The main line's travois read as silhouettes-with-poles rather than explicit triangles — defensible as honest two-mile distance.
- **s151 — the physicality showcase.** The procession line itself is the most travois-legible of the round: pole triangles clearly attached behind several horses, walkers and dogs interleaved, gorgeous wind-combed foreground. Two frame-shapes mid-line edge toward mini-cone/tipi reading at zoom, and the composition is flatter — a straight band rather than s153's bend.
- **s150 — drop**: good drags mid-line, but the far echelon renders as a standing crowd rather than a moving file.
- **B study verdict**: s150-B failed (resting travois → tipi-cones, plus a hull-like shape); s151-B genuinely resolved a lying pole-frame with rack, so the trick is *possible* — but variant A's combed grass and s153's dog-travois make the artificial anchor unnecessary, and it would need an engraver's-license gloss the A images don't. I'd retire the B variant.

Both survivors are DNI-clean (no resolvable faces anywhere) and the register held crisp in all four. My pick is **s153**: it's 12:59 rendered almost clause by clause — the strung-out line, the bend, the lodgepole silhouettes, the dogs, the combed grass — and its "flaw" (drags reading as distance-silhouettes on the far line) is exactly what an 1859 witness-vantage plate should do. s151 is the better proof the LoRA learned its lesson; s153 is the better *plate*.

Your call: s153, s151, or another round?

s174's line settles it at 4×: standing figures and loose quadrupeds, **no legible travois** — at the text's honest two miles the subject dissolves. Which is itself a finding worth keeping: 12:59's literal distance is unillustratable; the period engraver's compression isn't a compromise, it's the only way the plate can *be about* what it's about. Your "artist who has never seen a travois" gloss covers the rest.

**The round's verdict — s175 is my candidate.** It's s153's virtues with the register repaired: the line bending around the low rise in double file, pole-drags legible on horse after horse at silhouette scale (the zoom is genuinely lovely — file after file with poles trailing), pale ruled sky, worked hillside, combed grass leading in. Full disclosure on its one number: the foreground grass band pushes fine-texture stats above even homestead's busy willows (tex3 45.8 vs corpus max 32.5; the tonal metrics — mean, ink, paper — all sit fine). To my eye it reads as intentional foreground density, the near-grass a dark stage-edge band, which period plates do; but you flagged patch statistics before and I won't pretend the number is inside the envelope.

Alternates: s173 (dogs and walkers center-line, slightly muddier load shapes), s171 (clean but parade-regimented). s170/s172 mid-pack, s174 rejected on subject.

So: promote **s175** as-is, or one more micro-round holding this exact prompt but thinning the foreground grass language ("pale open grass, sparsely hatched") to chase an s175-class seed with corpus-clean texture? The bend has now appeared twice in ten seeds, so the composition isn't a unicorn — but it is a lottery. Your call.

## ASSISTANT

The two finished studies frame the decision well:

**s190** is the one that stopped me. The genre is immaculate — oval vignette with stipple ground fading to paper, seated three-quarter pose with hands folded, the plain chair, the dark dress and small collar, center-parted hair drawn smooth — and the face carries exactly what 03:3 describes: composed *in the way that daguerreotypes require*, gravity that reads as held stillness rather than sorrow. Mid-twenties, fine-featured, believably "like her father" in the sense of a face with sternness in its bones. If Catherine gets a face, this is a serious candidate for it.

**s191** runs younger and rounder, eyes wide enough to read startled rather than grave — closer to a girl than to a woman three hard years into a homestead marriage. Useful as a contrast study; I wouldn't promote it.

Two more seeds are re-rolling to fill the pool. But the real question is the one you set the studies up to answer, and it's now concrete: the plates can give the book's absent center a face of period-perfect provenance — the engraver copying the object Farrell carries. The cost is that every reader's Catherine collapses into one face, and she stops being the woman glimpsed through letters and a draft that breaks off mid-sentence. The gain is a frontispiece-class image whose stillness does exactly what the daguerreotype does to Farrell at the window. I can argue either side; this one is genuinely yours.
