## ASSISTANT

**The generation phase is closed** (e04623b, 49a59f3). Sixteen plates in art/, every unshelved catalog scene landed:

**Journal-side plates**: cover, first-sighting, speaking-to-her, offering-stake, morrow-hollow, homestead-alive, village-passing, finale-fifty-yards. **Farrell-side cuts**: fort-kearny, trading-post, homestead, homestead-interior, graves, tracks-north, morrow-witnessed, journal-found.

The last plate earned a good ending: after nine straight inversions, v4's double fix (starve the described subject, flex to landscape) broke the pattern in all three seeds — the banked rule is that in two-subject scenes you describe the *space* and let layout carry the figures — and the winner caught the calf with her head raised toward Morrow, which is JE II's "She raised her head and looked at me" arriving unprompted.

What remains is assembly, now scoped in planning: placement and sequencing, the fold-out call (s181 panorama vs s175 portrait — a bindery decision), the ferry-bridge gap check under your rule, the corner-crop pass, and the homestead texture revisit if vignettes print large. The shelved scenes stand as decisions, not gaps: three by plate policy, Catherine by your ruling that the absent center stays absent.

The engine's full history — five prompt generations for the village alone, two trained LoRAs, three measurement tools (rhyme, register, zoom-gate battery), and five text enrichments where the coherence loop pushed research back into canon — is all in `imagegen/findings.md`. It's been a remarkable stretch of work; the book has its pictures.

---

## USER

A lot of good work, but we can't claim mission accomplished quite yet.

There are a few issues in 3 of these last 4 images, but all of them may be fixable.

In homestead-interior.png, the coathanger appears anachronistic (maybe it's actually accurate, but we'll want research to back this up) and Catherine's hung up garment similarly looks contemporary.

For homestead-alive.png, the design simply doesn't match homestead.png. I don't have a strong preference for which, if either, version we should deem canonical.

For tracks-north.png, we have a pair of furrows with no discernable individual steps. Is this the intent?

The strongest is speaking-to-her.png, for which I have the minor quibble that the calf and the sun are white rather than the cream background. (And I think the image is in a traditional attractor of devotional imagery where the model's prior is quite strong that the calf should be a lamb, but I think you've managed to dodge this quite well.)

---

## ASSISTANT

Right — three real fixes and a register subtlety. My reads and moves:

1. **homestead-interior**: if a shaped hanger is visible, it's almost certainly anachronistic (wire hangers are 1869+; frontier practice is pegs and nails, garments hung by their shoulders or loops), and an 1850s dress should read full-skirted with a fitted bodice, not a contemporary silhouette. Commissioning quick research to pin the facts before the re-roll.
2. **homestead-alive vs homestead**: I'll make the call you left open — **s210's construction becomes canonical**, because the *dead* plate was always the compromised one (its promotion carried a standing "walls read lumpy-cobble at zoom" caveat) while the alive round proved the model can render a textbook coursed soddy. So the dead homestead re-rolls to match, which settles its old caveat and the pair in one stroke.
3. **tracks-north**: not the intent — the prompt asked for broken track-marks, and at plate scale they fused into furrows, which reads drag-marks. v4 asserts footfall spacing: separate prints with clean snow between.
4. **speaking-to-her**: sharp catch, and it's mechanically fixable without re-rolling — a print can't be whiter than its paper, so a channel-clamp to the paper tone is legitimate physics, not retouching. I'll build it as a tool and audit all sixteen plates for the same violation while I'm at it. (And thank you re: the lamb attractor — the devotional pull was real; "woolly-headed" nearly fed it.)

## ASSISTANT

All four fixes are in motion:

1. **speaking-to-her: fixed and re-promoted** (8ae7ac4). The paper-clamp tool now exists (`paper_clamp.py`), and the corrected estimator found your quibble is actually universal at low levels — every plate carries 0.5–10% neutral-white above its paper tone. I clamped only the flagged plate; the whole-corpus clamp is a one-command batch pass I'd rather you eyeball as part of assembly than have me apply silently to fifteen approved images. On this plate the effect is genuinely better than a fix: the sun became a disc of *reserved paper* against the ruled sky, which is how period engravings actually render suns.
2. **homestead-interior v3 staged** (e892d1d): the research confirmed your instinct — no hangers exist before 1869 (wire shoulder hangers are 1903); garments hang from pegs by collar loops, bunched at a single point in a long crumpled cone with *no shoulder shape*, and the 1850s dress cue is the waist seam with dense pleating and limp full-skirt yardage. All of it is now in the prompt and negatives.
3. **homestead (dead) v2 and tracks-north v4 are generating now** — the dead plate re-rolls to match s210's canonical soddy (settling its old lumpy-cobble caveat too), and the tracks get footfall-spaced separate prints with furrow/rut negatives.

Interior v3 runs as soon as the current sweep clears the GPU. Verdicts on all three re-rolls in one report when they're gated.

Standing mode: DK gives director-style taste notes on concrete artifacts; I execute autonomously with cheap veto via git. This session's explicit requests, in order:

   - Full personal read of the entire draft (no sub-agents) — done; six findings; DK ruled: (1) age seam easy fix; (2) "ship through water" blessed as journal-bleed device (b) + extend to (c) with more borrowed language; (3) my call on witness conflation; (4) JE VII direct observation saved + asymmetry dramatized ("I don't think Morrow skulks exactly"; "careful about the triad" in my draft); drop/nudge "hidden" toward occlusion; (5) Gunderson revised as I see fit but Elkhorn vetoed ("accidental rhyming with Elkhorn Tavern / Pea Ridge") — change destination AND background; (6) brief Farrell narration of the Niobrara crossing approved.
   - Village LoRA: after v1, "do a few more epochs on training with a few new reference images worked in... not getting its physicality quite right" — continuation training done. Validation harness firmed up while training (GPU contention avoided).
   - Catalog gap analysis; DK approved gap-fill scenes 1,2,3,5 (homestead-interior, homestead-alive, speaking-to-her, tracks-north); ferry-bridge deferred to assembly-time gap check; Catherine daguerreotype studies → after seeing them, "leaning against including Catherine... regardless of quality" → shelved by decision; googly-eye trick banked.
   - Village production iterations: fewer foreground riders, exploit ridgeline strength, resting-travois anchor idea; text pass over travois/village logistics FIRST ("might also bounce back to changes in our prompt/catalog") — JE VI enriched. Register drift caught by DK ("would show up in pixel and 3x3 patch statistics") → register.py. s175 promoted; s170 named alternate; fold-out back in play (validated, s181 candidate); "fine on texture for this batch... don't need to do anything proactively around the numbers."
   - Four catches on the last images: interior coathanger/contemporary dress (research first), alive/dead homestead mismatch (no strong preference which is canonical — I ruled alive-s210 canonical), tracks-north furrows ("no discernible individual steps. Is this the intent?" — no), speaking-to-her whites vs cream (praised my lamb-attractor dodge). All fixed. "Good fix on speaking-to-her... we can consider applying it more broadly" → batch clamp applied. "homestead.png has acquired a moat" → fixed.

   - LoRAs deployed (imagegen/loras/, diffusers format via convert_lora.py --target other): "morrow" (final, portraits), "morrow-ep12" (narrative scenes), "travois" = village_travois_v2e4_diffusers @1.0 (physicality continuation epoch 4 — wheels/harness persist through v1-ep12, only v1-final drops them; v2 ep4 = peak, v2-final regresses to detached rails), "travois-v1" kept. Register negative load-bearing for both LoRAs (grayscale-oil drift otherwise).
   - Village dataset: 14 curated PD art images (dataset/village/, repeats 10) + 8 structure references (dataset/village_structure/, repeats 5: Ewers BAE 159 diagrams fig18/19/26, Wissler fig56, 4 photos incl. NARA Stump Horn, LOC Blackfoot); captions name mechanism ("two poles crossed and lashed over the saddle at the withers, butt ends dragging"); photos captioned "black and white photograph" to bind register; raw sources gitignored (manifests carry URLs); training/village_raw/manifest.md + manifest-structure.md.
   - Measurement battery: rhyme.py (16×24 grayscale Pearson; hollow pair +0.562 @s122, bookends +0.484); register.py (mean/ink/paper/tex3/hf, z-scores vs art/ corpus; corpus paper 25%±12; homestead tex3 32.5/hf 95 = busy-content ceiling); paper_clamp.py (bright-band 75–95th percentile luminance paper estimate — margins useless, plates fill edge-to-edge; channelwise np.minimum clamp; batch-applied to all 16 plates, biggest movers village-passing 15.4%, journal-found/fort-kearny ~12%); zoom-gate (PIL crop + 2-3× resize, mandatory pre-promotion).
   - Banked prompt-craft rules: #1 defining attribute leads; #2 ≤3 compositional charges + zero-sum attention budget (compress validated details); #3 assert plains landform vs alpine prior; #4 anatomy morphology blocks; #5 period vantage (whole form, level, sketchable/respectful distance; anti-close battery: "close-up, foreshortening, low angle, dramatic perspective, wide-angle, looming"); every charge needs its STATE asserted ("a fire" buys sticks, not flame); positive absence assertion beats negatives (bare flagstaff, "their makers long gone"); DISTANCE LEADS for distant subjects (leading with subject mechanics pulls them near); in two-subject scenes describe the SPACE and starve the subjects (rich description = foreground magnet; 9/9 inversions until fixed); per-print/per-item detail invites zoom-in (trail-only description fixed tracks); weight is NOT the tonal lever, language is (0.8 probe was inkier than 1.0); the model will not break a chair (~12 seeds; accepted); multi-room hallucination is the interior leak mode; googly-eye fix = assert eye quality positively ("gaze settled and slightly lowered, eyelids heavy") + negatives "wide eyes, staring, round eyes, bulging eyes"; the engraver's distance compression is NECESSARY (at the text's honest two miles the subject dissolves — s174 lesson).

   - village_sweep.py = generic single-load sweep tool: SWEEPS dict (scene → seeds) edited via `python - <<'EOF'` re.sub; reads catalog.toml + styles.toml verbatim (production fidelity); skip-if-exists; lora-optional guard with `lora_label`; adapter zeroing for no-LoRA scenes.

DNI: killings (traces only: graves, scrubbed stain, broken chair), Morrow's death (traces), ending resolution, Lakota interiority (distance enforced, no faces). Format doctrine: journal-side portrait plates, Farrell-side landscape cuts — FLEXED twice (homestead-alive, speaking-to-her: lateral/valley subjects are intrinsically horizontal). ## ASSISTANT

Done and committed. Here's where things stand:

**The full read.** All 21 files, cover to the kneeling close. The manuscript is settled — every edit from this session reads naturally in place: Brandt bound for the Wood River, the Niobrara crossing with Farrell sounding the ice "the way I should have done at the creek weeks before," the tree-or-rock inversion in JE VII, the enriched lodgepole passage in JE VI ("There is nothing in my life that needs dragging"), and "a great and quiet peace" landing once at Morrow's resolve and once at Farrell's kneeling. No new continuity findings; the art corpus matches the text everywhere I checked it against the plates.

**The art review.** All sixteen plates confirmed in place, clamped, coherent as a set. The finale gave me the match target plainly: white head and face, dark horns, dark nose, dark eyes, blood dark at the chest.

**The cover audit (the interrupted work).** All six candidates passed the white-head gate — the head-fix language worked on every seed, so the dark-headed incumbent is now strictly a prompt-era artifact. The interesting result was the register comparison you asked the split for:

- **v2's tone calibration worked exactly as written** — v2 candidates sit at z+0.4/+0.5 against the corpus versus legacy's z+1.2–1.6. But that turns out to be the wrong direction for this plate: the cover's natural tonal family is the *finale* (z+1.8, the brightest plate in the book — snow field plus white subject). Pulling toward the corpus mean pulls the cover away from its companion.
- **Promoted: legacy s78, clamped.** The shared seed preserved the incumbent's massing, stance, breath puff, and distant herd line — your weak preference for the incumbent's look survives intact; only the head changed. Dark horns match the finale at zoom, corners are clean, and the smooth pale sky keeps the title field.
- **Named alternate: v2 s79** — the richer worked-sky plate, and the best horns of the whole pass (16:17's "curved and thickened with age" visibly landed). It's one command away if you'd rather the cover carry the fuller engraving.

Rule banked in findings: register calibration targets the plate's *family*, not the corpus mean. With this, the generation-phase quibble queue is fully closed — what remains is the assembly phase: plate placement, the fold-out call (s181 vs s175), the ferry-bridge gap check, and the corner-crop pass.

## USER

Okay, we're about halfway through my imagination for this revision project, although I expect plate creation will be by far the lengthiest and most grinding part.

I see various things you can do, in no assumed order:
- plate placement, and more broadly assembly of the plates, chapters, etc. into a coherent built artifact
- figuring out corner cropping, remaining gibberish removal, and any other image level post-processing
- seeing where "gaps" in the images exist or may be perceived, and filling these in with new plates
- general Opus 4.5 style remediation ("perhaps" density is perhaps still too high, for example)
- enriching the story with events, incidents, and description (we may want to effectively "dilute" the appearance of the white buffalo and the man following it in the first half of Farrell's story, although I think anything red herring shaped is against the spirit of what we're doing)
- do additional research on material culture details you encounter; this has had high payoff for realism and interest
- firm up the timelines, both chronologically and geographically, for all the characters
- run blind sub-agents across various truncations of the text and get their reactions and expectations; where are they surprised? are the subversions of expectations effective or capricious?
- throw specific flavors of editor persona agents at the text and see what they think
- do the exercise of creating high school literature class rubrics or reading club questions for the text; try working the exercises and answering the questions
- write pitch or submission letters for the text; respond to these positively and negatively

And anything else in this general spirit is fair game.

A lot of this might be carried out asynchronously/hogwild, especially if you accumulate a stack or collection of issues to address rather than making edits as you see issues. But minor corrections or notes/ files can probably go in immediately.

<summary>Agent "Period-accuracy audit" completed</summary>
<result># Historical Accuracy Audit — *White Buffalo* (drafts/00–20)

Setting audited against: frame Nov 1858 – Jan 1859; journal Apr 1856 – Jan 1859. Severity scale: **breaks-credibility** &gt; **specialist-notices** &gt; **pedantic**.

---

## I. Railroads, Infrastructure, Travel

**1. Oswego–Syracuse–Rome–Watertown routing — 01-chapter-one.md:37; 03-chapter-two.md:51, 53**
Claim: rail from Oswego via Syracuse and Rome to Watertown; "the railroad had come to this part of the country seven years earlier"; return trip "Watertown to Buffalo, changing at Rome."
Doubt: almost none — this is *correct*. Oswego &amp; Syracuse RR (1848), Syracuse–Rome on the New York Central, Watertown &amp; Rome RR reached Watertown in 1851 — exactly seven years before 1858. There was no direct Oswego–Watertown lakeshore line until 1866, so the dogleg through Rome is the only right answer.
Severity: none (see "Rights" list). Source if you want it: Poor's *History of the Railroads and Canals of the United States* (1860); RW&amp;O corporate histories.

**2. One-day Watertown→Cleveland — 03:53, 69**
Claim: reads as if Farrell boards at Watertown and "the train pulled into Cleveland as the sun was setting" the same day. That's ~400 rail miles with two-plus changes (Rome, Buffalo) at 1858 average speeds (20–25 mph incl. stops), against a 4:45 pm November sunset. Almost certainly a two-day trip with an overnight in Buffalo.
Severity: pedantic (the text never explicitly says one day). Source: 1858 *Appleton's Railway Guide* timetables.

**3. "Towns that had been weeks apart by coach were now a day apart" — 03:51**
Doubt: hyperbole. Watertown-country towns were days apart by coach, not weeks. A native of the region wouldn't say this.
Severity: pedantic.

**4. Cleveland gaslights — 03:69**
Claim: gaslit main streets, Cleveland, Nov 1858. Cleveland Gas Light &amp; Coke Co. was supplying street gas from 1849–50. Fine.
Severity: none. Source: Cleveland city annals (WPA), Kennedy's *History of Cleveland*.

**5. Buying a horse in Chicago and riding six days to the Mississippi — 03:89–115; 05-chapter-three.md:19**
Claim: "The railroad did not extend much past the Mississippi, not yet" — true and nicely put (Mississippi &amp; Missouri RR ended at Iowa City; Hannibal &amp; St. Joseph not through until Feb 1859). But the Chicago &amp; Rock Island had run Chicago–Rock Island since 1854, and the M&amp;M ran Davenport–Iowa City. A man racing winter buys his horse at Iowa City (or Davenport) and saves ten days and the horse's condition. Riding out of Chicago is what a novelist does, not what Farrell would do.
Severity: **specialist-notices**. Settle with: 1858 railway guides; Riegel, *The Story of the Western Railroads*.

**6. The Rock Island bridge and the *Effie Afton* — 05:27–29**
Claim: "the first ever thrown across the river," draw open for a steamer, "Steamboat took a span out of her two years back. She'll burn again, God willing."
Doubt: essentially none — bridge opened April 1856, *Effie Afton* struck and burned a span May 6, 1856 ("two years back" ✓), first bridge across the Mississippi anywhere ✓, and the ferryman's malice is documented river-interest attitude (boats whistled in celebration at the fire; cf. *Hurd v. Rock Island Bridge Co.*, Lincoln for the defense, 1857). One quibble: a steamer coming down the draw in very late November/early December is at the ragged end of the upper-river navigation season; ice usually closed Rock Island by early December.
Severity: pedantic (the steamer). Source: Effie Afton trial record; upper-Mississippi navigation-closing tables in Corps of Engineers reports.

**7. The ferry worked by ropes — 05:23–25**
Claim: ferryman "and his crew worked the ropes" across the Mississippi. A rope/cable ferry on the main navigation channel at Rock Island — with steamboats passing — is doubtful; the Davenport–Rock Island crossing used steam ferryboats by the mid-1850s. A sweep/current ferry is conceivable but the crossing described (a dozen horses, wagon, mules) says steam ferry.
Severity: **specialist-notices**. Source: Davenport/Rock Island county histories, ferry franchise records.

**8. The Missouri crossing that never happens — 05:95–133**
Claim: Farrell reaches Council Bluffs (east bank), then simply "rode out of town, following the trail that led along the Platte." The Platte road from Council Bluffs requires ferrying the Missouri to the Omaha side — a significant, sometimes dangerous December operation (ice running). It's silently skipped. Likewise Fort Kearny sits on the *south* bank of the Platte; a traveler on the Council Bluffs (north-bank) road must cross the braided Platte to reach the sutler (07-chapter-four.md:89), and again to "turn north" after riding west from the fort (07:137). Three river crossings, none acknowledged.
Severity: **specialist-notices**. Source: emigrant guides (Horn, Child); Fort Kearny post returns; Mattes, *The Great Platte River Road*.

**9. Council Bluffs "the bluffs that gave the place its name" — 05:95**
The name derives from Lewis and Clark's 1804 *council* with the Otoe (held at a bluff well north of the town), not merely the bluffs. A local would know the story.
Severity: pedantic.

**10. Pikes Peak silence — 07:107**
Claim: at Fort Kearny, "a rumor about a gold strike somewhere in the mountains that no one quite believed." In Nov–Dec 1858 the Missouri River towns — Council Bluffs and Omaha above all — were in full Cherry Creek/Pikes Peak fever; news arrived Aug–Sept 1858 and outfitting mania was underway. Council Bluffs saloon talk (05:103–107) with *no* gold talk, and the fort reducing it to one disbelieved rumor, understates the single loudest topic of that exact season and place.
Severity: **specialist-notices**. Source: Hafen, *Pike's Peak Gold Rush Guidebooks of 1859*; Omaha/CB newspapers Sept–Dec 1858.

---

## II. Military History

**11. Farrell at Monterrey *and* Chapultepec — 03:63; 05:55; 17-chapter-nine.md:27, 57–69**
Claim: Farrell fought in the Monterrey street fighting (Bishop's Palace, 17:27) and later at Chapultepec/Mexico City.
Verdict: workable — Worth's division stormed the Bishop's Palace (Sept 22, 1846) and its regular regiments transferred to Scott and were at Chapultepec (Sept 13, 1847). So a *regular* Farrell is fine. But then 05:53's "a man I had watched lead his volunteers into the streets of Monterrey" (Davis) is a problem: Davis's Mississippians assaulted La Tenería on the *east* end of the city while Worth's division was two miles away on the *west*, with the city between. A Bishop's Palace man did not watch Davis lead his regiment in. Also, "I had seen them at Monterrey… I had seen them at Chapultepec" (05:55) reads loosely as including Davis and Lee — Davis left Mexico in mid-1847 and was never at Chapultepec; Lee was never at Monterrey.
Severity: **specialist-notices** (the Davis sighting); pedantic (the "them"). Source: Bauer, *The Mexican War*; regimental positions in Smith, *The War with Mexico*.

**12. Morrow's record: Vicksburg, 1st Mississippi, Monterrey, Buena Vista — 18-journal-entry-ix.md:19–39; 06-journal-entry-iii.md:29–37**
Verdict: largely excellent. The 1st Mississippi Rifles organized at Vicksburg in June 1846 ("I was at Vicksburg when the war came" ✓), fought in the Monterrey street assaults ✓ and at Buena Vista ✓; the ~4,000 vs. Santa Anna's claimed 20,000 ✓ (effectives nearer 15,000, but 20,000 is what a veteran would say); Taylor's commendation ✓; lancers at Buena Vista ✓ (the famous repulse of Torrejón's cavalry). Doubts:
- **"Colonel Davis was wounded and carried off the field" (18:39):** Davis was shot in the foot and famously *refused to leave the field*, staying in the saddle until the fighting ended. This is the sort of detail a Mississippi Rifleman would have retold for the rest of his life, correctly. Severity: **specialist-notices**. Source: Davis biographies (W.C. Davis, Cooper); Rifles' regimental accounts.
- "Cavalry first, then infantry, wave after wave" (18:33): on the plateau the sequencing was more infantry-led; forgivable as memory blur. Pedantic.
- "the war ended and they sent me home" (04-journal-entry-ii.md:43): the 1st Mississippi's twelve-month enlistment expired and it mustered out at New Orleans in June 1847 — seven months *before* the war ended. Pedantic, but a veteran would say "our time was up."
- "I have waited in the trenches before an assault" (02-journal-entry-i.md:47): nothing in Morrow's record (Monterrey, Buena Vista) involved trench-waiting before an assault; that's Veracruz siege imagery, which belongs to Scott's men. Pedantic.

**13. Kill-count arithmetic — 04:41 vs. 10-journal-entry-v.md:87 vs. 18:35**
Claim: "I killed seventeen men with certainty" (July 1856) → "the man who killed seventeen men at Monterrey and Buena Vista" (Aug 1857) → at Buena Vista alone "Dozens, certainly. Perhaps more" (Jan 1859). These can't all be true, and both use "certainty." Separately, the precise personal tally is a modern sniper-memoir convention; a black-powder infantryman firing volleys into smoke could rarely *know* seventeen. (Journal II half-earns it: "some of them I did not see at all.")
Severity: **specialist-notices** (the internal contradiction), pedantic (the bookkeeping). Fix is internal consistency, no source needed.

**14. Chapultepec: cadets, ladders — 17:57–69**
Claim: ladders "too short," ropes, then "a room full of… cadets… They fought, and we killed them. All of them. Every one."
Doubt: the ladder trouble at Chapultepec was ladders arriving *late* (storming parties pinned under the walls), not too short; and of the ~47 cadets remaining in the Colegio Militar, six died (Los Niños Héroes) — most were captured. "We killed them, all of them, in heaps" is the mythologized version. The dream-frame gives you cover — guilt inflates — but a Mexican-War specialist will read it as the legend, not the event.
Severity: **specialist-notices**, mitigated by the dream framing. Source: Bauer; Mexican accounts of the Colegio Militar's defense.

**15. Davis in the Senate, Taylor dead, Lee in Texas — 03:55; 05:53**
Davis: Senator from Mississippi again from March 1857, so a fall-1858 speech in the papers ✓ (nit: his famous fall-1858 speeches, in Maine and Boston, were conspicuously *conciliatory*; a "speech on the Southern question" reads more like his Senate performances — pedantic). Taylor "president for a year and then gone": 16 months; fine as speech. Lee "now held a cavalry command in Texas": Lee was Lt. Col., 2nd Cavalry (Texas), but had been on leave at Arlington since Oct 1857 settling the Custis estate. A newspaper reader in 1858 wouldn't know that; technically he still "held" the command.
Severity: pedantic (all three). Source: Davis papers; Freeman's *R. E. Lee*, vol. 1.

**16. Harney and Blue Water — 07:97, 157**
Claim: sutler: "Sioux been restless since Blue Water" (Sept 3, 1855); Creighton: "Harney did his work on the Blue Water, and they keep wide of us now."
Verdict: the correction is *right* — post-Harney and post-Fort Pierre council (1856), the Lakota largely avoided the road. Having the lazy version voiced and then corrected is exactly how it would have sounded.
Severity: none — rights list.

**17. Spirit Lake — 05:69–77**
Claim: Iowa tavern, Nov/Dec 1858: "Some Indian band raiding settlers near Spirit Lake… Santee, they say… That was last year. They caught the ones who did it. / They caught some of them. The rest are still out there."
Verdict: massacre was March 1857 ("last year" ✓); Inkpaduta's Wahpekute are correctly "Santee"; and "caught some of them" is accurate — Inkpaduta was never taken (a son, Roaring Cloud, was killed July 1857). "Killed a whole family" undersells ~35–40 dead across several cabins, but that's tavern compression. The first speaker presenting it as fresh news is realistic garbling, and the text corrects him.
Severity: none to pedantic. Source: Abbie Gardner-Sharp's captivity narrative; Iowa official reports 1857.

**18. Osawatomie mill burned "last month" — 05:35–49**
Doubt: by Nov 1858 Kansas violence had largely subsided after Marais des Cygnes (May 1858) and the fall elections; a fresh double murder-and-burning near Osawatomie in Oct/Nov 1858 is invented but not impossible (the border stayed ragged into 1859 — cf. Brown's Dec 1858 Missouri raid). "Whole country's going to hell" talk on a ferry ✓.
Severity: pedantic. Source: Kansas territorial press, fall 1858.

---

## III. Money and Prices

**19. $200 fee, half down, expenses — 01:149–155**
Verdict: plausible. A laborer earned ~$1–1.50/day; $200 for months of winter work with a possible killing attached is credible, and "large… not ruinous" for a Great Lakes merchant reads right. One quibble: "the bills were crisp" — a year after the Panic of 1857, an experienced frontier operator taking Oswego banknotes at face, to spend in Chicago and beyond where Eastern paper passed at a discount, is trusting; gold would be the professional's choice.
Severity: pedantic. Source: banknote reporter/counterfeit detectors of 1858; Berry's wage-price series.

**20. Fifty cents a night, no questions — 03:5**
Doubt: rural boarding ran $2–3/week *with board*; 50¢/night room-only in a backwoods lumber town is steep (city hotels were $1–2/day American plan). Defensible as the no-questions premium the narrator himself notes.
Severity: pedantic.

**21. Whiskey by the bottle — 03:45, 113; 07:91; 09-chapter-five.md:43–47**
Doubt: in 1858 whiskey moved in barrels and was decanted into the buyer's jug, flask, or bottle; branded, sealed retail bottles are essentially postbellum. Farrell repeatedly "buys three bottles" like a man at a package store. The trading post's "unlabeled, cloudy" bottle is right; the pattern elsewhere reads modern retail. The price gradient (Cleveland &lt; Kearny sutler ≪ trading post; "a price that would have bought a case in Cleveland") is exactly right.
Severity: **specialist-notices** (the bottle convention); the pricing is a right. Source: distilling/retail trade histories; sutler ledgers (Fort Kearny sutler records survive).

---

## IV. The Mail

**22. Creighton, dispatches Kearny–Laramie in winter — 07:141–183**
Verdict: plausible. Military expresses ran between the posts year-round, and the 1858 Hockaday mail contract (St. Joseph–Salt Lake via Kearny and Laramie) put riders on that road even in winter. A lone rider camped off the trail with hot coffee is credible; such men were famous for it.
Severity: none. Source: post returns Fort Kearny/Laramie; Hafen, *The Overland Mail*.
One internal nit: Creighton saw the pair "two weeks ago, crossing the Loup Fork" (mid-Dec), yet by Jan 1 the herd is settled "north of the river [Niobrara]" (16-journal-entry-viii.md:73–77) — ~100 miles *north*, in deep snow, in a fortnight, against the southward winter drift the journal itself describes (12-journal-entry-vi.md:55; 16:5). See item 28.

---

## V. Frontier Practice and Settlement

**23. A farm homestead in the Niobrara/sand hills country, 1856–58 — 09:67; 10:67; 05:105; passim**
Claim: George and Catherine Hardin farm (plowed field, garden, well, root cellar) at a site reached by riding ~6 days north of the Platte to a trading post, then 2 days up a river, then 3–4 days up a creek — i.e., deep in the sand hills, on unceded Lakota land under the 1851 treaty, days beyond the last white face, with no land office, no survey, no market, and no neighbors.
Doubt: this is the book's largest liberty. Actual 1856–58 Nebraska settlement hugged the Missouri and the lower Platte; the middle Niobrara and sand hills were not farmed until the 1870s–80s, and G. K. Warren — who crossed exactly this country in 1855 and 1857 — reported it unfit for agriculture and firmly Sioux. Pre-Homestead settlers were squatters anticipating preemption; preemption presumes eventual survey and sale, unimaginable there. Sand hills soil will not hold a plowed field. A single couple would also have been the *only* such settlers, which sits oddly with Aldridge's "there are many homesteads" (01:97) framing. The isolation is the story, so I don't ask you to move them — but a specialist will notice, and the trader's matter-of-fact "yeah, I know them" normalizes something that was, in fact, extraordinary to the point of suicidal.
Severity: **breaks-credibility** for a specialist as written; recoverable if the text itself marks the Hardins as freakishly far beyond the line (Hardin as a man "with more ambition than sense" gestures at it).
Source: G. K. Warren, *Preliminary Report of Explorations in Nebraska and Dakota* (1858); Nebraska GLO tract books; Olson, *History of Nebraska*.

**24. Monthly letters from that homestead — 01:93**
Claim: Catherine "wrote to me regularly—every month, sometimes more often" for nearly three years. From beyond the last trading post — itself with no post office — letters travel only when someone rides out; the Hardins visited the post "a few times, when they first settled" (09:71). Monthly *receipt* in Oswego from that location is not physically arrangeable. The "past year they came a little slower" helps but doesn't cure it.
Severity: **specialist-notices**. Source: territorial post-office registers (nearest offices: Omaha, Fontanelle, Columbus, Fort Kearny).

**25. The trading post itself — 09:31–47**
Claim: freelance log-and-sod post at a creek junction between the Platte and the Niobrara. Off-trail posts existed for the Sioux robe trade (Bordeaux et al.), but they clustered near the Laramie road and the White River agencies, and Indian-country traders needed licenses. One lone post with no visible trade traffic, subsisting on… the Hardins? Marginal but permissible.
Severity: pedantic. Source: licensed-trader records, Upper Platte Agency.

**26. Sod construction, wells, root cellar, Fort Kearny fabric — 09:31; 11-chapter-six.md:53, 103; 07:89**
Verdict: all good. Cut-sod blocks, cottonwood-log walls banked with sod, pole-and-turf roofs, packed-earth floors, hillside root cellar, well with pulley frame — right for the place and decade. Fort Kearny as an *unstockaded* cluster of sod/adobe and frame buildings around a parade ground with young planted cottonwoods is exactly right and most novelists get it wrong.
Severity: none — rights list.

**27. The Brandts, Wood River, December — 07:45–85**
Verdict: German emigrants bound for the Wood River in Dec 1858 is defensible — the Wood River settlements (near Fort Kearny) began drawing Germans in 1857–58 — and the text correctly treats December travel as folly. Nit: a lone family wagon without a spare axle slung underneath was a rarity among prepared emigrants; the scene works because they're portrayed as unprepared.
Severity: pedantic. Source: Buffalo County/Hall County pioneer histories.

---

## VI. Buffalo

**28. Herd numbers, range, and winter movement — 02:13, 21; 12:55; 16:73–77; 07:175**
Claims: "buffalo so numerous that a man cannot walk a mile without seeing them" (1856, sand hills); winter band of ~200; herds drifting south for winter; herd "north of the river" in valley shelter, Jan 1859.
Doubts: bison persisted in the Niobrara/sand hills country into the 1870s–80s, so presence ✓; density "every mile" in 1856 is generous but not absurd there. Winter band of ~200 rather than horizon-blackening millions is *right* and refreshing. But the December–January movement is internally against the grain: the book states the southward winter drift twice, then has the herd end up north of the Niobrara in January, having been on the Loup in mid-December (see item 22).
Severity: **specialist-notices** (the north-in-January drift as internal inconsistency); pedantic (density). Source: Warren's route journals note buffalo distribution 1855/57; Hornaday, *Extermination of the American Bison*.

**29. "Bulls ranging out on the edges" — 02:21**
The bulls-as-perimeter-guards image is folklore; mixed herds don't post bulls on the flanks, and mature bulls run separate most of the year. The book itself gets the spring bull dispersal right later (10:13), which makes the April "bulls on the edges" stand out.
Severity: pedantic.

**30. "Pawing through the snow" — 08-journal-entry-iv.md:73**
Bison clear snow with the head — the great muzzle-sweep is their signature winter behavior and the reason they out-winter cattle. Horses paw; buffalo plow. A one-word fix a bison person will bless you for.
Severity: **specialist-notices** (it's *the* diagnostic winter behavior). Source: Hornaday; McHugh, *The Time of the Buffalo*.

**31. Growth: autumn calf to "taller than any cow" in 3.3 years — 02:43; 16:17; 14-journal-entry-vii.md:5**
A cow reaches full frame at 4–6 years; ~1,000 lb at 2.5–3 is heavy; "taller than any cow in the herd" at three is not natural. The text knows it and hedges twice ("An autumn calf, born when no calf is born," 02:43; "no cow reaches her size in three summers, but she was born out of season and has been early in everything," 16:17). Autumn births are rare-but-real. Also 02:57 "The bulls are enormous, a thousand pounds or more" — true only via "or more"; bulls run 1,600–2,000 lb.
Severity: pedantic, because deliberately marked as uncanny. If the white cow is meant to be supernatural, this is license spent correctly.

**32. A white buffalo at all — passim**
Leucistic/albino bison are documented (Big Medicine, b. 1933; several 19th-c. hides attested); odds folklore says ~1 in 10 million. Acceptable premise. The coat "kept clean by something I do not understand" (04:7) is announced as mystery, fine. Note that a true albino would be weak-eyed and sun-blistered; leucistic is the survivable variant — the text wisely never specifies.
Severity: none.

**33. Habituation: a herd tolerating a following man for three years — 04:9; 02:21**
Doubt: 1850s wild bison were acutely wary of upright men — that's why hunters crawled under wolf skins (Catlin). A herd accepting a pedestrian at 100–200 yards, permanently, is the mystical premise doing heavy lifting. The book acknowledges the strangeness through the trapper/trader reactions, which is the right instinct.
Severity: **specialist-notices**, accepted as premise.

**34. Morrow's survival: one blanket, no gun, no robe, three plains winters — 06:21; 08 passim; 19-chapter-ten.md:21**
Doubt: a man scavenging wolf-kill and sleeping under one blanket in −30° cold does not see March, once, let alone three times — and the killing detail is that he never takes a buffalo robe from the carcasses he butchers. Any plains survivor would be wearing hides by December of year one; robes are lying frozen around him. Also: where does the Jan 1859 candle come from (18:7; 19:21), three years after an inventory of "a blanket, a knife, flint and steel, this journal and a pencil" (06:21)? And one pencil stub can't write three years of entries — actually the text says "worn down short," which nearly covers it.
Severity: **specialist-notices** (robe omission; candle); the raw-frozen-meat scavenging, cottonwood-hollow sheltering, and the flint-and-steel-vs-Farrell's-matches contrast are all good. Source: winter survival passages in Larpenteur, Denig, fur-trade journals.

**35. Wolves — 02:13; 05:135 (coyotes)**
Plains gray wolves following herds and leaving scavenge ✓, abundant 1856–58 ✓. Nit: Farrell hears coyotes "for the first time" three-plus weeks into the plains (09:135) — he'd have heard them nightly since Council Bluffs.
Severity: pedantic.

---

## VII. Lakota

**36. White buffalo reverence — 02:29; 18:47; and the ending, 20-chapter-eleven.md:59–69**
Claims: "To kill one is the greatest of sins. To see one is a blessing or a curse" (trader talk); White Buffalo Calf Woman brought "their pipe, their rituals" ✓ (correct, and correctly vague at second hand); the band receives and escorts the living white cow away at the end.
Doubt: the ethnographic record runs the other way on killing: among Lakota and neighboring peoples a white buffalo was ordinarily *taken* — by a chosen man, with rites, the hide consecrated, sometimes given whole to the sun — and a white robe was the most sacred object a band could hold. Wanton killing was the sin; ceremonial killing was the honor. So "greatest of sins to kill one" is a white man's plausible garble (and it's voiced as trader hearsay through Morrow — that's your cover), but the closing image of riders letting the cow "fall in among them" and herding her off alive is romantic rather than documented — and a live buffalo does not fall in with mounted men like a led horse. The text hedges it as possible fever-vision (20:63 "Or another vision—another gift from the fever"), which is the load-bearing hedge; keep it.
Severity: **specialist-notices** overall; would be breaks-credibility without the vision hedge. Source: Densmore, *Teton Sioux Music* (white buffalo ceremony); Walker, *Lakota Belief and Ritual*; Hassrick, *The Sioux*.

**37. "Mad men are sacred to them" — 02:11**
A frontier commonplace, and the narrator flags it with "perhaps… or they simply do not care." Plains treatment of the deranged was closer to wary avoidance of someone possibly spirit-touched than "sacred," but non-interference with a harmless solitary is right, and doubly right post-Harney (bands actively avoiding incident, 1856–58). Three years of tolerance for a white man shadowing Ptesáŋwiŋ's animal is generous but arguable — the watching-without-approaching (08:49–55; 16:47–55) is the credible behavior.
Severity: pedantic as voiced (hedged, in-character).

**38. October village movement — 12:59**
Claim: village moving south in October: travois, dogs, lodgepoles dragging, spare-horse herd behind, the line bending wide around a lone stranger, grass combed flat by the poles.
Verdict: excellent — the fall shift to sheltered wooded-valley winter camps, the pole-drag transport, the loose horse herd, and above all the *avoidance arc* are all right. Direction south ✓ (toward White River-country winter grounds, direction varies but south is fine).
Severity: none — rights list. Source: Denig, *Five Indian Tribes of the Upper Missouri*; Hassrick.

**39. The offering stake — 08:61**
Peeled stake, red cloth strip, facing the wintering valley: red is the sacred color and cloth offerings tied to poles/trees are thoroughly documented Lakota practice. Quietly one of the best details in the book.
Severity: none — rights list. Source: Densmore; Walker (waúŋyaŋpi offerings).

---

## VIII. Alcohol and Medicine

**40. Withdrawal course — 03:45; 09:9; 11:19–43; 13-chapter-seven.md:5–9, 101; 15-chapter-eight.md:5–9, 15–31, 63–77; through ch. 11**
Claims: tremor while still on rationed drinks → last whiskey finished → tremor/sweats/insomnia/nightmares → formed visual hallucinations → generalized seizure on the fourth day out → persistent organized hallucinations (silent soldiers, Catherine) for roughly two further weeks while tracking and riding.
Verdict, the good: relative withdrawal producing tremor *while still drinking small amounts* is clinically right; sweats-despite-cold, racing heart, insomnia sequence ✓; tonic rigidity then clonic convulsion then post-ictal prostration is a textbook seizure ✓; the folk description "the shaking and the fever and the visions… snakes crawling on the walls, insects covering the floor" (03:45; 11:23) is exactly the 19th-century picture of "the horrors" — zoopsia — and the book correctly never uses the clinical term (Sutton's *delirium tremens* dated 1813, but a laborer says "the horrors"). Rationing-by-the-swallow behavior ✓.
Doubts: (a) the seizure at ~4–5 days post-last-drink is at the outer edge (classic window 12–96 hours) — defensible given the taper; (b) more substantively, a *weeks-long* forced taper to two drinks a night should have blunted the withdrawal considerably; (c) the hallucinations are calm, coherent, personally meaningful, and persist ~two weeks in a man with otherwise clear sensorium who navigates, tracks, doctors a horse, and sounds river ice — true DT is 3–5 days of agitated, confused, autonomic-storm terror with real mortality (untreated, in a starving man, in a Nebraska January, likely fatal). What's depicted after the seizure is literary ghost-vision, or at best alcoholic hallucinosis, not DTs.
Severity: **specialist-notices** (b, c); pedantic (a). The blend with exhaustion/starvation/"the fever" (20:63) gives partial cover. Source: period asylum literature on mania a potu; any modern review of alcohol-withdrawal chronology.

---

## IX. Language and Concepts

**41. "You a lawman?" — 09:53** — "Lawman" in the sheriff/marshal sense is essentially a 20th-century Westernism; an 1858 trader says "You the law?" or "sheriff's man?" Severity: **specialist-notices** — this is the single most visible verbal anachronism in the book. Source: OED; DARE.

**42. "made something off the referral" — 03:27** — "Referral" in the commercial sense is late-19th/20th century. Severity: pedantic.

**43. "families relocating west" — 03:53** — "Relocating" reads mid-20th-century; "removing west" or "moving west" is the period idiom. Severity: pedantic.

**44. "evaluated him" — 01:7** — "Evaluate" in 1858 is a mathematician's word; for sizing a man up it reads modern. "Weighed him," "took his measure" (used correctly elsewhere, 03:103). Severity: pedantic.

**45. "come to resupply" — 05:97** — Verb exists early but the intransitive military usage is modern-flavored; "come in for supplies" is the period phrase. Severity: pedantic.

**46. "'Hello,' I called" — 11:61** — The frontier hail was "Halloo!" / "Hallo the house!"; "hello" as the default hail standardizes only after the telephone. Severity: pedantic.

**47. Trauma-psychology idiom — 13:89–91; 17:39; 04:39–47** — "men who lost some necessary part of themselves," "trying to fill the space that the war had hollowed out," "fill the emptiness." The *experience* depicted (Mexican-War moral injury) is historically real and the book mostly renders it in period-safe images (the weapon that "does not question its use" is excellent). But these particular formulations are recognizably post-Freud therapeutic English. Severity: **specialist-notices** (concept-level, low density; worth a pass).

**48. Cross-narrator echo — 16:97 and 20:33** — "a great and quiet peace" appears verbatim in Morrow's journal and then in Farrell's narration. Not an anachronism; flagging because a careful reader will either take it as deliberate resonance or as a seam. (Your own style profile flags exactly this pattern.) Severity: pedantic; decide on purpose.

**49. Things checked and passed:** "jumping-off point" (attested 1826) ✓; "the cars" ✓; "homestead" pre-1862 ✓ (the word long predates the Act); "coyotes" from a Mexican-War veteran ✓ (nice, even); "Loup Fork" ✓ (correct period name); "chophouse," "sutler," "daguerreotype" (an ~1855 portrait as a dag rather than ambrotype is fine, barely), "bleeding" Kansas (coined 1856) ✓; "Niobrara" (period usage split with "L'Eau Qui Court"/"Running Water" — a trader might use the older name; pedantic flavor opportunity, not an error).

---

## X. Internal Chronology (checked, mostly sound)

The Nov 1, 1858 → Jan 9, 1859 frame arithmetic *works* if Aldridge's tavern scene is very early November: 4 days Oswego→tavern town; 3 days settling; ~3 days rail; ~4 days Chicago; 6 days to the Mississippi; 11 to Council Bluffs (~Nov 28); 13 to Kearny (~Dec 13, matching "early December" on the Platte at 07:3); Creighton Dec 16; trading post ~Dec 22 ("December… more than half-gone," 11:9; "Christmas would come soon," 11:11 ✓); homestead Dec 26–27; two nights; north Dec 29; seizure ~Jan 2; camp found Jan 8; body Jan 9 — one day after Morrow's undated final entry (16:99 "January the Seventh… Tomorrow, I think"). Morrow's dates also check: born 1824 (18:17), 31 leaving Virginia ~1855 (12:47), "twelve years" since Buena Vista (18:27), calf six months old in April 1856 = autumn 1855 birth (02:43), settlers arrive early summer 1857 (10:41; 12:35 consistent), trader's "four months back" = Aug 1858 = the killings (09:117; 13:67). Two soft spots: **(a)** "I had been riding for nearly a month" (11:9) only works counted from Council Bluffs; **(b)** the tracks. Farrell follows buffalo-and-man tracks *in snow* away from the homestead (13:61–71), but Morrow left that neighborhood around August–September on bare ground; snow tracks there must be from a later, unexplained repass, and "disturbed within the past months" snow at the graves (13:57) has no author in the journal. Five-month-old sign readable under two snowfalls is also very generous tracking. Severity: **specialist-notices** (the track physics/timing); pedantic (the "month").

---

## What the Book Gets Right (a specialist would smile)

1. **The North Country rail geography** — Oswego–Syracuse–Rome–Watertown, "the railroad had come… seven years earlier" (W&amp;R, 1851), changing at Rome. Exactly the 1858 network. (01:37; 03:51–53)
2. **The Rock Island bridge cluster** — first bridge over the Mississippi, the *Effie Afton* span fire "two years back," and a river man growling "She'll burn again, God willing" — that sentence is practically a primary source. (05:27–29)
3. **Fort Kearny without a stockade** — sod-and-frame boxes around a parade ground, open to the plains, young planted cottonwoods. Nearly everyone wrongly builds a palisade. (07:89)
4. **Blue Water handled correctly** — the sutler's lazy "restless" corrected by Creighton's accurate "Harney did his work… they keep wide of us now." (07:97, 157)
5. **Spirit Lake as tavern talk** — right year, right "Santee," right "caught some of them" (Inkpaduta never was). (05:69–77)
6. **The Mississippi Rifles' record** — raised at Vicksburg, Davis's regiment, street fighting at Monterrey, the V at Buena Vista against lancers, Taylor's commendation, and Davis "who now sat in the Senate" — all true and all load-bearing. (18:19–39; 05:53)
7. **Sweet cottonwood bark as winter horse feed** — the authentic plains expedient (Indian ponies wintered on it), down to the worry about sweet vs. bitter cottonwood. (15:109–113)
8. **"A sweaty horse in winter is a dying horse, if you cannot get him dry."** (15:109) Correct, and correctly urgent.
9. **The horrors, not "DTs"** — snakes-and-insects zoopsia, tremor-first onset, and a clinically plausible seizure, all in period diction. (03:45; 11:23; 15:63–69)
10. **Winter band of ~200 bison** and spring bull dispersal, instead of the horizon-to-horizon cliché. (02:21; 10:13)
11. **The Lakota October move** — travois, dragging lodgepoles combing the grass flat, the spare-horse herd, and the line *bending wide* around a stranger like a river around a snag. (12:59)
12. **The red-cloth offering stake** facing the wintering valley — quiet, correct, unexplained to the reader. (08:61)
13. **Material culture throughout** — cut-sod construction, log post banked with sod, root cellar, well frame, packed-earth floor with the stained dirt dug out and re-tamped, pencil (not ink) journal in the cold, flint-and-steel hermit vs. match-striking hired man, the 1856 almanac, the commercial map with SIOUX across the blank. (09:31; 11:53, 93; 13:33; 03:117–119)
14. **The winter of 1856–57** rendered as historically brutal — it genuinely was (the same starvation winter that set the stage for Spirit Lake), a convergence the book may not even know it earned. (08 passim)
15. **The white winter jackrabbit** — "a flash of white against the snow" is exactly the white-tailed jackrabbit in winter pelage, native to those hills. (15:103–105)
16. **The internal calendar** — dates, ages, distances, and cross-references between journal and frame check out almost everywhere (see §X), which is rarer than any single fact.

---

**Summary of the gravest items:** the Hardins' farm location (23) is the one genuine breaks-credibility matter; the Lakota escort ending (36, saved by its vision hedge), the letter logistics (24), Davis carried off the field (12), the missing river crossings (8), Morrow's robe-less winters (34), "lawman" (41), buffalo "pawing" snow (30), the kill-count contradiction (13), and the December-north herd drift (22/28) are the items a paid specialist reader would put in the first page of a memo.</result>

**The new chain:** trading post Dec 19 → three days up the river (the wrong-creek episode now costs a morning, not a day) → two days up the creek → homestead **Dec 24**. Two nights there, north on Dec 26 with saddlebags filled from the Hardins' stores. Seizure Dec 29, food gone ~Jan 1, Niobrara crossing Jan 3, sighting at sunrise **Jan 4** (JE VIII's final entry, re-dated from the 7th), Morrow dies at dawn Jan 5, Farrell reads the journal that night, finds the body Jan 6.

**What it bought:**
- **Mail story fixed twice over** — the homestead is now ~40 miles from the post, and the trader's line became "They came in when the weather let them—flour and salt, and letters to send east." Monthly receipt in Oswego is now arrangeable.
- **Location credibility** — they're on farmable Loup-fork bottomland instead of deep sand hills (which won't hold a plow), while staying freakishly beyond the line. The trader's warning beat (stack #18) is still worth adding to mark the freakishness.
- **Food math resolved** — but note this needed the companion clause, not just the move: Farrell now provisions from the dead couple's stores (which also retroactively sources Ch 9's "heel of hardtack"). Foodless stretch: ~8 days → ~5.
- **A latent bug fixed for free** — the old geometry had the 10-day pursuit covering only ~25 miles (a crawl); it's now ~70 miles, which at the ledger's DT-impaired rate is exactly 10 days.
- **An unplanned gift**: Farrell now spends Christmas Day at the dead homestead, finishing the last of his whiskey on Christmas night — one day after musing that December twenty-fifth "would pass like any other day." It's completely unmarked in the text, only reachable by a reader doing the math. I logged it in continuity with a DO-NOT-POINT-AT-IT note; exactly the orphaned-significance texture the profile prescribes.

Also slipped in stack #1 while the calendar was open: Catherine's last letter is now dated "May the twentieth, eighteen fifty-eight" — the one-phrase fix for the frame-year hazard both blind readers hit.

<result>PART 1 — TWELVE DISCUSSION QUESTIONS

*White Buffalo* alternates between two voices: the unnamed-then-named journal of Josiah Morrow, dated April 1856 through January 1859, and the first-person account of Henry Farrell, hired in November 1858 to find Catherine Hardin. Part 1 may be handed out on its own.

**Comprehension**

**Q1.** The book interleaves Morrow's journal with Farrell's chapters, but the two narrations do not begin at the same time. Reconstruct the chronology: when does each strand begin, how far apart are they, and at what point in the book do the two timelines finally touch? What in the text lets you date the moment of convergence almost to the day?

**Q2.** What actually happened to George and Catherine Hardin? Establish who killed them, roughly when, what physical evidence Farrell finds at the homestead, and who buried them. Which of these facts does the reader possess before Farrell does?

**Q3.** Walk through the final chapter as a sequence of plain events: what does Farrell find in the meadow, what condition is the white buffalo in, who appears on the ridge, and what is the last thing Farrell tells us about himself? What, literally, is left undone when the book stops?

**Interpretation**

**Q4.** In Journal Entry VII, Morrow writes, "The settlers must be removed," and insists he reaches this conclusion "as a plain statement of fact," without malice. Test his stated rationale — that the settlers might endanger the buffalo — against what the journal actually shows him noticing about them (the laugh, the steam off the cup, "Something I do not want to name"). Is he protecting the animal, protecting his planned death, or acting on something he never admits? Which reading does the journal itself undermine?

**Q5.** In Chapter Seven, Farrell concludes that the man who follows the white buffalo murdered the Hardins — and he tells us plainly that "The care of the graves did not fit the violence, and I made it fit, because the tracks said what they said." He turns out to be right. Does being right vindicate his reasoning? Why does the book let us watch him force the evidence, and what does the chapter suggest his real motive for going north is?

**Q6.** Both men fought in Mexico; both came home, in Morrow's phrase, as "a weapon" with "no more use." Morrow seeks a sacred death; Farrell fills the same hollow with whiskey and hired work. In Chapter Nine Farrell says of the difference between them: "I did not like how small it was." How small is it? Where, if anywhere, does the book finally distinguish the two men?

**Q7.** Is the white buffalo sacred, or is she an animal? Gather what each kind of witness sees — Morrow, the traders and trappers, Farrell, the Lakota — and how the text handles moments where meaning is claimed ("there was something in them, or I imagined there was"). Does the book ever commit? Does it need to?

**Q8.** The Lakota never speak in this book. They watch Morrow in January 1857, leave a peeled stake with red cloth facing the herd's wintering valley, watch again as his death approaches ("waiting to see what I will do"), and at the end they receive the buffalo and turn away from Farrell. Why do they never interfere — with Morrow, with the buffalo, or with Farrell? Is their reticence the book's respect for what it can't narrate, or a failure to give them standing in a story about their own sacred animal?

**Q9.** Does Farrell live? The book ends with him kneeling in the snow — "He would carry me, if I could make myself move. I was not moving." Argue it both ways from the text: what has the book planted that would carry him out alive, and what has it planted that says he has arrived exactly where Morrow was the night before his death? What would the book lose if it settled the question?

**Craft**

**Q10.** The reader holds Morrow's journal from page one; Farrell only finds it in Chapter Ten, where he opens it and reads the very first line we read — "April the Nineteenth, Eighteen Fifty-Six. I have walked a long time." Someone, in other words, has assembled this book: journal entries interleaved with Farrell's account, plus a third-person opening chapter Farrell never witnessed from the inside. Who is the implied compiler, and what does your answer imply about Q9?

**Q11.** The murder of the Hardins — the hinge of the entire plot — is never shown. The journal skips from "I cannot wait any longer" (August 3) to "I have written of what was done in it, and I will not write of it again" (September 3); yet Farrell later reads an entry from that missing summer ("I did not know I would feel this. I thought I was past feeling"). The book also withholds Morrow's death itself, Catherine alive, and any return to Aldridge. What is each of these withholdings doing? Which one costs the reader most?

**Q12.** After Farrell reads the journal, Morrow's language starts surfacing in Farrell's own narration: the buffalo moves "like a ship through water," and kneeling by the body Farrell feels "a great and quiet peace" — Morrow's exact phrase from the eve of his death. Is this contagion, inheritance, or evidence about who is writing the book? Is it a blessing or a warning that Farrell now sounds like Morrow?

---

PART 2 — FACILITATOR'S KEY

**Q1 (timeline).** Morrow's journal opens April 19, 1856; Farrell's story opens in November 1858 (Chapter One's stripped November trees; Catherine's last letter "dated May the twentieth, eighteen fifty-eight," silence "since May"). So each journal entry runs roughly two years behind the chapter beside it, and the gap closes across the book. Convergence is datable: Journal VIII's last entry is January 4, 1859 ("Tomorrow, I think"), Journal IX is written the following dawn, and Farrell finds the camp, reads the journal through the night, and finds the body the next morning — so the whole book funnels to roughly January 5–6, 1859. A useful prompt: the six-month-old "autumn calf" of April 1856 means the buffalo was born autumn 1855, "born when no calf is born" — her out-of-season birth is why she's grown enough by winter 1858–59.

**Q2 (the Hardins).** Morrow killed both, in early August 1858 (Journal VII ends August 3: "I cannot wait any longer"), and buried them himself — Chapter Ten: he "taken two days to do it properly, piling stones on the graves." Farrell's evidence in Chapters Six–Seven: the tamped-over bloodstain by the door, the cracked wall, blood on the bedframe and in the barn, two carefully stoned graves, and the paired tracks — "A buffalo had been here. A man had followed it." The reader knows the killer's identity and motive one file before Farrell infers it (Journal VII sits directly after Chapter Seven confirms his guess — check your group on who realized what first; the dramatic irony is the point of the structure). Note also that Farrell corrects his own reconstruction ("So I had it half-wrong... The pieces would not sit still").

**Q3 (the ending, literally).** Farrell finds Morrow trampled and gored, face "peaceful," eyes open — "He had gotten what he wanted." The buffalo stands fifty yards off with dried blood "crusted on her chest and forelegs"; Farrell has a clear shot and declines it ("She was not my enemy. She had done what her nature demanded"). Three or four riders — "Lakota, I thought. Or another vision" — receive her; "she fell in among them," and they go north. Left undone: Farrell has not moved, has not eaten in two days, has not returned Morrow's journal or any report to Aldridge, and the commission Aldridge paid for is unfulfilled in every sense — the killer is dead by other means, and no word has gone east.

**Q4 (Morrow's decision).** The journal's stated logic is protective: the settlers "might see value in her... I cannot allow that." But the text undercuts it three ways. First, Morrow admits the threat is hypothetical — the man watched the herd and "did not seem to notice her." Second, the tell is what he can't stop watching: the couple's warmth. "The steam off the cup has stayed with me since. It is the only warm thing I have seen in a month," and earlier, "Or perhaps it is something else. Something I do not want to name." Third, his own frame slips from protection of her to protection of the project: "She is the only chance I will ever have to make something clean out of the wreckage of my life. I will not let anyone take that from me" — the referent of what's being protected is himself. The strongest reading the text supports: the rationale is real to him but constructed; the unnameable thing is most plausibly envy/longing for the life he can see and cannot feel (note "I saw a woman's face by candlelight once, in a time before the war. It does not compare"). Deliberately ambiguous which — the journal is written by a man practiced at not naming things, and the book honors that.

**Q5 (Farrell's inference).** The chapter is a study in motivated reasoning that happens to be correct. Farrell notices the disconfirming evidence himself — "The care taken here did not match the violence" — then overrides it: "I knew, with a certainty I could not have defended," and the remarkable sentence "The care of the graves did not fit the violence, and I made it fit, because the tracks said what they said." Being right doesn't vindicate the method, and the book knows it: the irony is that the "care" evidence was also true — Morrow both murdered them and buried them tenderly; Farrell's either/or was the error. His real motive is stated outright a page later: "But the truth was simpler. I wanted to see him... I wanted to find him and look at him and understand what he was" — recognition, not justice. Good pushback question: would Farrell have gone north even if the tracks had said nothing?

**Q6 (the mirror).** The parallels are thick: both at Monterrey; both describe themselves as instruments (Morrow: "The weapon does not question its use"; Farrell of his contemporaries: "moved through the world like ghosts wearing the shapes of men. I had been one of them, for a time"). The difference Farrell names in Chapter Nine: "I had tried to fill the space... He had gone looking for something large enough to swallow it whole. That was the difference, and I did not like how small it was." Where the book does distinguish them: Farrell keeps making small ethical motions Morrow can't — digging out the frozen stranger, advising Brandt to save his family, coaxing the horse off the ice, and finally refusing the shot. Morrow's largest act of care (the graves) comes only after murder. But Chapter Ten complicates any comfort: "He had made worse choices than mine — choices I could not forgive. But he had found something to die for. And I had not." The envy runs toward Morrow, which is the book's coldest observation.

**Q7 (the buffalo).** The text is scrupulously double. Naturalistic reading: white buffalo exist, rare; her behavior is animal throughout, and every numinous flourish is qualified — "there was something in them, or I imagined there was"; "Pity, perhaps"; and her killing of Morrow is engineered by Morrow ("I need to frighten her... she will choose fight"). Sacral reading: multiple independent, sober witnesses are shaken by her ("I never saw anything like that. Never want to again"); the herd defers to her; the coat stays unaccountably clean; she appears in Farrell's dream before he's seen her; the Lakota mark her wintering valley and come for her at the end. Morrow himself concedes the whole thing may be "the work of a broken mind... It does not matter. I believe it, and that is enough" — belief, not verification, is what the book is interested in. Both readings are kept alive on purpose; a facilitator should resist letting the group collapse it either way.

**Q8 (the Lakota).** Textual data: they watch and withdraw (January 1857); the peeled stake with red cloth faces the herd's valley — a claim or consecration Morrow won't touch or camp near; October 1858, "They know what she is... they are waiting to see what I will do"; and the ending, where they receive her — "she fell in among them" — and the leader looks at Farrell "for a long moment. Then he turned away." Non-interference readings the text supports: Morrow's own guess that a mad or death-seeking man is not to be interfered with; or that the buffalo's affairs are hers — Farrell's "She had done what her nature demanded" may be their view too; or that they were never absent at all — the stake suggests they've had custody of this situation from the start, and both white men have been walking through a watched country while believing themselves alone. The final turning-away can be read as judgment, mercy, or simple irrelevance of Farrell to what mattered. On the craft question, the live positions: the book's restraint is consistent with its no-interiority-for-anyone-but-the-narrators discipline and avoids ventriloquizing a sacred tradition; against that, the Lakota function structurally as silent authenticators of the buffalo's meaning — presence without voice. Both positions are defensible; expect the group to split.

**Q9 (does Farrell live?).** Deliberately ambiguous, and the text arms both sides. For survival: the whole account is retrospective first-person past tense; he told Garrett "I'll get back"; the escape route is explicitly seeded in the final page — the horse "would carry me south... back to the homestead where the Hardins had laid in enough supplies to last a winter"; and the worst of withdrawal appears to have crested (the visions turn benign: the soldiers "no longer seemed threatening... now they stood witness"). For death: no food for two days, deep withdrawal, extreme cold, "I was not moving," and — most damning — "I felt a great and quiet peace," which is verbatim Morrow's state on the eve of his chosen death ("There is only a great and quiet peace," Journal VIII). The book ends on "nothing left to follow," a man whose only remaining engine was pursuit. What would be lost by settling it: the ending's question is whether recognition of Morrow is fatal — answering it would decide, on the reader's behalf, whether Farrell is Morrow. See Q10 for the strongest survival argument.

**Q10 (the compiler).** The strongest inference: the book as an object implies someone who possessed both Morrow's journal and Farrell's account and interleaved them — and only Farrell ever holds the journal. On this reading the structure itself is the answer to Q9: Farrell got up, rode south, wintered on the Hardins' stores, and made this book; the past tense is earned. Complications the group should chew on: Chapter One is third person, from Aldridge's point of view, including things Farrell couldn't know (the rehearsals, the second-best coat, the unsendable letter) — either Farrell reconstructed it, or there is an editor above both narrators. Note too that the journal entries we read are a selection (the murder entry exists diegetically but is omitted — see Q11), which means the compiler curated Morrow, and the curation is merciful to the reader and to Morrow both. UNSETTLED: the text never confirms any frame — no preface, no discovered-manuscript device, no return east. The compiler is a legitimate inference from structure, not a fact; a facilitator can present it as the elegant reading but should not claim the book states it.

**Q11 (withholding).** Four withholdings, four different functions. The murder: Journal VIII's "I have written of what was done in it, and I will not write of it again" is Morrow's refusal, but the book's refusal goes further — the entry exists and we get only the two lines Farrell quotes ("I did not know I would feel this. I thought I was past feeling"), which are more devastating than the scene could be: the murder is the one act that briefly returned feeling to him. Morrow's death: unwitnessed, found after — the book denies Morrow (and us) the climax he scripted; we get the churned snow, not the consummation, keeping the act animal rather than transcendent. Catherine: she exists only as letters, a daguerreotype, drafts, a dress on a peg, and a ghost — the cost of both men's stories is a woman we're never allowed to meet, which the book seems to know (her unfinished draft, "We are building something... I have to believe that," is the moral center of Chapter Six). Aldridge: no return scene means the commercial frame — grief outsourced so "he would not have to" think — is never discharged; the reader is left holding the report Farrell never delivers. Likely group consensus on "costs most": Catherine; be ready to argue the murder entry as the alternative.

**Q12 (the echo).** Two verbatim migrations after Farrell reads the journal: "like a ship through water" (Journal III, quoted consciously by Farrell in Chapter Ten, then used unattributed in Chapter Eleven — "Her white coat moved through the snow like a ship through water") and "a great and quiet peace" (Journal VIII → Chapter Eleven). Three readings, all supportable: contagion — Morrow's death-logic has colonized Farrell, and the peace is the same peace, which reads the echo as the book's final alarm; inheritance — Farrell becomes Morrow's witness and executor, carrying his language as one carries a dead man's effects, consistent with the visions turning from accusers to witnesses; compiler's tell — if Farrell wrote this book (Q10), he wrote Morrow's phrases into his own chapters deliberately, and the echo is craft inside the fiction, a survivor's homage. The unattributed second use of "ship through water" is the detail to press on: quotation has become voice.

**Flags and small matters a facilitator should have in pocket:**

- UNSETTLED: the letters stopped after May 20, 1858, but the Hardins lived until early August and the trader saw them "last summer." Why no June or July letters reached Oswego is never explained. Frontier mail logistics can paper over it (their last trip to the post may have carried the May letter), but Farrell's own timing inference — "The letters had gone quiet in May" linked to the killer's August presence — doesn't actually align, and the text never notices. If a sharp reader raises it, concede it.
- Morrow's kill counts differ: "seventeen men with certainty and perhaps more" (Journal II) versus Buena Vista's "I do not know how many. Dozens, certainly" (Journal IX). Readable as certainty versus total, or as the journal's honesty deepening as death approaches — worth offering as interpretation rather than error.
- The Hardins' arrival dates work out: married early 1856, on the land by early summer 1857 (Morrow watches them build, Journal V; "They have survived their first winter," Journal VII), matching Catherine's one hard winter and "George says the worst is behind us."
- Whether the ending's riders are real or fever-vision is raised by Farrell himself ("Or another vision — another gift from the fever"); the blood on the buffalo is physical, the riders less certain. This is designed ambiguity, not a flaw — it belongs under Q7/Q8.</result>

## The fix batch (first commit)

All six consensus items applied: the Civil War paragraph is grounded — no named futures, ending on the brother's own words ("The whole country's going to hell... I had nothing to set against it"); the trader now warns the Hardins off ("nothing up that creek but Sioux and winter—the nearest white man two days off, and that man me, and I'm no neighbor"); the trapper sees the animal while Creighton keeps the man; JE VI is trimmed with the numbness beat now leaking ("The steam off the cup has stayed with me since. It is the only warm thing I have seen in a month"); the theme statement drops to twice; and the borrow consolidation landed — Ch 10's "waiting" closer cut, ship-through-water planted in the reading scene, registry back to three sanctioned echoes. Also snuck in: JE I's rock-tree pre-spend is gone, protecting the 09/14 inversion.

## The exercise (reading club, chosen over pitch letter)

I picked reading club because it directly probes whether the *just-revised* text supports the readings we think it does — the better interleave test. Design: facilitator writes twelve questions plus a private answer key from a cold read; a second blind reader answers from the questions alone; divergence is the signal.

**Results:** nine of twelve converged independently on the same passages and readings. The three divergences were all *productive* — refinements, not defects (the true timeline-touch is Ch 9's mile-off sighting; the killing readable as a failed experiment in feeling; the survival question staging cleanly as two defensible poles). And the exercise directly validated three of today's fixes on cold readers: the frame year was dated "almost to the day" off the new anchor, the steam-off-the-cup leak became the facilitator's central tell for Morrow's decision, and the borrow device was read as designed craft — "quotation has become voice" — rather than authorial tic.

**One new defect survived both cold reads** and is now fixed: Farrell's stable arithmetic linked the killer's August presence to letters that stopped in May without noticing the gap. He now notices — "May was not August. The two would not quite fit together" — which pre-echoes Ch 7's "the pieces would not sit still" and turns the flaw into his honesty.

## On the stack system itself

The interleave worked the way you wanted: rework → fresh-context review → divergences become stack items with proposals → rework, with every item carrying its status, evidence, and provenance so nothing gets applied twice or silently dropped. The one refinement I'd flag: review agents should always read the *current* text (cheap, since they're fresh anyway), because half of this round's value was validation of prior fixes, not just new findings — the stack now records "confirmed fixed by cold reader" alongside "open," which is the evidence layer a pure to-do list wouldn't have.

**Stack state:** 12 of 28 items applied or resolved; the big remaining text items are the homestead-tracks re-pass (#6), the JE VII wrestling entry (#8), the Pikes Peak dilution material (#25), and the survival-detail enrichments (#20). Next on my list per the earlier plan: the two new plates (ferry-bridge and two-stories), unless you'd rather I keep grinding text.

Not load-bearing — it's inherited scaffolding. The planning-era outline had the last letter in March ("winter is finally breaking"); the draft moved it to May, I suspect only to keep a spring-letter texture ("the garden is in, the creek is running high"). Its sole structural function is giving Aldridge a long runway of silence before his November trip — and it *costs* us the ten-week gap we've now patched twice (the trader's mail line, Farrell's "May was not August").

Moving it to **July the twentieth** closes everything at once, and buys three quiet gains:

1. Silence = death, one cause. The letters stop because they die two weeks later — no patch needed.
2. Farrell's stable arithmetic inverts from strained to damning: July silence + August passage *fit*, honestly strengthening his inference.
3. The unmarked resonance: JE VII's "The settlers must be removed" is dated July 17. The last letter — "George says the worst is behind us. I hope he is right." — would now be written **three days after** Morrow's decision. Nothing in the text points at it; a reader doing the math finds it. Same register as the Christmas-at-the-homestead find.

The one cost: Aldridge's inquiry window compresses to ~2.5 months (receipt late August → alarm early October → inquiries → November hire). That's workable — eastern federal/military offices answer in weeks, and it arguably paints him better: he moved as fast as a careful man could before winter closed the trails, rather than sitting on silence for half a year. Checking for stragglers, then applying:
